Chapter 39 — The Part They Could Replace

Yselle Valcienne put a dirty component in his hands before asking his name.

“This part works,” she said. Then she pointed to its other half on the bench. “This part costs us.”

Elias held the cylinder in both hands. A polished band ran around its inside; near the rim, the metal turned rough. The hand that had recovered from the cut protested at the weight. He looked for somewhere to put it and found only a sheet covered in figures.

“Not there.”

He stopped. The engineer took away the sheet and cleared the bench.

“Now.”

The cylinder landed with a low sound. Elias realized he had been holding his breath.

“Veyne. Elias.”

“Good. Don’t drop it to shake my hand.”

Behind him, Gaston set down the folder. Marcel took off his coat and stood looking at the two halves. It was the second of June; for the first time in weeks, Elias had entered a workshop without first having to explain why a piece of paper allowed him to be there.

Yselle’s workshop was narrow at the entrance and much longer than it looked from the street. A row of high windows lit the main bench. At the back, beneath a stationary belt, a pump stood on its base. Dried water marks covered the floor around it.

On the shelf beside it were three empty spaces. Elias followed the rectangles marked on the wood, then turned back to the cylinder.

“Which bit works?”

Yselle pointed to the smooth band inside.

“The piston runs here. When this surface wears, I want to take it out and replace it. The rest of the machine ought to be able to stay where it is.”

“So it’s a removable liner,” Marcel said.

“That was my intention.”

Gaston bent closer. The engineer’s black hair was tied well enough to keep it out of her work and badly enough for a strand to escape whenever she moved. She pushed it behind her ear with her wrist, leaving a dark mark on her temple.

“Is this the third?” Gaston asked.

“The third I’ve tried getting out without cursing the designer.”

“And the designer?”

“Listens and takes notes.”

Elias smiled before remembering the woman’s surname. She had already moved to the other side of the bench.

An opening in the prototype had been left uncovered. Yselle brought the liner to it without pushing it all the way in. The component seemed to find the right position on its own: a recognizable shape, one rim stopping against another, the places where it needed fastening.

“It works. We’ve run the tests on the bench circuit. The problem comes afterward.”

She pushed the liner into place, temporarily secured the cover and handed Elias a long wrench.

“Try imagining that six months have passed.”

He looked at the clean metal of the fastenings.

“I’m taking it out?”

“Preferably before the season changes.”

The wrench went in from the side shown on the drawing. Elias sought the first fastening. He reached it, found his grip and tried to turn. The handle struck the support.

He lifted it to change the angle.

It struck again.

Behind him he heard the faint scrape of Marcel’s beard against his collar. The master had tilted his head. He said nothing.

Elias tried the other side. To reach it he had to tuck an elbow behind the pump; the end of the wrench slipped off the fastening.

“You aren’t obliged to beat the tool,” Yselle said.

“I’d give it one more chance.”

“The tool or yourself?”

The wrench struck again. Elias pulled it out and looked at it as though some part of the trouble ought to be visible on the handle.

Gaston spread the drawing on the bench.

In the drawing, the support did not cover the area where the handle needed to turn. The line was correct. So, apparently, was the measurement beside it. Elias followed the front view with a finger, then the plan.

The cover hid the place where the free space narrowed.

He put his palm against the pump’s body and let the distances come.

The visible surface ceased to be merely the thing preventing him from looking. Beyond the rim he sensed the nearby geometry: the cavity, the stretch through which the wrench needed to change its angle, the thickness represented in another view. For an instant the component offered him both faces without letting him choose the more convenient one.

The handle could not make that turn.

“Here.”

Yselle followed his finger.

“Which here?”

Elias realized he was pointing to something the others could not see. He took his hand away and picked up the pencil. On Gaston’s copy he marked the relationship between the inner rim and the support.

“The wrench reaches the nut. There isn’t room to bring it back after I’ve turned it.”

Marcel asked for the caliper.

With two tools and a few minutes’ work, they reached the spot. The ordinary measurement found the narrowing Elias had sensed. Yselle moved to read it herself; the strand of hair fell again, and this time she left it there.

“How much should we take off?” she asked.

Elias already had an answer on his tongue. He looked at the support.

“I can tell you how much room is missing. Not how much metal you can remove from that.”

“At last, someone who doesn’t want to improve my machine by sawing through it.”

She released the cover. Marcel held the piece while she lifted it; Gaston noted a second measurement. Elias traced the path with the loose wrench. Without the cover, his hand found room. That was precisely the advantage they did not want to have to buy by taking everything apart.

Yselle drew the original sheet toward her. She crossed out a measurement, left the old one legible and sketched a small additional view.

“We bring the access round this way. The reinforcement can stay here. I needn’t make it thin to defend the first drawing.”

Marcel stopped her at the new path. For several minutes Elias held the sheet still while the others discussed it. The engineer’s hands moved between paper and component without seeking a perfect match: when there was none, she went back to the paper.

The work Gaston had asked him to do began taking shape. Actual measurements. Access for the wrench. Room for the arm of whoever would remove the liner.

“Don’t draw his,” Yselle said, catching him gauging Marcel’s elbow by eye. “He already knows every way of making a space look small.”

The master stretched his arm over the drawing.

“It’s a skill that takes years.”

The pump test came afterward. They reassembled what they had opened and cleared the measuring tools off the bench. Gaston took the receiving vessel; Elias stood near the one from which the water would be drawn.

Yselle checked the level, then started the bench mechanism. The piston began to move. Elias felt the first stroke through the base and up through his shoes.

Water appeared in the pipe, stopped, advanced again.

“Don’t trust the first spurt,” the engineer said. “Wait for it to work.”

Elias watched the vessel fill. He remembered blood washing away from Etienne’s arm and found himself keeping his hands too far from the rim. The water continued on its way. The noise was loud, but regular.

Then it changed.

A hollower beat fell between two strokes of the piston. Elias raised his head. Yselle pointed out the lower vessel: the level had dropped beneath the point where the intake needed to remain.

She stopped the mechanism.

“Air,” she said. “The machine hasn’t discovered an opinion about water.”

Gaston emptied the upper vessel into the lower one. Some splashed onto Elias’s shoe. He stepped back and the engineer looked down.

“Too late for the distinction of a dry visit.”

That quick smile reappeared, the one that never seemed to interrupt her work. Elias accepted the cloth and wiped the water off the floor before they began the next test.

The pump worked. It did not need his enthusiasm to do so.

When they stopped it, Yselle let the test section drain. The full vessel was heavy enough to need two people; Marcel took the other handle without waiting to be asked.

“Where is it supposed to go?” Elias asked.

“To the Mutual’s depot,” Gaston said. “We’ll go there this afternoon.”

Yselle showed him the site drawing. A low tank, a run of pipes, a vessel higher up. Elias looked for the height and found it marked provisionally.

“You didn’t take that measurement yourself?”

“It’s a design measurement. I want the one on site. And I want to know whether whoever works there can reach the shutoff without climbing onto a crate.”

A voice from the back of the workshop asked where two packages should be put. Yselle answered without looking; a moment later she raised her head.

“Not on the drawings. No, not the old ones either.”

She went to receive them. Elias saw Gaston follow their arrival with an expression he might not have understood had his colleague not immediately looked at the empty spaces on the shelf.

The packages were small. They contained material, but not what was needed for three machines.

Yselle returned with an envelope and slit its edge. She read one line, then another. She folded the letter without returning it to the envelope.

“They aren’t coming?” Marcel asked.

“Not by the date they proposed.”

The master indicated the cylinder.

“We can revisit those after we have the measurements.”

“I know.”

She put the letter beneath a weight, away from the water. When she turned to Elias, she once again had the attention of someone asking for one thing at a time.

“Two days for your drawing. Can you do it?”

“If the site lets us take the measurements.”

“Noemi will let you take those. You’ll have to earn the rest.”

Gaston had started gathering the drawings. Beneath the site plan was a list of components in three columns. In the first, Elias recognized the body they had just opened. In the second someone had underlined a date; the third was almost empty.

“How many did you order?” he asked before managing to stop himself.

Yselle followed his gaze.

“What we needed. Not all from the same supplier.”

“If it’s only this part missing, can’t you make it here?”

The engineer picked up the rough cylinder and had him tilt it toward the window. The polished band seemed to widen.

“We can machine it. We can’t expect the part to turn out well because the last one turned out badly. Look where the wear began.”

Elias found the area. Something his exercise had not told him joined the measurement of the space: how a surface endured thousands of passes. He handed the cylinder to Marcel. The master turned it slightly and asked about the tool used.

Yselle answered at once. Then she took the component back, set it beside the good sample and left the letter where it was.

“I still need the measurement,” she told Elias. “If the way we obtain the parts changes, I don’t want to discover afterward that they can’t be removed.”

Elias looked at the empty rectangles on the shelf. Three spaces, he thought. He did not ask what they were worth before knowing which machines they were meant to hold.

“And for the parts?”

Yselle put away the wrench he had been fighting.

“For an answer that concerns me, until the eighth.”

She added nothing. Elias wrote that date beside the delivery date, then drew a line to keep them separate.

Before leaving, he asked to try the tool’s path once more on the dismantled liner. Yselle left him the component and returned to the letter. This time he found his grip at once. Without the cover, it would have been very easy to convince himself the problem was solved.

He put the cover beside the component again. That needed to be in the drawing too.

Chapter 40 — The Height of the Water

The first bucket spilled water on Gaston’s shoe. The second struck Elias’s leg before he had even decided which way to move.

“If you stay there, we’ll get the rest of you wet too,” a woman behind him said.

Elias flattened himself against the wall. The worker passed with a bucket in each hand, leaning just enough to keep them from scraping her skirt. The Mutual’s courtyard smelled of damp wood and cloth dried without enough air. Spilled water ran between two stones to a drain.

Noemi Darel was waiting beside an empty vessel.

“You found the entrance.”

“We made ourselves known,” Gaston said, looking at his shoe.

She handed him a cloth. It was not clean enough for a wound, but cleaner than the leather he needed to dry. Elias followed it with his eyes and realized that she had already moved a stool out of the way.

“This isn’t a fountain for the city,” Noemi said. “First I’d like to stop sending people up and down with those.”

She meant the buckets, not the visitors. Elias managed not to ask.

Parts of prostheses were laid out on the bench behind her: a hinge polished by movement, an open leather strap, a hollow section that reminded him of a calf. A board set on edge divided them from the dirty objects. A closed parcel occupied the end nearest the door.

Elias put down his folder only when Noemi showed him the clear space.

“We’ve come about the route for the water,” Gaston said. “Yselle gave us the drawing.”

“I’ll give you the real thing.”

She led them past the bench and through a doorway so low that Gaston ducked for safety. Elias copied him half a step later. The back of the courtyard opened onto service stairs and a walkway. Higher up, a tank rested on supports, a drop creeping down its rim.

Below stood a larger basin that could be reached from the courtyard without steps.

“The service water comes in here. We take it up in buckets. Then it comes down where it’s needed.”

Noemi explained where: washing tools, cleaning the depot, tasks needing a steady supply modest enough to seem unimportant to anyone who could turn on a tap somewhere else.

Elias followed the visible pipe. One section had lost its paint; another had been painted around a fastening instead of being dismantled. He stopped where it went beneath the paving.

“From here?”

“It goes underneath.”

Gaston marked the limit of what they could see on the plan. Elias put a hand against the wall and reached for the distance between the courtyard floor and the tank’s rim.

The height came clearly, separate from the effort of climbing it. For an instant the stretch hidden by the walkway ceased to keep him from knowing the relationship between the two points. He sensed his position below and the rim above, like the two ends of the same exercise.

Then he took his hand away. The weight of his own body once again seemed quite different from a number.

Gaston set the reference and began the ordinary check. Noemi held the end of the tape while Elias wrote. A drop from the tank landed on his wrist; he moved the paper.

“Does it leak?”

“When we fill it too much.”

“How do you know when it’s full?”

She pointed to a place on the walkway.

From there, they could look inside. The level the water needed to reach had left a mark. Elias looked at the stairs, the mark, then the hands of the worker returning with an empty bucket.

The pump would save someone a journey. It could also fill the tank too much, much faster.

“We’ll need to see the level from below,” he said.

Gaston had him note it beside the first problem.

“And reach the shutoff,” Noemi added.

“Here?”

She took him to the side of the old pipe. The control needed a short key; it was not a crank anyone could find with a hand in the dark. The key lay on a nearby shelf, high enough not to be buried among things left on the ground.

Noemi picked it up, showed him the socket and put it back.

“Whoever uses it needs to know where it is. I don’t want the last person who finishes taking it away in a pocket.”

Elias looked at the shelf. A small lip kept things from falling, but the key could disappear under a cloth.

“Do we put this on the drawing?” he asked Gaston.

“First we ask whether it stays here.”

“It stays here,” Noemi said. “Until we find a better place.”

She did not sound as though she were making an eternal promise. Elias made a separate mark to remind himself. The distance from the key to the socket was short. Still, his ability to reach an object across a little space was not enough: whoever worked there after them would need to find it.

A voice in the courtyard asked whether a load could come through. Noemi called back to wait and moved them aside.

A man arrived pushing a small cart. At first Elias noticed only how he held the weight back while walking backward. Then he saw the leg: one knee did not bend with the other. The foot met the stone with a harder sound.

The cart fitted between the bench and the tank support. Just. The man still had to bring his hip against the wall, seeking a place where he could pull without losing his balance.

Elias reached for the handle.

“Which side?” he asked.

The man showed him, and together they passed the narrowing. Elias felt the load try to turn. He straightened it with his elbow close to his body, without suddenly making it light.

Once the cart was in the wider courtyard, his companion signaled that he could let go.

Noemi had stayed beside the narrow stretch.

“The machine was here on the drawing,” she said.

Elias opened the plan. The base’s rectangle occupied part of the passage they had just used. From above there seemed to be room: room for a man without a cart, able to bend both knees and turn whenever he pleased.

He repositioned the sheet against the wall. The paper offered no other solution out of gratitude.

“If we put it farther along, can people reach the bench?”

Noemi had him walk the route.

The first position prevented a door from opening. The second brought the base close to the basin’s edge. Gaston measured and made Elias return to the starting point.

For a while they found nothing that worked. The woman with the buckets passed twice, smiling openly the second time.

“At least now you know how long it takes us.”

Elias looked at the reddened hands on the handle.

“Some of it.”

He tried imagining his own body with one movement missing. The maneuver he had made with the cart would not have worked the same way. He went to the opposite wall and found a position they had not yet marked.

“The pipe is longer here.”

Gaston looked in the proposed direction. Noemi opened the nearby door all the way and slowly closed it.

“But we don’t lose this entrance.”

She placed a stool at the edge of the proposed base and went back toward the bench. First she passed alone, then had the empty cart brought through. The same man pushed it without lowering the load or seeking the wall with his hip.

Elias noted the clearance. It was a proposal to take back to Yselle; the height and length of the new section had to be compared with what the machine could do. At least they would not be discussing a pump set in a passage that did not exist.

Before leaving the courtyard to follow the pipe, Gaston asked where he could get a drink. Elias’s throat was dry enough for him to look straight at the vessel they had just filled.

Noemi followed his gaze, took a clean glass from the cupboard and pointed to the inner doorway.

“That water is for work.”

“I wasn’t saying—”

“I saw you looking. People who’ve been here for years do it too.”

He followed her to a jug kept on a separate shelf. The glass reached him full of water, without his having to defend where he had looked. He drank too quickly; when he put it down, Noemi filled it halfway and showed him where to leave it afterward.

“When the pump works, people see water coming out of a pipe. They don’t see the line on your drawing.”

Elias picked up the plan again. He marked which circuit would feed the tank and asked Gaston to check the wording before delivery. Then he finished the second half glass slowly. He already knew he was thirsty, and this seemed a good opportunity to deal with it before measuring again.

Gaston went off with the attendant who knew the circuit. Elias stayed to finish the sketch.

Noemi picked up a small worn hinge from the bench and held it between her fingers while looking at the drawing.

“The hearing?” she asked.

He raised his head.

“It’s over. I can work outside again.”

“I can see that.”

He managed a smile. She put the hinge back in its little tray. A few letters marked a label; Elias looked away before reading them as a question addressed to him too.

“Can that be replaced?”

“Yes. That’s the good news.”

Noemi showed him the worn place without handing him the object. Two surfaces had worked against each other until the edge had developed obvious play.

“The bad news is that what’s meant to arrive doesn’t always arrive when it’s needed. Meanwhile someone waits. And they can’t always stay still.”

Elias thought of the pump’s liner. A replaceable part had value beyond the person who knew how to draw it.

“Yselle wants the replacements to be made here.”

“So do I. Then we have to find someone who knows how to make them and pay for the work.”

She was not asking him to invent a workshop from the bench where they had put a drawing. She set down the hinge and moved a strip of leather away from the small dirty basin.

Something fell in the inner room. Noemi turned, listened to a reply and returned to her place. Elias had already risen.

“A tool,” she said. “It happens to us too.”

He put his pen back on the paper.

Gaston returned with the references for the visible section. Beneath the paving, some points remained to be checked before a different connection could be put to work. Gaston marked those points on the drawing. They would return before connecting the pump.

As they gathered their tools, Noemi asked Elias to carry the empty vessel to the bench where it was needed. He took one handle and waited for the worker to offer him the other.

“It isn’t full,” she said.

“I can feel that.”

“Then you can walk faster.”

He followed her across the courtyard. For once he had the lighter load and a route already chosen by someone else. They put it down with its rim facing the wall, where it would not collect dirty water.

Noemi approved the sketch of the clear position. Gaston explained that Yselle would need to check it against her own drawings. She nodded and retrieved the cloth from the stone where she had left it.

On the way out, Elias saw the worker return to the two buckets. One was still wet. She picked it up, checked the handle and went to the basin.

He opened his notebook again while Gaston said goodbye. He drew the route she kept taking, a line rising and falling twice. He did not need to ask anyone how good it would be to cross it out.

Chapter 41 — Three Exits

Sybille Kest counted three exits and ruled out two.

The window overlooked the courtyard, but a recently fitted grille narrowed its sill. The door beside the stove opened into a windowless storeroom. The main stairs behind her remained the only convenient way to take out a man who did not wish to cooperate.

For herself, perhaps, she could use the stretch of shadow beneath the stairs. That was not enough to make her like the room.

Mael Vaur indicated the chair.

“It isn’t nailed down.”

“The chair doesn’t worry me.”

He went on clearing the desk. He had moved three restored objects to make room for a package no one would gladly have restored: an ordinary component, badly wrapped in cloth too good for it.

Sybille sat where she could see the door. The room above the depot smelled of dry paper and wood soap. From downstairs came the thumps of the last delivery being unloaded. Every so often Mael’s glass trembled against its saucer.

“It came back?” she asked.

“Exactly what we sent.”

“Wasn’t that what you wanted?”

Mael handed her the piece.

The central curve resembled a hinge only if someone already knew what to look for. The inner surfaces had irregularities a hand recognized more easily than the eyes. Sybille turned it. One point pressed against her fingertip.

“It opens nothing,” she said.

“No.”

“Then they haven’t taken anything from us.”

Mael smiled as though he appreciated a good joke. He took the component back and laid it on the cloth. Beside his elbow he had left an unpaid bill.

“They realized the text wasn’t the inspector’s. They had the versions compared.”

“I told you a signature wouldn’t be enough.”

“I never said it would.”

Sybille leaned forward far enough to read the beginning of the commercial response. She could see the refusals clearly. The requests remained elsewhere, in sheets Mael kept on his side of the desk.

They had had a simple opportunity: bring the customer into the work, pay for a demonstration, leave with an object and a name to contact again. Ruy had refused. The draftsman had taken the documents to someone capable of recognizing them.

“Ilian?”

“He has yet to finish collecting his payment.”

“He finished the drawing.”

Mael rested a hand on the bill.

“We need to establish what the client considers delivered.”

Sybille looked at him. It was the sentence meant to discourage her from discussing an outstanding payment in front of him. He used it even when he was the client himself.

“A man can come back for his money and bring you an officer instead of a technician.”

“He can.”

No irritation. Mael considered objections of that kind only if she offered them as problems with the work. Sybille still could not tell how much of his calm was habit and how much pleasure at making her choose her tone.

She got up and went to the window. The courtyard was already almost dark. A porter had stopped to rub his neck before going back into the depot. Neither of the two men waiting for him told him to hurry; one simply took the load from his hands.

Sybille recognized the moment when she could have vanished from both men’s sight without creating a great darkness. She would merely have had to stop offering their gaze the signal it sought. She did not. The dirty glass reflected a little of the room behind her too, and she wanted to keep seeing it.

“You didn’t call me here about the bill.”

Mael pushed a sheet into the cleared space.

A plan. Not of a house. A stretch of street, the entrance to a depot and the room where the new work would be proposed.

“This time we’re only asking to have a checking collar measured.”

“Only.”

“A measurement. The object stays ordinary. It needn’t open a passage.”

Sybille read the note. It did not say to bring a working hinge. A technician could accept the visit without feeling he was being asked to sell everything he knew.

“And when he arrives?”

“We see what he knows how to measure.”

She raised her eyes.

Mael had not answered the question he had heard. For an instant the unloading stopped and the depot seemed empty. Sybille heard a wheel drag over stone; then a man called out, filling all the space the silence had left.

She remembered another gate, a departure passed off as a late delivery. She had kept the noise low on the hinge while two people crossed the yard. One was walking with the other’s help. She had not asked why his wrists were so tightly covered.

She had been paid for the stretch between the gate and the street.

Some of that money was still part of the money for the house she wanted to buy.

“I’m not good at arguing with whoever gets brought inside,” she said.

“You’re good at keeping the alley from knowing.”

Sybille sat again. The chair leg scraped, giving her a brief, childish pleasure. Mael merely looked up from the sheet.

“This can be fixed,” she said.

She pressed two fingers against the wood, reached the vibration through the contact and pushed the chair a handspan. It moved without the earlier complaint. From below came the porter’s noise again: she had not silenced the depot.

When she took her hand away and shifted her foot, the wood scraped once more.

Mael looked at the place on the floor.

“Will that be enough for the door?”

“For the noise of that door, if I can work on it. Not for everything that might happen on the other side.”

“I’m not asking you to silence the neighborhood.”

“Asking for less doesn’t make it easier to hold the right point.”

He picked up a pen. Sybille recognized the beginning of a cost item. Her skill became more expensive when they let her describe what it did not include; less safe when the client seemed too pleased to receive a short answer.

“I want to see the place empty first,” she added.

Mael looked at her as though the condition were already written down.

“You can see it now.”

The test room was downstairs, beyond a wooden partition that retained the color of an old warehouse. It was not a prison. Sybille recognized precisely what would let them use it without making it look like one: a bench, tools, two chairs. Someone could enter expecting work.

A lamp lit the middle of the bench. Mael moved it to show her a corner.

“We keep the light here.”

“If you want me working on shadows, you need to stop moving it first.”

He left the lamp where she indicated. Sybille stood at the edge of the darker area and looked at the partition, the floor, the stretch beneath the bench. Darkness did not offer her a destination merely because it would be useful. She needed continuity, something she could actually reach.

Mael waited.

“Can you see the bench from the door?” she asked.

He went to check. As he turned, he gave her a moment when no one was looking at her directly. Sybille damped the sound of her steps and moved against the partition. The edge of the bench brushed her skirt; she went around it without losing sight of the door. When Mael looked for her again, she was already where the light ended.

“From there?”

“From here I can reach the door. I want the bench far enough away that it doesn’t cut off my route.”

He considered the space. Then he pushed one of the chairs beneath the table.

The gesture made her see a man seated there. A technician busy looking at a part, his open folder beside him. Sybille did not know his face and realized she wanted to give him an unpleasant one.

“We don’t have anyone who has accepted yet,” she said.

“That’s why we prepare.”

Mael returned to the door. Sybille stayed in the room a moment longer, long enough to feel how easily she could cover part of a noise and how little that ability changed what someone decided to make happen there.

She followed him upstairs without asking who would question the technician. Asking that too would make it harder to call her own work a separate service.

In the upper room Mael had left a second folder open. He closed it before picking up the visit request. Sybille saw only the lines of a table and a space for a signature.

He had her check her assignment. A prepared support, access to the site, attendance required only on a date still to be agreed. No indefinite availability.

“Payment first,” she said.

Mael pointed to a line.

“Some first. The rest when the service is complete.”

“Ilian is still on the second half.”

He lifted his finger off the paper.

It was the first silence that evening Sybille felt belonged entirely to her. She could have taken the sentence back with a joke. Instead she left the sheet on the bench and waited.

Mael asked her to specify when she wanted the initial payment. He did not raise his voice or promise money he was already counting. Sybille spelled out the conditions one by one, until the assignment no longer obliged her to rely solely on her memory of the conversation.

By the time they finished, the glass had stopped trembling. The depot had finished unloading.

A couple’s footsteps sounded in the street. The windows opposite were lit; in one, a woman was moving a flowerpot to close the shutters. Sybille looked at the gap between the pot and the curtain. In her own house there would be room for both, and no one would ask her to keep a room available for a future assignment.

Sybille folded her copy and put it in her inner pocket. The edge met another, thinner sheet: the particulars of a house she had visited twice. On the second visit, the owner had mostly talked about the first offer someone else had made.

She kept the two papers apart with a finger.

“If the date changes, tell me before you make arrangements with the others.”

“Do you have another job?”

“I might.”

Mael’s eyes dropped to the pocket. He did not try to find out what it was. Sybille would have preferred him to ask outright instead of leaving her uncertain whether she had answered a question about her price.

“Confirmation through the usual contact,” he said.

She checked the address in the margin. It was where she could receive a request without having someone come to the place she slept. After months of working with Mael, none of his promises seemed more important than that distance.

A noise from below made them both turn. The last porter closed the depot door from inside and headed for the courtyard exit. Sybille followed his footsteps until she heard his voice in the street. The test room had stayed empty as she had asked. For that evening, at least, she could leave without checking whether Mael had already changed the job.

Mael handed her the coat she had left on the arm of the chair.

“When the draftsman comes back, we’ll have a question he can answer.”

Sybille put on a sleeve. The cloth caught a finger; she had to free it before reaching the door.

“Don’t let me find a person where you promised me an empty bench.”

“I’ll tell you first.”

She went down the stairs, counting the steps. In the courtyard she turned to look at the window. From outside, the grille looked like good protection against thieves.

Chapter 42 — An Invitation Without a Bill

Yselle’s letter did not ask for money.

Alexander turned it over, checking for a second sheet. Standing before the study, Maurice followed the movement without offering an explanation.

The invitation was for the fifth of June, in Lethorne. His aunt wanted to show him the work and ask what he thought of it. She had written after the technicians’ visit the previous day; the delivery had arrived that morning.

A water stain marked the final line without dissolving the name.

In the duke’s memories, Yselle often wrote with something running or open beside her elbow. Alexander remembered a letter his mother had insisted he answer with the same care he would give a formal invitation. It had cost him an afternoon; his aunt had then replied asking whether something had happened to his hand.

He had been twelve. He had been deeply offended.

Edran smiled at the sheet before realizing he had done so.

“Shall I confirm?” Maurice asked.

“The fifth. Yes.”

The butler looked at the calendar open on the table.

“You have two appointments already arranged.”

Alexander followed his finger. One could be moved to the previous afternoon. For the other he would have to offer a different date. He thought how easy it would be to get up and leave someone else the work of making an absence reasonable.

“Don’t cancel that one. Ask whether they can come the day before. For the second, suggest a date after my return.”

“When do you expect to return?”

The question stopped him. He had decided to travel for a visit without yet telling his aunt what he meant to visit.

He reread the invitation. Pumps, drawings, components to examine. No mention of the length of a stay. Yselle had not asked him to spend the rest of his life in the workshop in order to answer a question.

“Have the accommodation and length of the visit confirmed. For now, don’t promise a return on the same day.”

Maurice made a note and left the study. Edran remained with his pen poised above the reply.

He could have reached many things without a carriage. Not everything he remembered knowing how to do answered him in his present body, and he did not intend to mistake a wish for an exercise he possessed. Twenty-three kilometers were short enough to be a journey and real enough to take a morning.

Teren arrived before he had finished the first sentence.

“Maurice told me Lethorne.”

“Unless he added a farther destination between the corridor and the stairs.”

The head of security looked at the sheet. He did not smile, but the duke’s eyes remembered the face well enough to notice the brief movement at the corners of his mouth.

“The ordinary road?”

Alexander said yes.

Teren asked whether he wanted to stop elsewhere, who would receive news of the journey and whether the visit involved a place open to a large public. There was no answer to the last question. Edran picked up the letter and realized Yselle had described her work as she would to a nephew, not to a man who traveled carrying other people’s eyes with him.

“I’ll ask. I’d rather see the workshop while it works.”

“Then I need to know where we can wait without standing between people and machines.”

Teren described the needs of the carriage and the two guards he would assign to the journey. He would come too. The other shifts would not fill themselves because the duke was leaving the residence.

Edran asked only that they not send anyone running ahead to order the city to stop moving.

This time Teren really smiled.

“That isn’t how one arrives at a workshop.”

“I’m pleased to hear it.”

When the officer left, Alexander had a slightly longer reply that was finally useful to send. He accepted the fifth, asked where he could conveniently stay and explained who would come with him. The question about the project remained short.

He wanted to see it before talking about help.

He had not sealed the letter when Celine knocked twice in quick succession.

He recognized the sound from Alexander’s memories: a question Celine preferred asking before she risked changing her mind.

“Aunt Yselle wrote?”

Edran showed her the envelope.

Celine came close enough to read the addressee’s name, then looked back at him.

“For me too?”

“She wants me to see the pumps.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

Alexander lowered the sheet. She held a book against her chest, a bent finger pressed between its pages as though a bookmark were not enough to guarantee the place.

“This one is for me. I don’t know whether another arrived.”

“I asked Maurice. He was still looking.”

Celine looked at the paper on the desk without reaching for it. He offered it so she could read the invitation; she took only the less stained edge.

“On the fifth I’ve already told Sabine I want to go to the library.”

“It isn’t an order to come with me.”

“I know. I wanted to know whether you’d already decided to tell me I could choose.”

Edran accepted the small defeat with a nod. She handed back the letter, pleased to have found the point at which his kindness became predictable.

“I’m sorry I still haven’t come with you.”

“It isn’t the same outing I asked for before. The books change.”

He looked at the volume against her chest.

“Do you like that one?”

Celine hesitated, her expression once more that of someone with a second question.

She opened the page she had kept with her finger. A drawing showed a lever, two weights and an explanation taking up almost the whole margin. One word had been underlined.

“Aunt explained it. I remember the result, not how.”

Alexander recognized the subject. The duke’s memories gave him a day spent looking out of a window while Martin made a similar drawing. Edran, however, could see the relationship between force and distance clearly. What he did not remember was which part the girl had already been taught.

“Show me where you stopped.”

Celine pointed to the underlined word. The sentence asked her to change a point on the figure, but she had moved the weight along with the support. That made the arrows in the next step seem to contradict one another.

“You don’t move that as well,” he said.

“But if the point goes here…”

She came to the desk and showed him the diagram with a fingertip. Alexander took a free sheet and copied only the line of the lever.

“What we move is the support. The weight stays where it is.”

Celine stared at it.

He took the paper knife, rested it on the pencil and set a securely closed inkwell at one end. The metal tilted. He held it still with two fingers before the ink could become an altogether different demonstration.

“Here I can press without coming closer to the weight.”

“If you break the paper knife, Maurice will say you shouldn’t have rested like this.”

“Then let’s try to finish before he arrives.”

She put down the open book. Alexander replaced the inkwell with a small paperweight and had her press the free end. Celine felt the weight lift. Then he moved the pencil without moving the paperweight.

“Now try.”

She pressed again. The metal went down less, and the weight tried to slide toward the middle. Edran stopped it with a finger.

“That wasn’t in the drawing,” Celine said.

“In the drawing no one has to chase it across the floor. But this is the change you need to look at.”

He had her repeat the pressure before and after. She stopped staring at the diagram and looked at the pencil. When she returned to the page, she followed the arrow with her finger to the new support point.

“I moved two things.”

“Yes.”

“Martin would have said that before the paper knife.”

“Martin knows how to teach. I’ve managed to make you dirty my desk.”

Celine looked at the little graphite mark the pencil had left. She took the cloth from the edge of the desk and began wiping it, then stopped.

“What if I put it even closer?”

Edran let her try. This time the paperweight really slid; they both caught it, one hand on the other. She burst out laughing before setting it still again.

He realized he had remembered the way Celine laughed, not the actual weight of her hand. He left her the paper knife and returned to the book. The next line concerned exactly what they had just seen.

When Maurice returned with the second envelope, he found them bent over the same page.

Celine looked up at once.

It was the reply she had been waiting for. She recognized the paper, not the name. She took it and moved away from the desk to read; Edran remained with his half of the reasoning and a book his sister seemed to have forgotten she wanted back.

“I’ll bring it later,” she said, already at the door.

“The book or the solution?”

“Whichever comes first.”

Maurice waited until her steps had faded before asking for the duke’s reply. Alexander signed it, checked the address and handed it over.

Later, Albin brought the matters needing a decision before his departure. They laid them out without burying Celine’s drawing. One issue could be dealt with that morning; another needed clarification that had not arrived.

“They won’t wait for your return to seek it,” the administrator said.

“Good. I couldn’t invent it from a carriage window.”

Albin made no comment. He indicated the person who would receive the answer and left the duke only the part of the decision that remained his.

Edran signed the last instructions and handed them to Albin. The administrator gathered up the papers; the book and the pencil mark remained on the table. He liked how little the departure resembled an escape.

When he was alone, he picked up his sister’s volume again.

Beneath the lever was the name of a man who had written three generations before Alexander’s birth. The note called him an ancient author. Edran ran his thumb beside the letters and went still.

Ancient.

In the duke’s life, that word had a precise place: books, coins, stories given a particular tone of voice. In his own there were people who had already forgotten more generations than that name had passed through. He tried setting the two times beside each other and found no reference that truly belonged to both.

Yselle was alive. He could hear it in the impatient handwriting, the stain at the edge, the choice to invite a nephew without first telling him how moved he was supposed to be.

The people he wanted to seek in the world had not sent him a letter.

He closed the book. A silence so long could not yield a conclusion: perhaps they did not know where to find him, perhaps they no longer existed, perhaps their silence had a reason he would hate discovering.

The thought left a cold, lucid anger, at last without a household object on which to spend it.

He rose and went to the window. In the courtyard a man carried a bundle of boards, followed by someone showing him where to put them. The carriage was not yet ready for him, and none of the people below had made him the center of their afternoon.

He would go to Lethorne on the fifth. He would see his aunt’s work, speak with Martin and return Celine’s book with an answer he could defend.

Then he would find something to read that did not call a man born only yesterday ancient.

Chapter 43 — The Open Drawing

Martin read the first instruction with the sheet so far from his face that Elias thought he was missing his glasses.

“Open the head.”

He looked up at the pump. Its head, beneath the workshop windows, had four bolts and two things that might have been handles. One of them was part of the pipe.

“Mine or the machine's?”

Yselle went on cleaning a brush over a jar. “Start with the more useful one.”

“See? That's why I married you. The instructions are always encouraging.”

Elias hid his smile behind his folder. He had returned to the workshop that afternoon with provisional dimensions and an excellent reason to keep the engineer's husband from touching the pipe. But Martin was not a visitor expecting entertainment. He had put his jacket beyond the reach of splashes, rolled his sleeves, and arranged the wrenches on the bench according to their openings. Chalk from his morning lesson still clung to the seam of one cuff.

“If you tell me,” he said to Elias, “she wins.”

Yselle put down the brush. “I'm not competing.”

Martin pointed to the sheet. “There is the first false statement of our experiment.”

Elias studied the drawing. A heavy line indicated the cylinder's upper rim; no line pointed to the head. He recognized the component because he had seen it open the previous day. The sheet would not have taught him.

“May I add an arrow?”

“You may record where my husband gets lost,” Yselle replied. “You may not help him find his way before he has become lost.”

Martin approached the machine. A container beneath the base caught the remaining water. The drive was stopped and disconnected; the handle rested along the frame. Elias watched the teacher distinguish the catch from the securing bolts. He chose the right wrench on his second attempt and did not try forcing the part that refused to move. He lacked information, not capable hands.

“First question,” Martin said. “Should I drain it?”

“You already did.”

“I did because you told me when I arrived. It isn't here.”

Elias wrote the sentence at the beginning. Before loosening the head, isolate and drain. Yselle bent to read it and pointed out a word to replace: this was not an ordinary lid, and two people were already giving it different names.

“Head,” he said.

“Head,” she confirmed. “The same word on the drawing, too.”

Meanwhile Martin had put the four bolts in a shallow box. Elias might have left them on the bench. Its rim would keep one from rolling into the channel, and now he could hardly believe he had not thought of it. He noted that as well, without turning it into a command. The sheet needed room for things that could actually cause a mistake.

The head lifted. Martin waited for Elias to take the other side, and they carried it onto two dry strips of wood. The gasket clung to the metal at one point. Yselle made a small movement with her hand, then closed it on her apron.

“Do you have a suggestion?” her husband asked.

“Several. I'm saving the most offensive.”

Martin did not tear the gasket. He raised its edge with a flat tool and worked it free. “Write that it shouldn't be lifted by that edge.”

Elias wrote it down. The component that most interested him, the removable liner, was now visible through the opening. A polished band caught the window light; farther down, the water left a smell of iron. Martin searched the illustration for the two lifting holes.

“These?”

Elias nodded, and Yselle coughed.

“He asked whether those were the holes.”

“And you gave him the answer.”

“They're the only two holes.”

“Then he didn't need it.”

Martin laughed and fitted the lifting tool. The component rose freely through the first stretch, then stopped with a sharp knock. Elias felt it through his arm: a brief stop, different from the long friction he had found on June second. He looked at Yselle. She did not move.

“It isn't the narrowing anymore,” he said.

“No.”

Martin tried turning the tool a little. The liner moved with his hand and stopped again, higher up. There was no distorted mouth; something prevented the component's shoulder from passing the edge.

“My wife has designed a machine whose removable component cannot be removed,” he declared.

“My husband skipped the third line.”

“The third line says: release the catch.”

“Exactly.”

“I released a catch. Have you hidden others as prizes?”

Yselle finally came closer. Beneath the removed head, a tab retained the liner's rim. It appeared on the drawing, very small, beside the linkage. It looked like part of a dimension line. Elias remembered moving it himself during the previous test; Yselle had only told him to hold the component up.

Martin lowered the liner onto its seat. “If I pull harder, what do I damage?”

“The tab,” Yselle said. “Then the rim.”

“And if I'm less strong?”

“You'll call someone and say it won't come out.”

“And what will he say about your pump?”

Yselle took the pencil from Elias's fingers. She looked less amused than before. On the clean margin she enlarged the detail, drew a line to the actual tab, and wrote a second name, which she then crossed out.

“Liner catch,” she said. “The drive catch stays the drive catch.”

Martin rested his hands on his hips and waited. Elias watched the sheet fill. He had spent the morning making distances exact, and now a man who had read three lines had shown him a different distance: the one between an object you knew and the paper you put into someone else's hands.

The released tab made a tiny sound. Martin lifted again, and this time the whole cylinder came out. The component was heavier than the drawing suggested. Elias brought the second support beneath it as the teacher set it down.

Yselle ran a finger over the place that had stopped the extraction. The surface was marked, but undamaged. Elias read relief on her face, though she did not offer it to her husband.

“You were lucky,” she said.

“I had an audience that wanted me alive.”

A cart passed outside. The wheels rattled the pane nearest the bench. For a moment the polished cylinder seemed crossed by a tree's shadow, then became only metal again. Elias rested his fingers against the rim and reached with Near Coordinates into the volume already opened. The relationship between the liner and its axis resolved with a quiet clarity, without written figures. He found the place where the narrowing had been; the correction had left an even passage. The caliper confirmed the measurement wherever it could enter.

Martin was watching him.

“You sense it before you measure it?”

“What I can reach. I don't know whether an alloy is sound because it seems straight.”

“I asked about the hole.”

Elias realized he had prepared to defend his entire profession. “Yes. This one, yes.”

“That must be satisfying.”

It was. He could not remember the last time anyone had said so without immediately asking what else he could do with it. He moved the caliper and showed Martin the difference between its reading and the reference he had reached. The teacher followed his own note with a finger, then asked whether Yselle had left enough room to insert that same instrument without removing the guide.

She took the drawing before Elias could answer.

They changed positions to put the liner back. Martin wanted the instructions beside the machine, rather than on the bench behind him; the damp sheet threatened to curl, and Yselle found him a wooden stand. Elias drew the direction of the gasket lip. Writing that it should be fitted correctly was not enough. If you fitted it backward, the sentence remained correct and the pump still leaked.

“How long have you taught?” Elias asked as Martin brought the component closer.

“Long enough to stop saying: it's obvious.”

“He says it at dinner,” Yselle observed.

“At dinner it's obvious you're about to steal my bread.”

The liner descended to its seat. Martin moved the right catch, checked the clearance again, and raised both hands. He waited for Elias to verify the fit before taking the head. This time the instructions made sense without summoning their author.

The water test took longer than expected. A drop appeared beneath the edge, so small that Elias first took it for moisture left by their fingers. Martin wiped it away, waited, and watched another form. Yselle did not blame the assembly. She had them drain it again, opened it with them, and found a fiber caught beneath the gasket. Elias raised it on the pencil tip. It looked like a pale hair.

“The cloth,” Martin said.

Yselle looked at the one they had used to dry the surface. The tear along its edge explained the fiber. They changed cloths, cleaned the surfaces, and closed the head. The second test left the metal dry. Elias watched the water reach the container in a steady stream and wished the three empty spaces on the shelf could fill simply because the work was possible now.

Martin wanted a copy of the sheet. Not to keep: he would ask someone at his school, capable with tools but unfamiliar with the prototype, to read the passage about the tab.

“Without taking him the pump,” Yselle said.

“Were you afraid I'd tuck it under my arm?”

The engineer gave him the damp sheet and kept the clean one. Then she looked at Elias's dimensions. Her smile faded over a list marked with two red strokes. He recognized the missing liners and materials still to be obtained. The prototype worked, but sending a clear drawing would not be enough to receive what they needed.

“I'll take the final dimensions to Marcel tomorrow,” he said.

“Before noon?”

“Before.”

Yselle nodded and folded the list without hiding it. Martin had stopped joking. He took the full container to the drain with both hands; Elias cleared his way. When he returned, the teacher moved the empty bolt box beside the drawing, where anyone entering would find it.

The last thing Elias added was not a measurement. He drew the little box, the head on its strips of wood, and the liner resting horizontally. On the sheet, at last, the parts removed from the machine had somewhere to go.

He was about to close his folder when Martin asked him to wait. He had found another problem: the sheet told them to replace the head before they could see whether the catch was in position. Whoever did the work could close everything without knowing whether the tab had actually engaged.

Elias returned to the machine. A side slot revealed part of the tab. From where he stood, the metal's reflection seemed sufficient; from Martin's side nothing showed. They exchanged places, and the view disappeared for Elias too.

“A notch here,” he said, touching the outer edge.

Yselle stopped his pencil. “On the actual catch or on the drawing?”

He looked at both. “Both. So you can see when it's home.”

The engineer found a point and made the mark on the accessible surface without weakening the tab. Martin crouched to check that the notch showed from the worker's side. Then he and Elias lifted the component a little: the catch held it. They would not have to discover that by dropping the liner during some future repair.

Yselle put the point away and erased a long sentence. She replaced it with the small drawing of the notch.

“Now I can send this,” Martin said.

“You can send a copy. I'm keeping this.”

Elias watched her take the drawing to the window to see it whole. It no longer looked like an elegant project for display. It bore fingerprints, one damp corner, and uneven details. Only then did the engineer smile, as if recognizing a machine she had previously seen from too close.

Chapter 44 — The Security

The pot had begun to burn while Yselle searched for the right sheet.

Martin came over from the table, lifted the lid, and turned off the heat. He opened the window before facing her. Someone was beating a rug in the courtyard; with every blow, a gray cloud passed the sill.

“A difficult choice,” he said. “Would you rather eat smoke or dust?”

“There are carrots.”

He looked into the pot. “There were.”

Yselle put the papers on the stool, finally beyond the steam, and took two plates. Her husband left her the clean spoon. The upper part of dinner could be rescued; the lower part would require a thorough scraping. She served him with an irritated precision that neither he nor the carrots deserved.

Martin tasted it. “The salt survived.”

“Eat it before it changes its mind.”

They sat. It was June third, and enough light still came through the window to read the headings on the papers. Yselle had promised herself she would not touch them until after the meal. Instead she watched the fold of the sheet on the stool, where one word remained uncovered: security.

Her husband pushed the bread toward her. He said nothing. That absence of a question made her wish, with unreasonable impatience, that he would ask.

“The liner came out,” she said.

“I noticed. I played an important part.”

“I mean without distorting. After the second assembly, too.”

“And the drawing is better.”

Yselle turned the spoon against her plate. The food seemed too hot to eat and too cold to justify leaving it there. “If a drawing were all we needed, we would be finished.”

Martin stopped with the bread in his hand.

This was not the first evening they had discussed the project. He knew about the material problem, the June eighth deadline, the difference between a demonstrated machine and an order someone was prepared to support. He knew his wife had pledged her shares. He had asked how much risk there was, and Yselle had answered that the security was proportionate to the work. That was a precise sentence only until you tried living inside it.

“I brought the contract,” she said.

Martin finished chewing. “For me?”

“So we can look at it together.”

“I thought you had already shown it to the people who needed to read it.”

“They've read it.”

He did not immediately reach out. That small delay was worse than a reproach. Yselle got up, fetched the contract, and put it on the clear stretch between their plates. A wrinkle in the tablecloth prevented it from opening flat; she smoothed the cloth with her palm before turning to the clause.

Martin read slowly. He taught mechanics; interpreting other people's precautions was not his livelihood. He returned to the start of the paragraph and followed a line with his nail.

“It says here they can claim against the pledged shares.”

“Yes.”

“And what remains yours to decide if they do?”

Yselle had imagined questions about money. This one entered the place where she had stopped looking for an innocent word.

“Less than now.”

“Yselle.”

She pulled her plate closer, needing something to move. “The part that lets me oppose a decision wouldn't be the same. And I might never get it back.”

Martin lowered his hand. Another blow sounded from the courtyard. Less dust came in now; someone had moved the frame. Yselle could have described that person and where they worked instead of remaining seated opposite her husband.

“When did you sign?”

She told him without checking a calendar. She remembered the room, the scratching pen, the dry skin on the knuckle of the man who had shown her where. Nothing in the memory resembled an impulse. She had calculated, negotiated, rejected other forms of commitment. That was precisely why it had been easy to tell him she had settled the matter.

“I wasn't asking your permission,” she said.

“I didn't say you should have.”

“I know.”

Her husband leaned against the chair back. The wood gave a creak she had known for years. “You told me you were risking part of the value. You didn't tell me you might lose your say in what happens afterward.”

She looked at the line beneath his nail. She had read it many times. It had not changed because Martin was reading it now.

“I thought I was making it smaller for you while I could still solve it.”

“For whom?”

Yselle did not answer. It was a question he sometimes asked pupils who had reached the correct result with the wrong figure. She would have preferred him to use it at school.

Martin picked up his spoon and immediately set it down. “I'm angry. Can I be angry without you having to prove the project is good?”

“The project is good.”

“I know.”

The certainty with which he said it made her feel worse, then better somewhere she could not have named. She took a breath long enough to smell the smoke still in the room.

“I don't want to end up asking a board for permission to do the work they keep me there to do.”

Martin looked at the contract, then at her. “And I don't want to find out afterward what price you've accepted to avoid that.”

The bread remained between them, untouched. Yselle found the table edge beneath the cloth. It was chipped at the corner where they had struck it bringing their first bench into the house; Martin had never sanded it properly. Facing the contract, that household flaw seemed something solid to rest her thumb against.

“Alexander is coming on the fifth,” she said.

“I know.”

“I haven't asked him to cover the security.”

“I know that too. I read the invitation while you were looking for an envelope without stains.”

She closed her eyes for a moment. “He might want to see something that wasn't made to receive him.”

“It will be good for him. For you too.”

“He can say no.”

Martin frowned. “To what?”

Yselle realized she had already assembled a request in her mind that she had never written. It was not money, yet. It was the expectation that a nephew who had become a duke should find his aunt's possible failure interesting. She rubbed her forehead, perhaps leaving another workshop stain.

“To taking an interest. To coming back. To wanting a part in this.”

Her husband nodded. He offered no consolation borrowed from someone else's wealth.

They finished eating with the contract open. Martin rescued the last carrots that did not taste burned and carried the pot to the water. She told him to let it soak. He tried with the spoon anyway, made an unbearable sound, and admitted she was right about that metal, at least.

Yselle cleared the table. She returned with a clean drawing board, the lamp, and the estimate. Her husband stood behind his chair, still far enough away that they could not pretend everything was resolved.

“I want to prepare the smaller version,” she said.

“One machine?”

“One service line, to begin with. The same required height, less simultaneous work. We'll have to tell the Mutual what changes.”

Martin took the chair. “How much of what you've already paid for remains useful?”

At last it was a question she could answer with an object. Yselle opened the drawing and pointed to the removable liner and the guide. The courtyard survey remained useful; so did the work on the surfaces, the gasket test, the correction to the internal passage. Components ordered but not obtained needed a separate distinction. She could not cancel a cost merely by erasing the line from a drawing.

She made two lists. Martin removed their nephew's letter from beneath her elbow before she could stain it with ink. It was not enough tenderness to make peace, but it was the gesture of a man who intended to remain at that table.

“Doesn't that reduce the time to fill the tank?” he asked, indicating one change.

“It increases it.”

“Then don't just write lower capacity. Write how long they'll wait, once we have the figure.”

Yselle erased the phrase. That wait had arms, buckets, and a woman who had to choose who could use the courtyard. She would not leave Noemi to translate a saving into all its consequences.

Before going on, Yselle searched the drawer for the pipe sample she had kept. She put it between the bread and the lamp. Its edge had the flare she had rejected for the larger version. It might be enough for the smaller one; she still had to check whether the connection left the locking ring accessible.

Martin brushed the crumbs aside and held the component while she set the drawing board on edge. The line passed too close to the support. Yselle shifted the outlet, but her husband made her try with a real wrench from the box near the door. The handle struck the wood before completing its travel.

She looked at the marked point, then at the sample. The smaller version would not come into being simply by erasing two machines. She removed the pipe from the table and began a second view in the margin, leaving room for the hand that would have to tighten the ring.

They drew until the window became a black mirror. Yselle did not like the smaller version. It had the humiliating appearance of a compromise that genuinely worked: it preserved the part of the project she wanted to defend and lost some of the service she had promised. Martin checked the lever's travel and found an obstruction she had allowed to pass through a support. Yselle corrected it without arguing. Her hands had resumed work before her irritation surrendered.

Eventually he got up to wash the pot. This time its bottom came away in soft flakes. She heard him humming a tune that never ended in the same place, and had to stop herself from calling merely to hear his reply.

When he returned, she showed him the smaller drawing. “If nothing changes on the fifth, I'll send this with its limits. I won't wait until the eighth to say something is missing.”

Martin studied it. “And if things change?”

“I'll redo the figures.”

“All of them?”

She met his eyes. “The ones I haven't told you about, too.”

Her husband laid his hand on the edge of the drawing without covering the lines. Yselle rested hers beside it. They did not immediately take each other's hands. It was strange how much space could lie between two thumbs on the same sheet.

“I'm teaching late tomorrow,” he said. “Leave me the paper where I can find it.”

“Which paper?”

“The one you're afraid of.”

Yselle wanted to make a joke. Instead she slid the contract toward him. Martin folded it along its existing creases, with a care that did not remove his anger. He left it beneath the lamp beside the smaller alternative, and only then brushed her fingers.

She remained at the table after he had closed the window. No sound rose from the dark courtyard. The pump in the workshop was stopped, dry where it should be, capable of working. June eighth had not moved farther away. The security clause seemed no less dangerous.

Yselle took a fresh page and wrote Noemi's name at the top. Beneath it she listed the things she would have to tell her if they chose the smaller version. The first was time. The second was what they had already promised. At the third she raised her head and called Martin, because he had seen something in the lever's travel that she wanted to check again.

Her husband answered from the next room, his voice full of the bread he had finally decided to eat.

Chapter 45 — Six Crowns to Earn

Marcel read the dimension a second time and turned the drawing to see whether the figure appeared on the other side too.

Elias pointed to the section. The line ended in the right place, before the gasket seat. He had gone to sleep afraid he had confused that stretch with the usable opening; now he seemed to see his mistake before the master even spoke.

“It's right,” Marcel said.

“I checked again.”

“I noticed. You've written it three times.”

Elias took his finger off the sheet. It was June fourth; the dimensions had to leave that morning. On the drawing, the enlarged detail of the catch occupied the space he had originally reserved for his signature. He would have to sign farther down. He did not mind.

Marcel put the instruction copy beside the drawing and slid a ruler along the edge to hold the fold open. The small picture of the notch had survived copying better than the words around it.

“Did Martin ask for this?”

“He tried pulling without seeing it.”

“How hard?”

“Hard enough to make us change the sheet.”

The master smiled. Then he separated the documents for delivery from those that would remain at the workshop. Elias saw the record of his assignment appear and straightened involuntarily.

“Six additional crowns,” Marcel said. “For your contribution and the assistance we're agreeing on. At the end of the assignment.”

The figure remained between them for a moment. Elias had thought about payment several times, and never found a way to ask without seeming to have moved on before finishing the work.

“For tomorrow too?”

“Tomorrow too. And the work expected afterward. If that changes, we'll discuss it.”

“If it takes more time?”

Marcel looked up. “Before you give it away.”

Elias studied the record. His name was not followed by a new qualification. There was the task, the days still to be agreed, and the figure listed separately from his wages. Six crowns did not sound like coins in his palm, but for the first time they let him imagine the end of this work without filling it entirely with gratitude.

“All right,” he said.

Marcel passed him the pen. Elias confirmed the part concerning him and let the ink dry. Then they wrapped the drawing in a rigid cover. The reinforced fold must not crush the corner of the instructions. The master checked the address; Elias checked the list of sheets.

Before the delivery, Marcel had him check the drawing in its wrapping. One edge rubbed against the rigid fastening. Elias slipped a scrap beneath the corner and pulled gently: the paper slid without creasing. He loosened the tie, then retied it firmly enough to keep the package closed. The master lifted the parcel and handed it to him. Now it could be carried without keeping two fingers against the fragile point.

The person who came to collect them did not need to hear how much work had gone into putting that notch in the right place.

Once the documents had gone, the bench seemed too large.

Elias still had to clean the caliper and check the points. He opened his toolbox. One empty slot immediately made him afraid he had left a tool at Yselle’s workshop; instead he found it beneath the cloth, wrapped to keep it from marking the wood. He had done that himself. He returned it to where he was used to looking.

“The duke will be there tomorrow,” he said.

“I know.”

“Yselle wants us there too.”

“So we'll go.”

Marcel continued counting the parts of a small regulator. Elias wanted a longer sentence, perhaps a story about an important customer in whose presence the master had spilled oil. Instead Marcel found a spring caught in the fabric and showed him the solution to the problem that interested him.

“Should I call him Your Grace?”

The master raised his head. “When you address him, yes. If he asks for a figure, give him the figure.”

“And if I don't know?”

“You call me. That's why I'm coming.”

The answer was too practical to allow Elias to continue being elegantly afraid. He finished cleaning the caliper.

Livia arrived during the break, a parcel beneath her arm and a printed sheet poking from her pocket. There was an ink stain between her thumb and forefinger; Elias recognized the kind that resisted the first washing and worsened at the second.

“Edith says this has to stop drinking,” she announced, setting the parcel on the clear bench.

Inside was the nurse's notebook, carefully protected enough to survive the journey. One corner of the cover bore an old damp mark. Livia opened it only far enough to show the binding, keeping the written pages closed.

“The wrapping slips. When she tries to pull it out with one hand, the wrapping comes away and the notebook stays behind.”

“A nurse ought to have at least four hands.”

“One of them for hitting whoever suggests that.”

Elias found a scrap of fabric among the instrument covers. He spread it beside the notebook. It was not large enough, but it could help test the fold. Livia already had two samples tucked into the parcel. One was thin and flexible; the other stiffer, with an edge that tended to scrape the back of the hand.

She tried folding the first. “This withstands water better than it withstands Edith closing it in a hurry.”

Elias took a piece of cardboard of the right size and passed it inside the fold. “It mustn't grip here. If it catches the cover's edge, it takes the whole thing away with it.”

Livia lifted the notebook by the wrong point. The wrapping stayed on the bench, and she showed him the result with satisfaction.

“You've built a different way to fail.”

“It's a prototype.”

“Oh. Then we can blame the materials.”

He laughed and shortened the flap. They tested it without opening the pages, using the cardboard for movements that might crease them. By the third fold, the spine could slide while the edge stayed caught. Livia tested it with her left hand, then her right. She chose softer reinforcement where Edith would insert her thumb and marked it with chalk.

“She can keep this even while I'm asleep,” she said.

Elias looked at the stain on her hand. “Are you tired?”

“I still have copies to finish.”

She did not say it as an invitation to investigate. She put the notebook away and kept the folded cardboard, which she would show Edith before having the good cover cut. Elias told her about the test with Martin, the liner finally coming out, and the visit planned for the next day. He left out Yselle's financial papers: he remembered the list, but had no story to hand around with lunch.

At the duke's name, Livia stopped tying the knot.

“Alexander Valcienne?”

“Do you know many other dukes who are her nephews?”

She hit his arm with the cardboard. “You start being funny when you're frightened.”

“Then tomorrow they'll want to keep me until evening.”

Livia tightened the knot and placed the parcel away from the cups. “Is he the one who asked you to do the work?”

“No. Marcel was commissioned. Yselle wants to show him the prototype.”

“So he has something to look at.”

Elias thought of the machine beneath the windows. As long as he had imagined it in front of a duke, he had forgotten that the prototype would not change shape when the carriage arrived. The liner had to come out the same way; the distances would not bow.

Livia took out the printed sheet. It was a new installment of the novel she had begun. The edge was badly trimmed and the title crossed a printing stain, but the corridor illustration occupied almost half the page.

“Don't look at the wall,” she said.

Elias looked at it.

“I haven't opened my mouth.”

“You made the face.”

In the picture, a man vanished into a passage between two rooms. The caption promised an impossible return. Elias held the sheet by its margin and searched his memory for the previous installment's drawing. “You said the room faced the street.”

“The room does face the street.”

“Then behind that wall is the street.”

Livia took the page away. “And he'll cross it very discreetly.”

At the nearby bench, Marcel made a sound that might have been a cough. Elias looked at him. The master was bent over the regulator with unconvincing concentration.

“It could be an alcove,” Elias tried.

“For a man two fingers wide?”

“We don't know how wide he is yet.”

Livia gave in to laughter. To avoid upsetting her cup she had to put the sheet on her knees. Elias felt a knot he had carried since morning loosen; the duke remained where he was, a day away, while Elias tried defending a passage that did not belong to him.

They read the first exchange in low voices. Livia refused to give him the role of the character who explained everything at the end; Elias chose the one presumed dead, because he would not have to speak for two paragraphs. They stopped before the reversal, when she checked the clock and put the installment back in her pocket. She would not tell him how it ended even when he asked whether the wall would be acquitted.

After she left, Elias prepared the tools for the visit. He left the things needed for ordinary work at the workshop and packed the caliper, suitable points, his notebook, and a spare pencil. He tested the clasp twice. It made the same small, reliable sound.

At home that evening, Lise had occupied the center of the table. She was working on a page of her own and raised a hand when he brought his toolbox near the edge.

“The chair.”

Elias put it on the chair. Adrien was reassembling something beneath the lamp; a small tray kept the screws apart. The place where Elias usually dropped his gloves was already full.

“Marcel has agreed on six extra crowns for this assignment,” he told his father.

Adrien looked up. “When?”

“At the end.”

“And what's left?”

“The tests. The assistance. It depends how tomorrow goes.”

His father nodded and asked about the liner. Elias described the catch hidden in the drawing and imitated Martin accusing Yselle of distributing extra catches as prizes. Lise laughed without leaving her work. He wanted to ask about her interview, but nothing on the table announced its outcome, and instead she began asking what the duke would see.

He described the pump and the Mutual's tank. His sister listened most closely to the maintenance. When he said you only had to pull out the liner, she looked over her page.

“Only?”

Elias sighed. “There's a new sheet explaining how much only.”

After dinner he cleaned his jacket with a cloth. Lise showed him a dry stain on the cuff that he would not have noticed until greeting someone. The glue came away in flakes; beneath it the fabric was a little paler. He did not own a jacket capable of looking new overnight.

In his room he reopened the caliper case and cleaned the groove where dust had gathered beneath the catch. He closed it and returned it to the toolbox prepared for morning. He brought out his jacket, hanging it so the cuff could finish drying.

From the doorway, Lise asked whether a duke knew how to recognize good work.

“I'll tell you tomorrow.”

“Tell him stains don't count.”

Elias turned toward the cuff. His sister had already gone. He arranged the cloth on the chair back beside his jacket and realized that, for the first time, he wanted tomorrow to arrive at least as much as he feared it.

Chapter 46 — Borrowed Ink

The word heirs ended beneath a stain that did not belong to the original.

Livia raised her sleeve and found the dark spot on her cuff. She had rested her arm on the sheet before the ink dried. In the document she was copying, the same word had thin, clean letters. In her copy it looked as though someone had rubbed a finger across a family's name to erase it.

She could not deliver it like that.

She set the sheet on the discard shelf and took another. Only a few pages remained, but the ruined one was dense: names, property shares, a sentence that resumed below the lower margin and seemed to have forgotten where it was going. The original manuscript at least had the courtesy not to lie about its own ugliness.

It was the afternoon of June fourth; the copyists' room held the street's heat. A colleague had gone out, leaving a chair in the passage. Voices came through the window; a woman was calling someone who, judging by her growing irritation, could hear her perfectly well. Livia moved the chair with her foot and shifted the inkwell beyond her sleeve's reach.

In her parcel, the cardboard for Edith's cover held its new fold. She had checked it on the way back from the workshop. The notebook did not need her to think about keeping it dry every moment. Now she wished her own arm could remember where the page ended.

She began again at the first line. With Held Moment she preserved the passage she was reading, clear enough that she need not keep searching for the names. Her fingers still had to form the letters.
