The ability would not bring her a finished sheet while she rested, and after two lines she paused to ease the tension near her thumb.

The clerk arrived just then.

“Someone is asking for you.”

Livia looked at her open hand. “For a copy?”

“He said he knows you. Uren.”

The name brought back a wrapped sample and correspondence that had not all been written on the same side of the truth. Ilian had not come to request an extract from the inheritance document. She checked the clock, then the pages still to do.

“May I see him at the entrance table?”

The clerk nodded. Livia covered the original, put down her pen, and took only her own notebook. Slanting light and the smell of warmed paper filled the entrance. Ilian stood beside the public table with his case pressed beneath one arm. His coat had a crease on the shoulder that seemed made by a strap's edge.

“I wasn't sure I'd find you here,” he said.

“You found me.”

“I won't take long.”

Livia looked at the case. “What do you need?”

He put it down without immediately opening it. The sides of his fingernails were marked, as though he had been working on something small and stubborn. “A different question. A simpler one.”

The copyist indicated a chair and took the one opposite. The table was wide enough for both sets of papers. It was not wide enough to ignore what lay in front of the other person.

Ilian took out a letter and a drawing. The component shown was a metal collar, split so it could close around an existing element. A gauge was to establish its opening and alignment; dimensions were blank where the actual object needed measuring. No illustration showed people in transit. Livia read the request down to the signature before speaking.

“You want me to find a surveyor?”

“I wanted to know whether Ruy could take it on. Or Veyne, with him. It's an ordinary collar. They aren't asking for a connection.”

“Have you seen what needs measuring?”

“The collar, yes. The complete assembly, no.”

Livia returned to the text. The first sentence concerned a comparison of external dimensions. The second requested confirmation of compatibility with the stated use. She stopped there. Ilian had followed her gaze closely enough to know which part she was reading.

“I already had that changed,” he said.

He opened his notebook and showed her an earlier draft. The initial question took up no more lines, but joined the physical comparison to a phrase about the usability of the whole. There were different words in the new text. Half of the phrase that concerned Livia had survived.

“What use have they stated?”

“What it says. A support for an apparatus.”

“Which apparatus?”

Ilian looked down at the drawing. “They didn't give me that to draw.”

The answer was dry, irritated above all by its own insufficiency. Livia knew that voice: it belonged to someone who had prepared for a question and discovered he did not have the answer he thought he had.

“May I read your request for payment too?” she asked.

He looked at her. “That isn't the work I'm asking you to do.”

“You put the two sheets together.”

Ilian removed the bill from the case and set it beside the letter. The balance for the previous work remained outstanding. A line added to the message said that defining the new inspection would allow the overall matter to be concluded more quickly.

Outside, the woman finally stopped calling. In the resulting quiet, the scrape of Ilian's heel on the floor sounded very loud.

“If I can bring an answer, I'll have something when I go back,” he said.

Livia did not tell him he could simply stay away. The purchased paper, the hours already delivered, and the client who decided when a matter was closed lay on the table before her; the draftsman did not need to describe his finances.

“I can compare these texts. I can't accept on Marcel's behalf.”

“I'm not asking you to.”

“Or Elias's.”

“I thought talking to you would keep me from arriving with the wrong question again.”

Livia looked at the newer sheet. The man had brought her precisely the work she knew how to do. Her annoyance shifted: it did not vanish, but ceased to point entirely toward the opposite chair.

She asked permission to copy the passage. Ilian nodded and moved the bill to make room. The copyist transcribed the whole sentence, then the one from the earlier version. Beneath them she added the date and circumstances in which she had seen them. She showed him her words before investing True Mark in the relationship between her observation and the text.

The mark looked no finer than the neighboring ink. Livia felt its precise hold, limited to the fact actually before her. She had not seen the client dictate the words. She had seen Ilian produce two sheets, and could preserve unambiguously which sentence appeared on each.

“This attests that you've compared them?” he asked.

“That these words were in the texts you showed me. Not that the collar is suitable.”

Ilian followed the lines with his nail. “Can you also note that I didn't ask you to certify it?”

She looked up. “Yes.”

She did so. She gave him the page, waited for him to read it, and copied the necessary passage for herself without keeping his correspondence. The bill returned to Ilian's case with a small rasp of paper against the edge. He straightened it immediately.

“I want to get paid without giving them another week,” he said.

“Then the technical question has to stand on its own.”

She took the collar drawing and indicated the blank spaces. They required an object, access, and someone competent to measure it. The other phrase required a defined use. Asking for one thing did not automatically obtain the other. Ilian did not need her to explain how to draw metal: he separated the two requests himself in the margin of his draft.

“Like that?”

Livia read it. “That I can take to Marcel.”

“Today?”

She glanced toward the copyists' room. The stained page still waited beside the original; nobody would rewrite it because a more interesting problem had appeared at the entrance table.

“When I've finished my work. I can't promise you an answer tonight.”

Ilian picked up the letter. He seemed to age as he folded it, then asked only where he could receive news. They agreed on the address already used by the workshop. Sending an answer did not require opening every place in Livia's life to him.

As he rose, the draftsman hesitated with his hand on the case. “Is Veyne working again?”

“He's working.”

Ilian nodded, and this time smiled enough to show her that the question had not concerned commercial information. Livia did not add the duke's visit. He said goodbye and stepped into the sunlight beyond the door, turning his shoulder to avoid someone entering with a bundle of records.

The copyist watched the copy of the two sentences for a moment. An artisan could carry out the new request without touching anything extraordinary. That was precisely why its words needed to end where the work ended. The collar looked harmless on paper; the way it was being paid for looked less so.

She returned to the room. Her place had retained the shape of her absence: the pen laid across it, the original covered, the clean sheet she had just begun. Her sleeve threatened to slip again; she secured it above her wrist with a ribbon.

She resumed copying at the heirs' name. This time she let each stroke dry before moving her hand. The ache near her thumb forced a short pause. Beneath the table she opened her fingers and closed them one at a time, feeling how little the tendon cared about the world's problems.

When she finished the page, the lower margin coincided with the end of the sentence. Livia checked it against the original and felt a simple, almost physical pleasure. She put the sheet where nobody passing could brush it.

The clerk returned to ask whether he could collect the file. Livia showed him the completed pages and kept the original beneath her hand. He looked at the discarded sheet; the stain seemed larger now it was dry.

“Should this be thrown away?”

“After the comparison.”

He said the man waiting had sent to ask twice. The copyist felt a sharp answer rising and stopped it long enough to ask the person's name. It was the one on the commission. No urgent arrival, no altered deadline; a man did not want to remain seated longer than expected.

Livia indicated the blank page. “Tell him this one remains. If he wants to come back, the file will stay here.”

The clerk went to report. She brought the two completed pages closer and checked the names. In a central line, the second heir's surname had a letter that could be read two ways. In the original, the pen had skipped over a ridge in the paper. Livia remembered the exact shape, yet that precision did not tell her which letter the writer had intended.

She marked the place in her copy's margin, called the clerk again, and had him take the question to the man who had supplied the original. He returned shortly with the name written on a scrap. This time the letter left no alternative. Livia attached the clarification and completed the line.

She had lost more minutes to that name. But the waiting man's delay had acquired a purpose. She could give him a file in which his brother did not become a stranger because of a faulty pen point.

One last copy remained. Before beginning, she put the note about Ilian in a different pocket from the novel installment and Edith's cover. The edges of the three papers had briefly made her think she could carry them all the same way.

The fresh page waited. Livia moved the inkwell to the safe place, chose a point that did not scratch, and wrote the first name with her hand still tired.

Chapter 47 — Twenty-Three Kilometers

Celine slipped the letter into the wrong pocket of his coat and immediately accused him of losing it.

Alexander took it out. The envelope was smaller than Yselle's invitation, sealed with a care his sister had not devoted to handing it over. Their aunt's name ran diagonally across the front to avoid a slight crease.

“Here,” he said, putting it in the opposite inner pocket.

“That's where you put important papers.”

“Exactly.”

She watched him long enough to decide whether he was teasing. Then she turned toward Maurice, who waited at the door with something somebody else had forgotten. The duke was not the only person leaving or running late that morning.

“I'll be back after my lesson,” Celine said.

Alexander nodded. He wanted to ask whether there was an answer hidden in the name on the envelope, but had no wish to begin the journey by demanding an explanation of a letter that was not his. Teren signaled from the carriage. Two guards had already taken their places according to the head of security's arrangements; nobody looked as though they were accompanying a man unable to endure twenty-three kilometers of road.

Maurice reminded him where messages would reach them in Lethorne. Edran recognized the agreed address, confirmed it, and climbed in. The step sank beneath his weight. The duke's body held complete memories of that movement, yet the present sensation did not feel like a memory correctly performed. The spring yielded, the seat leather held the warmth of a hand that had rested there, and he was there.

The carriage set off on the morning of June fifth.

They crossed a street still occupied by deliveries. Teren had requested a route that did not need clearing before their arrival. A porter watched them wait while he finished carrying a package into a shop, then backed his handcart just far enough. The driver thanked him. Alexander saw the man return to the second package without seeking his eyes.

Rovenne spread beyond the window in pale walls, courtyards, and corners where washing water had not dried. Two children disputed a board resting on a wheel; a woman seized its end and made both children pass beneath her arm so she could reach the door. The smaller protested so loudly that Edran heard him despite the wheels.

Teren turned his head a little.

“An inheritance dispute,” Alexander said.

“At least they know what they're fighting over.”

The reply made him look up. The head of security had resumed watching ahead. Edran wondered how long the man had been making jokes Alexander's memories had not considered worth keeping.

Beyond the last houses, cart traffic had scored the ground. The road rose a little, then revealed a more distant line of roofs. The air carried smoke, crushed grass, and briefly the stink of a ditch nobody had hidden for their passage. Alexander shifted on the seat. The carriage made him feel the road through his back.

He had wanted to go out. Now that the house no longer filled his view, he realized he had chiefly imagined leaving as the absence of his own corridors. Here instead were people and tasks that had not been assigned to distract him. The field to the left ended in an incomplete fence; a man held up part of it while someone else brought an uncooperative animal underneath.

The sound that reached the window was laughter. He could not tell who had begun.

Ancient memory offered another road: dark earth, an iron ring around the ankle of the person walking ahead, a sound every step repeated without ever completing. Edran felt his hand close on the seat's edge. He did not try to make the two images alike. The man at the fence released the support, chased the animal a few steps, and called it a ridiculous name. Nobody dragged him away for laughing.

Alexander lowered his eyes to his glove. The seam ran between thumb and forefinger; it took a moment to stop pulling it tight.

“Shall we stop?” Teren asked.

“Not yet.”

The head of security nodded without assigning an explanation to the silence. Edran was grateful for the way he returned to his work. He had agreed to travel in company, and that company did not require turning every movement of his hand into an announcement.

They reached the bridge behind a loaded cart. A wheel had met a stone at the entrance, and the driver was trying to find the angle that would free it without shifting the load. The carriage stopped. At Teren's signal, one guard got down and went to speak with the man.

Alexander watched the water beneath the planks. It was the color of things carried downstream, not the sky. A branch turned near the pier and returned to the same little eddy. The cart driver pulled the reins, then slackened them. Someone on the opposite side made a gesture too broad to be mere advice.

“If you keep pushing there, you'll put it back in the rut,” Teren said quietly.

He spoke quietly enough that the man at the cart could not hear him. Alexander followed the wheel, the stone, and the available space, and saw the problem an instant before the driver changed his grip too.

The cart edged sideways. The guard returned, dusted his palm, and said the way was clear. Their journey resumed after a wait long enough for Alexander to reread the name on the envelope.

“You said three or four hours,” he observed.

“Yes.”

“I'm beginning to understand the breadth of the estimate.”

“The cart hadn't written to announce itself.”

Edran laughed. This time Teren did not hide his smile.

Along the next stretch, factories appeared first as smoke and then as walls. Movement came through high windows, too distant to distinguish; outside, a group of men sat with food wrapped in cloth. One had taken off a shoe and was massaging his foot. Another spoke, using bread to point at something beyond the roof.

Alexander's memories knew owners' names. Edran watched the men's hands, the shoe on the ground, the way one set aside part of his food. He could not derive what they earned, what they owed, or what they would say on seeing his face. The freedom he had recognized in the gesture at the fence did not answer every question of the world that followed. But it gave him something he wanted to keep looking at.

Farther on, repairs had narrowed a stretch of road. The driver brought the wheels slowly along the side still open. Alexander saw a young man bent over an empty box, trying to retrieve a nail fallen between the stones. He indicated where the metal had caught the light. The young man looked up, followed the gesture, and found the nail. He raised it to show Alexander, then stepped aside with his box.

The carriage gathered speed before Alexander could say anything else. A pale imprint from the tight glove remained on the hand that had pointed. Edran watched it while the young man returned to work behind them. He would remember the gesture without even knowing the name of the person it had helped.

The carriage passed a woman standing with a basket against her hip. She turned because of the dust, covered her face with a handkerchief, and resumed talking to a girl seated on the low wall. Alexander waited fruitlessly for the two figures to offer a greater meaning. They remained two people engaged in a conversation he had not heard.

He leaned back.

When Lethorne's first houses appeared, the light had changed its angle and the road had used up its morning. The time had not been empty. He felt he had passed through more gestures than he could bring home without betraying them in a sentence.

The driver slowed in a narrow street. A wheelbarrow with a shrieking axle emerged from a courtyard. Teren sent word to the house without dispatching a guard to impose silence on the workshop. Their accommodation had been agreed; the rest of the stay did not need to become a procession before Alexander stepped down.

Yselle stood in the doorway when the carriage stopped.

The duke recognized her before she offered a word: the way she put her weight on one leg, the escaping lock of hair, the gaze that went from his face to his hand on the door. Memory held affection together with impatience; now both had a body moving toward his.

“You really came.”

“The road tried to discourage me.”

Yselle took his arm, looked at him closely, and slipped a hand behind his shoulder to embrace him. Edran smelled soap and some remnant of workshop work on her sleeve. She was shorter than her voice suggested. She did not restrain her embrace because a duke occupied the coat.

“You've recovered,” she said as she released him.

“People often point that out.”

“You must have looked dreadful before.”

Martin appeared behind her with a pale stain on his cuff. He greeted Alexander, hesitated for a breath, and offered his hand. His grip had the care of someone who remembered a recent illness and did not yet know how much of it he could forget.

“The journey?”

“It taught me how springs work.”

“If you've understood why that carriage transmits every pothole, I have a problem to show you.”

Yselle tapped his arm. “Let him come in first.”

Alexander produced Celine's letter before his coat changed hands. His aunt looked at the name on the envelope and smiled differently. He liked the thought that he had only been entrusted with carrying it more than he had expected.

“She told me not to lose it among the important things.”

“She's wise enough to know what a man would lose.”

Teren spoke to the person responsible for the carriage; the two guards went to the place where they could wait without obstructing the passage. Alexander followed Yselle inside. The house and the work met in a smell of bread, water, and metal he could not entirely separate.

Food lay on the table beside a stack of papers kept away from the plates. Martin moved a book to make room. Yselle opened the letter only after showing Alexander where to sit; she read it standing, smiled at one line, and folded it without explaining.

They ate something before the visit. Martin told them about a pupil who had believed he could lighten a machine by changing the scale of its drawing. Alexander laughed and asked whether the error had lasted long enough to save transporting it. Yselle's husband began answering seriously, then stopped and laughed too.

His aunt set down her cup and looked at him. “People are coming to show you the work. You needn't stay here listening to us until evening.”

“That was part of the invitation too.”

“What I left out of the invitation was the amount of paper.”

A blow against metal sounded beyond the door, followed by two voices. Yselle rose. Alexander followed her to the workshop entrance. Beneath the windows he saw the base, the prototype's body, and a bench where several hands had already prepared something to examine.

His aunt pointed out a hook for his coat. He hung it without covering the drawing fixed to the wall. Then he turned toward the voices, ready to meet whoever was arriving.

Chapter 48 — The First Meeting

Elias arrived with the toolbox in his right hand and his prepared greeting in the wrong part of his mouth.

The duke stood beside the bench. He had left his coat at the entrance; window light caught his black hair and settled in his golden eyes. Elias had imagined a seated man, surrounded by enough space for anyone entering to know immediately where to stop. Instead Alexander Valcienne was about to move a chair out of the way.

Marcel greeted him with his title. Elias followed without stumbling, then realized he was still gripping the toolbox. Martin showed him a clear corner of the bench. The young man put it down; its clasp clicked too loudly in the silence he had constructed around his own entrance.

Yselle already held the drawing.

“Veyne,” she said, introducing him. “The dimensions are his. You can ask him about the liner too.”

The duke looked at Elias. He did not smile to reassure him in advance, and the young man preferred that as yet undecided attention to a kindness he would have to earn without knowing how.

“Does this passage remain accessible with the machine assembled?”

Alexander indicated the enlarged detail. Elias approached closely enough to see the exact point and forgot the clasp.

“With the change to the edge, yes. We passed the tool through with the head in this position. Here you can see the catch's notch.”

He looked up. The duke had not looked elsewhere. Elias took the sample lifting tool left beside the prototype and put it in the position shown. The head was still closed; the usable route remained visible from outside.

“To replace the liner, the operator opens it here. This catch isn't the drive catch. The first sheet didn't make that clear.”

“It took a man of ingenuity to demonstrate it,” Martin said.

“It took a man who didn't read everything in front of him,” Yselle replied.

Alexander looked at his aunt's husband, then at the drawing. The brief smile changed his face enough to remind Elias that this was also a nephew visiting. It did not remove his height, his name, or the way the room made space for him.

“Have you tried reassembling it from these instructions?”

“The day before yesterday,” Elias said. “With one more notch to add. And a cloth to replace.”

He showed the corner where they had drawn the supports. The duke asked about the cloth. Martin described the fiber beneath the gasket without making the account a defense of his own work. Alexander bent over the head's edge; he did not touch it until Yselle showed him where it might be dirty.

Elias suddenly wanted to show him the machine running. Until that morning he had imagined every question from the duke extending the wait for judgment. Now he wanted the sound of the stroke to give the words a body.

Yselle called Gaston for the water. The space around the base had been cleared; the containers were those used in the test, large enough that nobody would need to run at the first startup. Teren remained beside the side door, with one guard visible beyond it. Alexander briefly signaled him when he moved from the drawing to the prototype. The head of security merely changed his vantage point.

The young duke indicated the tank on the site drawing.

“Is that the height you need to reach?”

Elias answered before thinking about the title. He gave the figure and showed the reference used in the Mutual's courtyard. Gaston added the water's ordinary route; Yselle let both finish before pointing to the machine's intended outlet.

“Does the old section pass there?” Alexander asked.

“The drawn route is the one surveyed,” Elias said. “For the covered stretch, we have the available references. We haven't opened every joint.”

The duke studied the difference between the pipe's line and the base's outline. He asked where the operator could stand without blocking the transport of containers. Elias showed him the passage he had seen occupied by buckets. He did not describe the visit as though he had discovered a hidden city: a low door, a staircase, two people who had to wait for someone else to pass before doing their own work.

Alexander moved the chair back half a step. The route between bench and prototype opened. Then he looked at Yselle.

“Now?”

She nodded. Martin checked the container was in place and began the test. The first sound was more modest than Elias would have wished for a duke: a dry stroke, a regular knock, the support wood answering faintly. Then water reached the outlet and fell into the container with a full sound.

Alexander followed the lever's movement. He did not merely watch the stream. He looked at the linkage, the guide, and the place where a hand would need to exert force. Elias realized he was following the duke's gaze instead of the pump and made himself return to the head's edge. It was dry.

Gaston asked a question about output that the young man could not resolve from the interval he had just observed. Marcel passed him the time reference. Elias noted volume and duration; the answer came from their relationship, not the impression that there seemed to be plenty of water.

Yselle compared it with the previous test. The value was consistent with what they expected from the prototype. The duke listened, asked about a return flow, and had them show him the relevant part of the drawing.

“If water remains above the pump when it stops?”

Yselle explained the intended behavior and the separation from the old circuit. Martin stopped operating the drive. The stream changed, thinned, and left a tail of drops. Elias looked at the container and thought of the time Noemi spent filling it by hand.

Alexander waited for the sound to end before asking to see the liner.

They opened the head with the right tools. Martin took the time needed to release the catch, lifted the component with Elias, and rested it on the wooden strips. The duke removed a glove. Yselle gave him a cloth, then the component.

The cylinder passed into his hands without visible effort. Elias remembered his own straining arm two days earlier. He could not decide whether the duke was stronger than he appeared or whether he himself had held the component in the worst possible way. Alexander's attention was already on its inner surface.

“Is this the corrected one?”

“Yes,” Yselle said. “The troublesome sample is there.”

Elias passed her the rough component. On the bench the two cylinders looked almost identical until light entered them. Alexander leaned closer, changed his angle, and asked what machining had produced the difference. Yselle answered precisely enough to force a second question.

Marcel joined the explanation when they reached the control measurement. His voice was not the deferential one of his greeting; it was the voice he used with a customer capable of understanding why something was taking time. The duke did not seem offended by the change.

Elias opened the toolbox and took out the caliper. He showed where it could enter and the part that needed a different reference. Yselle indicated the faulty liner. “He found the first narrowing, using Astrolabe.” Alexander asked whether his Effigy let him find it before dismantling.

“If I can reach the volume, I can compare its geometry. I don't get the alloy, or how it will wear, from that distance.”

“And someone without your Effigy?”

“They can check the dimensions here. That's why the drawing has the section and the access for instruments.”

The duke studied the drawing again. “So the measurement stays useful when you leave.”

Elias nodded. It had never seemed a small thing to him; hearing it asked that way made him realize how often he had presented it as a detail of his own exercise.

Yselle took back the cylinder and set it beside the faulty liner. Alexander returned his attention to the three empty spaces. They had been cleared for the intended machines, with supports prepared and room to move between one body and another. Their order made the absence more visible.

Beside the first support, a small board bore a dark burn mark. Alexander brushed it and looked at his aunt.

“This bench wasn't in the house.”

“I found it when I took the premises. The one you remember was too short.”

Martin indicated the joint beneath the surface. “So was this one. Then she decided she could lengthen it.”

Yselle showed where the addition had been supported to keep the top from flexing. The duke bent to look underneath; Elias had to move the toolbox blocking his view. For a few moments they discussed the old wood with a familiarity that included neither him nor Gaston. The young man felt outside the conversation and, at the same instant, less watched.

When Alexander straightened, dust marked his knee. Yselle pointed it out with the same pencil she had used to correct the drawing. He brushed the fabric and returned to the empty spaces. Elias saw the attention on his face change; the man who remembered the family bench was again seeking the missing part of the work before him.

“How long will it take to obtain the others?” he asked.

His aunt did not answer with a single figure. She showed him the material list, the difficult operations, and those for which they might find another workshop. Some parts could be obtained; others kept the entire machine in the form of a list. Elias watched the engineer's finger stop at the line he had already seen marked red.

“And if only these arrive?” Alexander asked.

Yselle produced the smaller drawing prepared with Martin. She did not present it as the real project they had hidden. She set it beside the other drawing and showed what the service would lose. Martin approached the locking ring, where they had needed to leave room for a wrench.

The duke studied both drawings for a long time. Elias had time to see he was not looking for somewhere to sign. He followed the components that reappeared unchanged and those altered to remain accessible. Then he returned to the still open prototype.

“Which of these items would a complete machine change?”

Yselle raised her head. “A purchase already available?”

“A machine in this configuration. Already whole.”

Marcel rested a hand on the bench's edge. Gaston looked at the prototype as though it might have concealed a second outlet. Elias felt a question form and stop: did the duke intend to bring other pumps from some place they knew nothing about?

Yselle did not look toward the door.

“How whole?”

“Assembled. With the moving parts and surfaces you've defined. And the spare parts, if you show me which.”

Elias lowered his eyes to the caliper still open in his hand. It was the instrument with which he had measured the problem. It offered no help in understanding the scale of that sentence. He closed it before setting it down; the small gesture let him remain where he needed to listen.

“Can you do this?” Yselle asked.

Alexander looked at her. “I want to understand exactly what I'm offering before trying it here.”

His aunt drew the sheet toward herself, far enough to include the prototype and the supports in one view. Elias waited for an instruction to leave. Nobody gave it. Martin remained beside the corrected component; Marcel lifted his finger from the material line he had followed until then.

“Then tell me what you want in exchange,” Yselle said.

Alexander did not look for the chair. “Let's begin with that.”

Elias stayed with his hand on the open toolbox. The work he had brought had reached the drawing. Now, between the same windows, the question had taken a form nobody had taught him to measure.

Chapter 49 — A Form That Works

“Three,” Yselle said. “Not thirty.”

Alexander followed her finger to the bases. His aunt indicated a place for service, one for testing and teaching, one for availability that would not require stopping the first machine. She had already thought about using what she did not possess. She was not asking him to fill the workshop to the ceiling for fear the offer would vanish.

“Three complete pumps,” he repeated. “And the spare parts you show me.”

She held the pencil between her fingers. “In exchange?”

“I'd like to see one working at the Mutual.”

“So would I. That isn't something in return.”

Edran looked at her. It was the face Alexander remembered from conversations in which an adult's answer had failed to convince his aunt. He wanted to smile, but the question was not meant to amuse him.

“I'm not asking for shares. Or a position from which to choose who works with you.”

Yselle lowered the pencil a little. “And when I say I don't like something?”

“You'll tell me what.”

“I already do.”

“Then we'll know how to continue.”

Martin looked first at his wife, then at Alexander. Young Veyne stood motionless beside the open toolbox; Marcel Ruy held a corner of the drawing with the backs of his fingers. Nobody had a bag of coins before them. The difference was finally beginning to enter their expressions.

“I need to be able to examine them,” Ruy said.

“Of course.”

“Even if we find something to correct.”

Alexander nodded. “That's why I'm asking you to stay.”

His aunt put down the pencil. The gesture did not yet mean trust in everything he had left unexplained. It meant they could begin.

They went over the prototype's configuration. Yselle had him follow the water's path from the inlet to the chamber, the piston's stroke, the opening of the valves. Martin moved the linkage with the head open so the relationship between the parts remained visible. When one component passed in front of another, Alexander asked him to repeat the movement and stopped him at the point that interested him.

In his use of matter he had known structures far more complex than this. What was new was the exact design before him: what this woman had chosen to make replaceable, where she had left clearance, which surface had to retain its shape after thousands of strokes. Remembering what a pump was did not tell him which pump Yselle wanted.

Veyne held the drawing as a draft lifted its edge. Alexander asked for an internal measurement; the young man indicated it with a finger still dirty from the prototype and waited for Ruy to confirm the check. Edran liked the rhythm he found in the pair: the master did not take his voice away, the apprentice did not need the last word to make the first useful.

The material list passed beneath the light. Alexander distinguished the metals, worked surfaces, and seal according to the samples they showed him. He asked about the point where the gasket deformed during closure. Yselle took the correct component and bent it between thumb and forefinger, showing how far and in which direction it had to yield.

“This one comes back like this,” she said. “The one on the bench doesn't.”

The second had lost some of its elastic response. Edran felt the difference in his fingers. He did not need to reproduce a history of wear; he needed to give the new machine the useful condition they had described and shown.

For spares they agreed on one corrected liner and one gasket per pump, plus the linkage pins Yselle indicated. She arranged them on the sheet without piling them together. Ruy moved a faulty sample out of the group before Alexander could take it as a reference. That calm gesture, made with the same hand that would check an ordinary order, freed the room a little more from the need to be amazed too soon.

By the end, Alexander could reconstruct the machine in thought without inventing a component he had not seen. The configuration preserved the empty spaces, the future water, the access for tools, and the route of a hand. Had he filled them with metal merely to make the body seem stronger, he would have created a handsome immobility.

Yselle called Gaston and had him move a crate off the first support. Martin gathered a cloth left on the second; Veyne took a long ruler from the third, returned for the pencil he had put there, and smiled to himself at forgetting it. Everyone took places where they could see without standing beneath the new weight.

Teren followed the movement from the door. Alexander indicated the space he would occupy. The head of security looked at the bases and the men, then changed position to leave the exit clear.

“Here?” the duke asked his aunt.

She checked the distance from the bench, although she had prepared it herself. “Here.”

Edran stood before the first base. The high window still trembled from a passing cart. He could hear water remaining in the container and the breathing of those behind him. He did not seek a greater silence.

He willed the configuration into existence.

Cradle answered within his own existence. The machine's body took its place on the base, whole: the distinct metals, the liner in its housing, the piston, linkage, and valves already in the relationship he had understood. The empty spaces stayed empty. Its weight settled on the support with a low sound that crossed the floor to their soles.

Window light broke across the new edge.

For an instant Edran heard only that sound: matter finding somewhere to be. Then Martin released his breath too loudly, and a point fell from Gaston's fingers. Its small bounce against the floor seemed to come from another room.

Yselle stood near the bench. Her hand had remained open above the drawing; she was not looking at Alexander, but at the place where the drawing and the machine had ceased to be separated by what was missing. Her face had lost the expression with which she had asked for the terms. Nothing had replaced it yet.

Edran placed the second pump on the second base. It came into being assembled like the first, distinct in the place it occupied. The linkage did not need adding by a startled hand; the gasket did not wait on the bench for someone to make the promise true. The entire functional relationship was there.

The third machine took weight on its support.

A row of machines could make its presence visible in ways a single one could not. Passages appeared between identical bodies. The windows sent different reflections across the surfaces. Yselle's bench ceased to be the only place toward which the work turned. Even the sound of the room changed, held by forms that had not existed a moment earlier.

Gaston lowered his eyes to the fallen point. He tried to pick it up, struck his boot against the chair, and remained bent for a moment with one hand on his knee. Martin stooped from the other side and put it into his palm. Neither found a sentence to accompany the gesture.

“Is it finished?” Yselle asked.

Alexander looked at the third machine. “The machines, yes. The spares remain.”

She walked a step along the row. Edran watched her seek what she had followed on the sheet. The relationship between the components was hers; the hands that would use them still belonged to her workshop. She reached the third pump's side and looked through the passage left for the tool. Light reached the floor beyond the metal.

“It fits through,” she said softly.

Veyne raised his head, as though called by the precise thing he had measured. Yselle indicated the passage. The young man looked, then opened his hand in the empty space between the two bodies without touching either. Edran followed the movement: they could already find a route there, a measurement, room for their own presence. The first fear in the room seemed to have changed direction. Now they wanted to come close enough to understand.

Alexander created the spares on the cleared surface: three liners, three gaskets, and the specified pins. He placed them separately as his aunt had requested. Nobody had to search a mass of metal for an unfinished project.

He let the exercise end.

The silence that followed was not his. The machines rested on the floor with their own weight; light continued moving across their surfaces, and a drop from Martin's cloth spread near the first base. The world had already included them enough to dirty them.

Veyne took a step, then stopped. The desire on his face was so precise Alexander did not need to guess it.

“You may come closer,” he said.

The young man did not run. He set two fingers against the first pump's body and immediately removed them, as though expecting heat. Then he touched it again with his palm. His smile arrived only after contact, hesitant and broad.

Yselle passed Alexander without noticing that she brushed his sleeve. She looked at the liner access, the catch, and the point where the notch should appear. She bent to find it. Martin joined her from the other side and bent at the same moment. Their foreheads collided.

“You've taken my place too,” he said.

She raised a hand to her temple. She looked at her husband, then burst into a short, incredulous laugh that seemed to let her breathe again.

Edran laughed with her.

At that instant he did not remember palaces, armies, or names the world had lost. He had created enormous things and people whose first voices had remained within him for spans no calendar in this house could contain. The pump did not ask to compete with them. Yselle was laughing against her palm, and he felt a new pleasure in not having to make the moment larger in order to remain in it.

Ruy went to the spare parts table. He took a liner, examined its mouth, and passed it to Gaston. The instrument maker searched for a word, failed to find it, and returned to studying the component. The complete machine behind them did not cease to exist because the two men were interested in a spare.

Yselle straightened. Her eyes were wet, perhaps from the blow to her forehead, perhaps not.

“Where did you get them?”

Alexander shook his head gently. “I brought them into existence here.”

She looked along the bases. “All of them.”

“All of them.”

“And you don't have to stay nearby?”

“No. They're real materials. You can move them, open them, use them. They don't switch off when I leave.”

She took his wrist. It was different from the embrace at the door, a brief grip to keep him within reach of a question that had no words yet. Edran gave her time.

Martin moved the first pump's lever a little. The stroke answered with an ordinary sound. He turned to Yselle as if showing her an object found at sea.

“Can we open the head?”

“We must,” Ruy said.

Yselle released her nephew's wrist. Her profession returned to her hands along with her breath.

“We'll begin with the first. I want the references before adding water.”

Edran stepped away from the base. Veyne returned to the toolbox, took his notebook, and sought an unstained page. Ruy gave him the pencil he had left on the bench. Nobody had ceased to be necessary because the machines were there.

Alexander watched his aunt rest her hand on the new metal. He had seen her make the same gesture on the prototype, but now her palm stayed open longer. Then she looked for the caliper and began calling out the points she wanted checked.

Chapter 50 — The Weight of Metal

Elias's palm had left a print on the first pump.

He noticed it when he returned with his notebook. A duller patch, with three broken lines where his fingers had lifted away. He was dirty from the prototype and had dirtied this machine too. His hand sought the cloth before he decided to do it.

“Leave it,” Yselle said. “For the moment we at least know which one you touched.”

The prototype stayed on its own bench, unnumbered. Gaston found the chalk. She had him mark one on the first machine body, two on the second, and three on the third. The white figures were uneven: the three too broad, the two with a long tail. None of the pumps seemed less impossible for entering the world of bad handwriting.

Elias put the number one at the top of his page. Beneath it he wrote the date, June fifth, and left space for the test conditions. His heart still beat so hard that he angled his hand to avoid mistaking his pulse for a tremor.

Marcel handed him the caliper. It was the one he had cleaned that morning; the metal jaws bore small signs of use that the pump's body did not yet possess. As the two surfaces met, Elias expected a different sound. He heard only the instrument's delicate contact.

The external dimension matched the drawing.

He checked twice without hiding it. Then with Near Coordinates he reached into the volume they had opened, followed the relationship between axis and seat, and indicated the internal stretch to Marcel. The master inserted the instrument where it could enter. Both readings found the same geometry.

“Write where, too,” Marcel said.

Elias did. The figure alone would not say what they had touched. He turned the page far enough into the light and for a moment stopped feeling the duke behind him.

Martin had opened the head. He lifted the component from its seat while Yselle kept the cloth away from the new gasket. The interior was dry, complete, arranged exactly to perform the stroke demonstrated by the prototype. They found no gap left to the imagination of whoever would make it work.

“The catch,” the engineer said.

Martin released it. The notch appeared at the indicated point, and the liner came out without the stop that had interrupted the test on June third. Elias supported its other side. His fingers recognized the weight before his thoughts could make the object ordinary. They rested the cylinder on the strips; its surface made a clean sound, identical to a well delivered component.

Marcel took the gasket between two fingers and let it spring back. Yselle compared its edge with the correct sample. They did more than observe that it had the right shape: they checked how it yielded, how it sat in its housing, and the contact it left when pressed. Gaston brought a little water for the separate seal test.

The duke remained near the bench without reaching to adjust what the others examined. Elias looked at him only once. Alexander was listening to Yselle's question about a surface, with the attention of someone who had delivered something and wanted to know what they would do with it.

Ruy compared the accessible metal with the samples and tools available. He was interested in the surface's response, the worked edges, the seats that would endure movement. Elias could measure the internal space; the metal’s response to those checks was less familiar to him. He followed his master's work, passing what he requested and noting the limits of the checks performed.

At the second pump he changed his grip on the pencil. His thumb remained cramped from the first inspection. Martin had already opened the head and checked the notch; Gaston kept the second machine's sheet separate from the first. Elias began at the axis. No dimension made him stop the others.

At the third he realized he had been expecting a smaller fault he could understand. A misplaced edge, a measurement to correct, something to restore the birth of these machines to the rhythm of work he knew. Instead he found the intended passage and the distances he had delivered. The chalk three remained the only irregular thing in sight.

“Do you need me to do it again?” Gaston asked, following his gaze.

Elias laughed. “The number is readable.”

The man looked at his chalk. “It comes out better on paper.”

“The machine intimidates you.”

Gaston considered the row. “The machine hasn't asked me anything yet.”

The young man had to turn away to avoid laughing over his notebook. Marcel continued checking the seat with a seriousness that did not entirely hide the corners of his mouth.

They replaced the opened parts after inspection, using the supports and the drawing Martin had tested. They had intervened to examine, not to complete the machines. When the first head was closed again, Yselle made sure the container was ready and asked for water.

Elias took his place beside pump one. Two and three remained on their bases, marked, inspected dry, and separate from the pages of the next test. The spares lay in a row on the table, not installed to make up for something missing.

Martin began moving the lever.

The first stream struck the container wall closer to its edge than Elias expected. A splash reached his cuff. He lowered his arm, looked at the water, and laughed before he could say they needed to move it.

“The container,” Yselle said. “Not the pump.”

Gaston centered it better. The flow acquired its full sound and remained steady through the following strokes. Elias watched the head's edge, then the volume reference. He wrote the figure when Marcel called the time. The point made a small ink blot because he had forgotten to ease his grip.

Yselle crouched near the support. Martin slowed, stopped, and resumed at her signal. The machine answered the actual movement of hands. Water had not learned a different way to fall because the metal had come into being before them.

Alexander left the bench to speak with Teren beyond the door. Elias saw him leave his field of vision while checking the second interval. The first pump continued filling the container. Part of his attention followed the duke, expecting something the young man could not have named; the volume reference forced him back to the water.

Nothing changed.

The machine weighed on its base. The lever offered the intended resistance, the container filled, and the edge remained dry. When Alexander returned, the world did not need to begin believing him again.

Yselle looked at him in the doorway and raised a hand slightly. Then she called for a stop. Martin completed the stroke, stopped, and waited with the others for the last water to fall into the container.

Gaston wanted to repeat a gasket check after the cycle. Elias held the sheet still while he marked the point. Nobody was eager to declare perfect something they could finally examine for long enough.

The door opened before they had finished putting things in order. The man who had come about transport looked at the three machines and asked which was to leave. Gaston indicated the first. The man checked the passage, the threshold, and the place where he could bring the cart alongside.

“One at a time,” he said.

“Only one tomorrow,” Yselle replied.

Elias took the site drawing and showed it without setting it on the wet edge. The man was not looking for the pipe's route. He wanted the door's width, the step, and the point where the wheels could stop. Elias turned the sheet and indicated the access he had surveyed with Gaston. The same page offered a different route to a different task.

The transporter asked whether the weight included everything. Martin looked at the machine and gave an incredulous laugh.

“This time, yes.”

Yselle repeated the answer without laughing, then they agreed where to bring the supports and which side to keep accessible. Elias noted the threshold to protect. The pump already had enough weight to need a cart, men, and space; the fact that nobody had brought it in did not grant it a different way out.

The afternoon filled with ordinary workshop sounds: the toolbox closing, a container emptied, wood scraping as Martin moved a support closer. The wonder did not diminish. It found room among those sounds instead of suspending them.

Elias went into the courtyard for a moment to send word that he would be home late. The outside light made him close his eyes. A man passed with a sack on his shoulder and asked him to move; he moved without finding a way to explain that beyond the wall something worth seeing had just begun.

When he returned, the row was still there.

He found himself beside Alexander while Yselle and Marcel compared notes.

“I thought they'd be hot,” he said.

The duke looked at the first pump's body. “When you touched them?”

“Yes.”

“The metal needed to be in this condition.”

Elias stared at his own handprint. “I've seen objects created.”

Alexander waited, leaving him the rest of the sentence.

“Not something like this. Not three.”

“My aunt looked at me as though I’d put a fourth machine on her table,” the duke said.

Elias laughed, remembering her expression. Alexander leaned against the bench, clear of the water patch, and asked what else he would like to check.

The young man had questions that no longer concerned the pump. He chose the one he could use. “How it behaves after longer work. These are the first tests.”

The duke nodded. “I'd like to see that too.”

At the end they covered two and three with clean sheets and left the first accessible for the next day's transport. Yselle put the notes beside the site drawing. The three replacement liners found supports of their own; Gaston marked the list of things to load. The prototype remained in its place, distinct from the new row.

Elias returned home with his cuff still damp.

Lise saw it before asking about the duke. “Did it rain in the workshop?”

“We tested the first pump.”

His sister waited. Adrien, seated near the lamp, looked up as Elias began his account. He tried beginning with the dimensions and realized he was hiding the largest fact behind the part he knew how to describe well.

“He created them. Whole. In front of us.”

Lise lowered her sheet. She did not ask the question he had prepared about how it was possible. She asked how large they were. Elias showed her with his arms; he had to move a chair to keep the gesture from becoming a false description. Then he described the lever, the liners, and the weight on the bases.

“I'd like to see them,” she said.

“I have to ask Yselle.”

“Can you ask?”

“Yes.”

Adrien wanted to know whether they already moved. Elias told him about the water on his cuff. His father's expression passed from wonder to recognition of his wet son without needing to choose between them.

Lise picked up her sheet but did not start reading. “And you touched the first?”

Elias looked at his hand. The lines of his fingers were clean. The mark now remained far from him on the metal.

“Yes,” he said. “Before I knew what number it had.”

Chapter 51 — The Hand on the Drawing

Yselle kept passing the second pump on her way to the same cupboard.

The first two times she had looked for something: a clean cloth, then the folder of estimates. On the third she stopped with her hand already raised toward the door and looked at the sheet covering the machine. The fabric described an angle where there had been empty space. She lifted a corner and touched the metal.

Cold. Solid. Beneath her fingertip, a worked surface caught the light like the other worked surfaces in her shop. It was June fifth, three days remained until the deadline, and the thing that had kept her from sleeping stood beneath an ordinary cloth.

Martin found her there.

“Have you forgotten what you were looking for?”

“The folder.”

He showed it to her. He held it beneath his arm with the smaller drawing. “You put it on the table and asked me not to let you lose it.”

Yselle let the sheet fall. She stepped back to see the whole row. The first machine was ready to load the next day; the second and third had numbers she would recognize even beneath a strip of fabric. A little farther away, the prototype still bore the marks of the work that had led them to the correct configuration.

“How long have we been here since the others left?” she asked.

“Long enough to need a lamp if we want to read.”

She had ignored the changing light. Martin took the lamp from the shelf and carried it to the table. Yselle followed with the folder. From the courtyard came Teren's voice speaking with a guard; Alexander had left the workshop for his lodgings, close enough to return for their agreed evening. The pumps had received no news of his departure.

The engineer opened the estimate. The first line she sought concerned the missing liners. She placed the point over the total but stopped before drawing a stroke.

“What do I cross out?” she said.

Martin sat opposite. “What you no longer have to buy.”

“Yes. But knowing I no longer have to buy it isn't enough.”

Her husband passed her the order book. Yselle's fingers found a page she had opened often enough to wear its edge. The intended material, the machining, a delivery promised but not received. They had to distinguish what remained payable from what would no longer be requested. The created metal did not retroactively cancel work someone else had already performed.

She prepared a separate sheet. She did not touch the old total until she had found the corresponding entry in every document. Then she removed from the new requirements the components already in the workshop, leaving the earlier figures legible in the preserved estimate.

The red line changed position.

For weeks that line had added up things the project knew how to ask for and her time could not obtain. Now the same words described goods present. Yselle lifted the point from the paper and had to lower it again because her hand had ceased to find a useful pressure.

“Are you dizzy?” Martin asked.

“No.”

“Would you like to sit more comfortably?”

“I'm sitting.”

He looked at the chair, of which she occupied barely the edge. Yselle moved back until she felt the backrest. Laughter rose in her throat and stopped before emerging; it had the unpleasant taste of something that might become a sob without asking permission.

Martin put a cup of water beside her.

“I don't know what to do with this evening,” she said.

Her husband did not look at the list. “I can see that.”

“I had prepared what I'd say if we still lacked the parts. I had prepared how to defend the work already done. Now I keep starting there.”

Martin turned the smaller drawing. The wrench mark beside the locking ring remained visible the right way up. “This hasn't vanished. You have another possibility, even if nobody uses it tomorrow.”

Yselle laid her hand on the drawing. She knew every correction. Her nephew had given her the machines, but he had not erased the afternoon when Elias found the narrowing, or her husband's stubbornness in front of the catch. The three pumps had that form because they had reached that form.

“I don't need capital to buy what I possess,” she said.

“And you don't have to say everything else is free.”

She nodded. Transport remained, installation, tests on site, the people who would work, and the checks that would allow them to declare the service complete. The estimate could not remain inflated merely because nobody yet knew how to describe in one line the gift that had changed it.

She began a second column. Martin counted the work already performed with her, then let her find the point where remaining requirements separated from the previous exposure. It was less. Much less at the decisive point, though she did not yet want to pronounce a figure before discussing it with those who had to recognize it.

The security over her shares remained written in the contract. Yselle reread it without trying to negotiate with the sheet that evening. The change lay in the facts she could bring to the table, not a stamp appearing by itself.

“We test it at the Mutual tomorrow,” Martin said.

“Yes.”

“And tonight?”

Yselle took writing paper. “Tonight I tell them the requirements have changed.”

She wrote that they now had three complete machines and the specified spares, that the first had passed workshop tests under the recorded conditions, and that the intended installation would allow checking on site. She requested a review of the financial requirements on the basis of the new statement, keeping expenses already incurred distinct. She did not write that the security had been released. That was why she was preparing the communication, not news she had received.

In the first draft she had left a sentence about the miraculous availability of the machines. She reread it, put down the pen, and sought the test sheet. The addressee's name at the top reminded her of a room where she would have to answer questions less easy than her own relief.

She took Marcel's note and replaced the sentence with a reference to the conditions observed. The machines' presence did not require an adjective capable of making them appear. It required that the recipient know where to find them and who had checked the first one's operation. She added the workshop address and left the test sheet beside the attachment. Then she placed the discarded draft beneath the folder, where she would not send it by mistake.

Martin read the page when she passed it to him. He rested a finger over the phrase describing the machines' availability.

“Do you want to say how?”

The question made her look at the door. “First I want to ask him.”

Alexander's voice came from the passage a little later, preceded by a greeting to whoever had opened. Yselle gathered the two pages, then left them on the table instead of holding them against her chest. Her nephew entered with his coat over one arm. The small dust mark he had not quite managed to remove still marked his knee.

He looked at the lamp, the papers, the covered row. “I left you working.”

“You brought us a great deal of new work,” Martin said.

Alexander stopped. Yselle heard how different the words could sound from her husband's intention and intervened before they became a misunderstanding.

“Good work. But I need to redo an estimate, and I can't do that merely by admiring how lovely it is not to search for more liners.”

Her nephew nodded and put his coat on the same hook. “Do you need me to describe what I've given?”

“That too. But first I want to understand who's answering me.”

Martin remained seated. Alexander did not seem surprised by the question, and that struck her more than an attempt to evade it would have. The boy had grown up with an important name and people who had to choose their words carefully before him; Yselle had known his whims and his courtesies. This man was young enough to retain the same face and different enough not to hide from her that he knew it.

“What do you want to know?”

“Where did what we saw come from?”

Alexander looked at the drawing's edge beneath her hand. “It's Cradle. I can create matter, already in the configuration I understand.”

The word gave Yselle a reference and left much of the distance intact. She had known Bearers, heard descriptions, and seen things a workshop could not obtain with a good lever. But three complete pumps had taken the place of absences in a fraction of an afternoon.

“And do you always understand it?”

“No. I understood your machine because you showed it to me. You showed me the materials, the passages, what needed to remain accessible.”

Martin shifted on his chair. “If the drawing had been wrong?”

“I would have had a faulty design to reproduce. That's why I asked questions.”

Yselle looked at her thumb on the sheet. The metal had arrived whole. The knowledge she had put into the machine had not been unnecessary to him. She breathed out and discovered she had held part of that breath since the first pump appeared.

“How long?” she asked.

Alexander remained silent long enough for her to understand that the answer would not be a simple date. When he spoke, his voice had not become ceremonial.

“I can't tell you everything tonight. I don't want to give you a short explanation I'll have to correct later.”

Yselle felt irritation and fear touch. She could insist. She had kept information from Martin to make her own day easier; she had no wish to receive a comfortable version from her nephew now. But the face before her was not asking her to consider the question closed.

“Then don't call it a complete explanation,” she said.

“It isn't.”

Martin looked at Alexander. “Are you still the one who tried to convince me a longer lever would make the weight disappear?”

Her nephew smiled. “I remember arguing that the problem was the floor.”

Yselle remembered too. The boy had possessed an exasperating certainty and a piece of wood much longer than his patience. The shared memory did not answer everything she lacked; it let her see Alexander's hand relax on the edge of the chair.

“We can test their use before we know how to tell your whole life,” she said. “But if a machine needs something from you, you tell me.”

“These don't. The machines are yours. Stable materials, ordinary mechanisms. You can repair them with components made by anyone capable of making them.”

Yselle drew the communication for the financier toward him. Alexander read it, asked where to specify the offer, and wrote a confirmation of the goods given without any demand for shares or control of the project. She had him leave enough space for an addition about the technical inspections. The duke did not hesitate when Marcel was named as the contact for the checks performed.

The letter left that evening with the revised statement and the attached confirmation. Yselle followed its bearer to the threshold, then returned to the workshop. Martin had put the smaller drawing back in the folder. Alexander was studying the number three through a fold in the sheet, as though the chalk tail were a detail he had not expected.

She extinguished one of the two lamps still burning and beckoned them both outside. Before closing, she looked at the first pump ready for transport.

Tomorrow they would take it where the water was needed. At last it was a project for which she could prepare the morning, instead of preparing only what she would say if it failed.

Chapter 52 — A Price After a Face

The carter had brought his son instead of his brother.

Mael saw it from the landing before the overseer pointed out the difference. The boy held his cap in both hands and watched the load with the concentration that preceded a first question. The crates had to pass from one entrance to another without being overturned. It was not difficult work until someone tried doing it alone to prove he deserved a grown man's pay.

“How old?” Mael asked.

The father turned. He had a thin beard and a neck reddened by the strap. “He can carry.”

“That isn't what I asked.”

The man gave an age. The boy opened his mouth; his father laid a hand on his shoulder, and he closed it again.

It was June sixth. Low morning light entered through the gate, cut by the bars of its leaf. Mael descended two steps and indicated the load.

“That stays on the cart. He waits outside.”

“My brother has a fever. I have to finish the round.”

“Then you shouldn't have promised two men.”

The boy raised his cap as though to put it on and could not find the courage to finish. Mael recognized the movement and looked at the father. A man with someone to protect had a reason to respond better to the second proposal.

“You can use a warehouse porter,” he said. “His time comes off your round.”

The father looked at the overseer, then the cart. He accepted with a nod he tried to turn into thanks. Mael let him. He went upstairs while the boy was sent to wait on the side of the gate where he would not obstruct the work.

Sybille had entered the room without sitting. She had opened the window a little, and the air shifted the edge of the plan on the table. Ilian's bill beneath a paperweight did not move.

“You gave him expensive help,” she said.

Mael closed the door. “I gave him a man.”

“Taken out of his round.”

“The warehouse shouldn't pay because his brother is ill.”

Sybille watched him long enough to make clear she had heard the unspoken part too. The father would finish unloading; the difference between finding help and accepting its price would emerge at delivery. The boy outside would wait without knowing how much of his father's time would be charged against him.

Mael removed his gloves and put them beside the returned sample. The object remained in its fine cloth. He disliked looking at it in the morning: it held the weight of an answer others had formulated calmly. He covered it before sitting.

“Uren took the question.”

“Has he come back?”

“He left a note.”

Sybille approached. The note said the collar comparison could be presented as a separate assignment, and a technical answer had to come from whoever would do the work. It contained no offer from Ruy, appointment, or promise from Veyne. Mael remembered well how little space a refusal needed. This was not one yet.

“He spoke to the copyist,” Sybille said.

“Yes.”

“And brought both drafts.”

It was an observation, but Mael heard the judgment in her emphasis. He had not forbidden the draftsman to keep his papers. Forcing him to destroy them would turn a man returning for a balance into a man who wanted a witness. That difference interested him more than the satisfaction of seeing him obey.

Mael pushed the plan toward her. “Let's look at the entrance.”

The room chosen for the inspection had a wide door to the courtyard and a narrower side entrance. There was a bench, sufficient light, and room to set the collar without asking the technician to bend over a crate. The description need not be false. A place where the work could actually be done was more likely to receive a competent man than one built only to lure him.

Sybille followed the side passage with her nail.

“Does this door open?”

“Yes.”

“For whom?”

Mael took the key from the drawer and left it on the plan.

She did not touch it. “When a surveyor arrives, I don't want two porters between him and the street.”

“Why two?”

“Because three are noticed sooner.”

Mael's smile drew no answer. He had learned to distinguish her warnings from her challenges; sometimes Sybille granted him the courtesy of disguising one as the other. This morning she seemed unwilling.

“We aren't fixing a visit nobody has accepted,” he said. “I want to know how to describe the access.”

“Describe it as it will remain. A door, a street, the collar. If the question asks only for dimensions, the man must be able to give only dimensions.”

Mael rested his thumb where the plan showed the bench. He was interested in a Bearer's gesture at work, what he needed to approach, the kind of reference he chose. The other request had sought the final result too soon. An ordinary measurement could show him the person at the moment he stopped talking about his abilities and began using them.

Ilian did not need to know that.

Mael rose and opened the low cupboard. The collar they had commissioned was wrapped in coarse canvas, unlike the sample's fine cloth. He carried it to the table. Its halves opened around the pin; the inner edge had a dull stretch where the finishing had not been completed.

Sybille rested a finger on the edge and withdrew it. “That scratches.”

“It needs measuring, not stroking.”

“A man notices when he picks it up.”

Mael ran his thumb over the indicated stretch. The roughness was not enough to prevent the gesture, but left a pale mark on his skin. He had asked for an ordinary object, not a neglected one. He took the chalk and marked the point.

Then he closed the collar around the wooden cylinder used to support it. The halves would not meet without leaving a gap. Sybille put a hand beneath the component before it could slide off the edge.

“That's why we're seeking a comparison,” Mael said.

“That's why the man needs to open it without asking you for a key every time.”

He left the catch loose. She withdrew the cylinder and put the collar back on the canvas, open. It seemed smaller like that, without the closed form that made it resemble something ready for an arm.

Mael noticed where her gaze had stopped. He did not ask what she had imagined. Instead he indicated the chalk mark and told her to have the point finished before showing the object to anyone who needed to work with it. Sybille nodded slowly and wrapped the metal, leaving the catch accessible.

“Another surveyor,” he said. “If Ruy doesn't come, we find another surveyor.”

Sybille looked up. “For the collar?”

“For the collar.”

He added nothing. Leaving a question open was less tiring than preventing someone from formulating the answer they wanted. Sybille looked at the covered sample, then the key. Her hand paused at the plan's edge, close enough to take either object, and took neither.

A sharp knock rose from below. Something had struck a threshold, followed by a muffled curse. The overseer called a name. Not the boy's.

Mael opened the assignment ledger. Among deliveries of objects, some lines concerned people: an accompanied journey, two attendances for an evening, renewal of an availability. Each entry had hours, a place, and an amount. The descriptions made faces unnecessary until something happened that required one person to be distinguished from another.

Sybille moved to read the page without bending over him.

“Is this still open?”

She indicated an attendance recorded for three days. Mael knew whom she meant. A woman hired to accompany someone to a station had waited beyond the agreed time for the guarantor's return. It was distant enough not to bring him a new problem that morning; near enough to leave someone available to ask for news.

“The return confirmation is missing.”

“Did she return?”

“That isn't the question recorded.”

Sybille removed her finger from the line. It was a small movement, but Mael saw and kept it. He disliked her trying to rename things before an open book. She had done it in the past, then collected her share anyway. The memory of that choice should make judging his less comfortable.

“Someone else can provide confirmation,” she said.

“You can seek it, if the time is yours.”

She did not immediately answer. That stretch of silence was a cost, and Mael waited to see whether she would pay it. Sybille looked at the line, then Ilian's bill protruding beneath the paperweight.

“The inspection first,” she said.

Mael closed the ledger. He had not obtained complete obedience; the sequence would do.

The carter knocked shortly afterward. He held his cap like his son. A streak of blood on his thumb had been badly wiped against his trousers.

“The load is inside.”

Mael followed him to the stairs. Two crates had been placed beyond the mark on the floor. The overseer showed the point and waited. The father began saying the space had been occupied, saw Mael, and changed his sentence.

“We'll move them.”

“You will,” Mael confirmed.

It was brief, unpleasant work. The load had already found a balance and did not want to lose it; the father called the porter by name instead of ordering him. Mael saw the second man hesitate, then set his shoulder. When they finished, the passage was clear and nobody could maintain that the request had been unreasonable. The dust on the man's coat told another part of the operation.

The boy still waited outside. Seeing his father, he took a step toward him. The man showed his palm to stop him, not strike him. Then he turned to the overseer and asked how much of the round had been charged.

Mael left them to discuss the time. He returned upstairs quietly wanting a warm cup.

Sybille had taken the key.

“I'm going to look at the bench,” she said.

“Take someone capable of fixing it. If the collar is to be measured, it mustn't rock.”

“I know.”

She folded the plan without covering Ilian's note. Before leaving, she looked at it once more.

“He's owed the balance for the drawing he's already done.”

Mael did not rise. “And he'll keep asking for it.”

Sybille stayed in the doorway one breath too long. Then she descended. Her footsteps sounded ordinary and clear on the wood; the warehouse did not need to become silent for her to cross it.

Mael drew the new note toward him and marked the work to offer another surveyor if necessary. External dimensions, alignment, ordinary collar. The price would be discussed after he had seen the face of whoever could do it.

Below the window, the cart moved off. The father had not put his son on the load; the boy walked beside him, his cap finally on his head. The gate stayed open long enough to let them both out, then the overseer brought it against its stop.

Mael took Ilian's bill and put it back on top of the workshop's answer, where it would be impossible to miss at the next visit.

Chapter 53 — Before the Jet

The number one passed through the doorway a finger's width at a time.

Elias saw it appear between the jamb and the transporter's shoulder. The pump advanced on its prepared supports, a man on either side and enough wood beneath the weight to keep the metal from striking the threshold directly. The cart remained in the courtyard. From the porters' breathing, witnessing a miracle, or merely hearing about one, did not seem to make a machine lighter.

“A little toward me,” the man on the left said.

Elias indicated the space behind his heel. “There's the edge.”

The man found it with his sole and changed his footing. Gaston held the door fully open. Noemi had moved two containers and a basket that had occupied the service room access on the survey day; one stubborn corner remained, too narrow for transport and wide enough for those who passed through every day.

The morning of June sixth had begun with that problem, rather than the stream of water Elias had imagined on his way home the previous evening.

The pump crossed the threshold. Martin guided the frame's side toward its support, then removed his fingers and let the men lower the weight. The white number faced the entrance. For the first time the machine had a place other than the one where it had been born.

Noemi looked at the cleared passage. “Does the door close now?”

Gaston moved it slowly. The leaf passed without touching the frame and came to rest against the wall again. The woman nodded and took the basket outside.

Elias followed her with his eyes. People in the courtyard waited to resume small tasks already begun: a strap to adjust, a tool to wash, a parcel that could not stay damp. The Mutual had not closed for the day because a pump had arrived at the heart of its service. It had merely needed to make room for the test.

Marcel called the young man. The base needed to remain legible against the references, and the final position did not coincide with the edge along which the transporter wanted to slide it. Elias reached the nearby distances, compared them with the drawing, and indicated the correct point. The man looked first at him, then at Yselle. Marcel confirmed the reference without changing the young man's words.

They shifted the support. The wood made a low sound. Elias wrote down the position reached while Martin checked he had room for the lever's stroke.

Alexander arrived with Yselle from the other side of the courtyard. Teren stopped at the corridor entrance and examined the room before choosing where to stand. The two guards did not both station themselves in front of the door. Elias saw one keeping clear the stretch of passage they had just used.

Noemi showed the duke the wet patch on the floor.

“That's slippery without a new machine.”

Alexander changed his route. “Thank you.”

“Don't thank me yet. I have a whole place to show you.”

The young duke smiled, but looked toward the connection immediately afterward. The pump remained stopped; its metal showed no change from the previous day's tests. Elias felt a sudden pleasure at recognizing his handprint, almost erased beneath the cloth Martin had used.

The connection required hands and tools. The attendant held the prepared section while Martin brought the connector closer; Marcel checked the fit before they tightened it.
