Elias passed the wrench, then took up the drawing to leave the bench clear. When they removed the temporary support, the weight remained on the base and the points prepared to receive it. Yselle watched the transition and had them check that the drive could still complete its stroke without striking the edge.

The Mutual's attendant explained the sections he knew. Service water entered from the indicated side, the prepared section led to the rise toward the tank, and the old shutoff isolated the existing circuit. Yselle had him show the point. She did not settle for a gesture toward the wall: she wanted to see where the new connection ended and what they had found there began.

Elias traced the same division on the drawing. They had measured accessible references and opened the places needed for connection. The pipe could not all be read from the room: it continued behind surfaces, with a history not fully represented in the drawing.

“This section first,” Yselle said. “Then we see how the rise responds.”

The attendant nodded. He was close enough to the work to ask what they meant by response; Marcel indicated where they would observe return flow and sealing during the test. The man then showed an old stain on the wall. He said it predated his shift without giving it a date he did not know.

Elias marked it in the margin. The stain ended before the visible joint; he followed its pale edge without finding fresh water. It was part of what they could see, beside other things they still had to examine.

Noemi approached the old shutoff. The control lay beyond a pipe joint, beside the three steps leading to the walkway. The handrail separated the rising edge from the working area. It was not handsome: hands and damp had worn the finish, leaving a smoother surface in the most frequently used places.

“The key is still here,” she said.

On the nearby shelf lay the short key she had shown him during the survey. Noemi lifted it, displayed its opening, and replaced it with the grip facing anyone approaching from the pump. No cloth covered it.

“Closing this stops the return from the old section,” the attendant said.

Yselle had him trace the route again with his hand. The key remained on the shelf, a little beyond the first handrail post. Elias saw its position in relation to his shoulder, the edge, and the shutoff's socket. The distances were short, concrete enough not to preserve merely in a sentence.

He rested his hand on the rail. The metal was cold; the post did not move when he transferred a little weight to it. He reached that point with Spatial Bearing and maintained the reference. The relationship to the edge remained available even when he turned his head toward the bench.

Gaston followed the gesture. “The handrail?”

“Yes. If there's a splash, I want to find the passage without groping for it.”

“The one going up or coming down?”

Elias looked at the steps. “Both, if I don't stand in it.”

The other man smiled and moved toward the dry side with the notes. Elias released the handrail, took a step, and touched it again. The reference preserved the relationship to his movement; it had not changed the surface beneath his hand.

They moved unnecessary containers away for the test. Martin kept the collecting vessel accessible without blocking the exit. The attendant remained where he could see the connection; Noemi stood near the old shutoff, where she knew the route to the corridor. Alexander was beside the base with the pipe in view. Yselle made sure she could speak to whoever moved the lever without shouting over her own shoulder.

Elias sought the time reference. The morning did not yet seem destined for a particular memory. A man coughed in the courtyard, an inner door closed, and Noemi nudged aside something small that would trip the first distracted person.

A worker appeared from the courtyard with a hinge in her hand. The leather had been removed from the metal, and a little screw remained in its housing, too dirty to reassemble. She looked at the shifted containers and asked Noemi where she could wash it.

“In the low basin. Leave the other water here for the test.”

“I need a cloth too.”

Noemi indicated one without leaving the shutoff. The worker passed along the clear side, took what she needed, and went out again. Elias saw her sit with the component on her knees. A bandage left her thumbnail exposed; she used the other hand to hold the screw without dropping it between the stones.

“When it reaches the top, we'll use it for this too,” Noemi said, seeing him watch.

He followed the tank's height. Until then he had chiefly thought about the buckets that would stop climbing. Now he saw the work below: one hand occupied holding a component, one washing it, the need for water that arrived without demanding a third person.

Gaston called the first volume reference. Elias returned to the page and wrote it beside the space for time. The worker no longer watched the pump. The screw had begun turning, and she bent over the metal, too close to success to raise her head.

Noemi told her to call if the hinge still had play. Then she turned toward Yselle, ready to follow the test.

“Ready?” Yselle asked.

Answers came from different places. The first stroke was short.

Water entered the prepared section. Elias followed the sound, hollow at first, then fuller as the circuit took what they gave it. The pump's head stayed dry. Martin regulated the movement according to the engineer's signal. Gaston called the first reading, and the young man wrote it down.

The test advanced without demanding the full stroke immediately. The attendant watched the new connection, scanned the old pipe, and confirmed he saw no leaks. Yselle had him wait another interval. Water rose toward the tank, changing the sound in the section reached. Elias looked in the direction he knew from the survey.

Noemi saw him looking up. “It isn't full.”

“I'm waiting for the mark.”

“You'll see it. Carrying buckets, it seems much closer.”

He smiled. The engineer called a pause, and the lever stopped. They listened to the circuit settle. The pump did not move on its own to finish the interrupted work; the return through the old section made a separate sound, which the attendant recognized as he indicated the rise.

They resumed after checking. Elias shifted half a step to avoid blocking Gaston's reading. The handrail stayed on his right, the key ahead and a little farther over on the shelf. Noemi was closer to the valve than anyone else, with room to turn.

The first unusual sound was not loud enough to fill the room.

It resembled a knock inside the pipe, followed by a brief vibration. Elias looked at the pump, found its movement regular, and sought the connection. Yselle had already turned toward the old pipe.

“Stop.”

Martin completed the part of the stroke beneath his hand and left the lever still. The pump stopped. Elias saw the drive motionless and still heard water moving beyond the connection. The tank and the full section had not ceased weighing on the circuit.

“The return,” Yselle said. “Look there.”

The attendant changed position to see better. Noemi turned toward the shutoff. The young man heard a second knock, lower than the first. A bright thread appeared on the existing joint, absent a moment earlier.

“Don't go closer,” Marcel told the man.

Elias raised a hand without knowing whom to show it to. The key was on the shelf. His handrail reference stayed precise as the light on the joint became water.

The old union's edge moved.

Not a tremor in the new body, not a part Martin could stop by gripping the lever: the pipe's mouth began separating from its support. Elias saw pale metal appear in the gap and Noemi shift her weight to reach the socket.

The joint gave way with a sound that had no time to remain distinct from the jet.

Chapter 54 — Inside the Water

Water struck his face before he saw where the joint had gone.

Elias closed his eyes. The sound filled the room, lower near the floor and hard against metal. He tried to inhale and took water with the air. His body folded as his hand sought the rail on his right.

The reference was still there.

Not a picture of the stairs: the relationship to the point he had reached, precise even beneath wet eyelids. One short step. The step's edge remained beneath his sole, the post beyond his hand. Elias opened his fingers and found actual metal.

Noemi slipped in front of him.

He saw her shoulder change height and her hip strike the rail. She had sought the shutoff as the old joint opened; now her foot could not find the position she had counted on. Her left hand caught the bar, but her body kept turning beneath the water's force and the movement of the wet floor.

Elias reached her side. He released the rail and brought his left hand beneath her elbow. Weight passed into his arm, more alive and difficult than the dismantled liner's. Noemi tightened her fingers on the bar and stopped the movement enough not to fall.

“The key!”

He did not know whether he or she had shouted. The shelf lay ahead and a little farther over, the same place they had examined before the test. The distance had not grown. What had changed was the possibility of crossing it: moving his body to reach would remove the support he was giving Noemi.

The jet cut his vision into strips. Through the nearby geometry already reached, he sought the shelf's edge, recognized the key's small volume, and opened his right palm in the clear point beside his hip. Between object and hand was a short, connected space the curtain of water had not closed.

Small Transfer took the key from there.

Iron arrived in his palm with a precise coldness. Elias closed his fingers enough not to drop it. There had been no throw to follow, no object forced to challenge the jet to reach the right side. The hand supporting Noemi had not left her elbow.

“Here,” he said.

She moved her right hand without immediately finding his. Elias touched her wrist with the backs of his fingers, then put the key's grip into her palm. He felt her fingers close on the metal. He released it only when its weight no longer belonged to him.

The water changed its voice.

It had happened while he sought Noemi's hand: a curved surface had taken its place between the break and them, high enough to intercept the jet and bend it toward the floor. Elias saw its edge when the veil before his eyes opened. The new metal had broad supports holding it on the actual floor; its curve left the water a route along the clear side, toward the drain Noemi had had cleared.

Alexander stood near the base. The young duke did not need to cross the room to make the shelter present. His open hand lowered beside him while the already existing matter received the water's blow. The pump behind him remained motionless, its lever where Martin had left it.

Elias heard the jet against the shelter as one hears rain beneath a roof too low: the sound was very close, but the full force no longer struck his face. Spray slipped over the edge and reached his cheek. Most of the water went elsewhere, along a route that did not pass through Noemi's body.

She bent to see the shutoff.

“Hold me.”

The words gave him something to do that noise could not confuse. Elias brought his left foot closer to the step, kept his right wide enough not to cross his legs, and raised Noemi's elbow a little. He did not lift her. He gave her time to find purchase with her own body.

The wet sleeve slipped against Elias's palm. He sought a higher hold beneath the part of the arm Noemi kept bent. He felt fabric pull against the seam and eased his fingers a little, spreading support along the forearm without gripping the wrist that needed to remain free.

“Not toward you,” she said, close enough to be heard. “Hold.”

Elias stopped drawing his arm back. Noemi did not want to leave the position she had reached: she wanted to stay in it. The difference came to him as a change of weight. Her shoulder stopped seeking his, her elbow gave him a clear direction, and her body turned toward the control again.

On the curve's outer side, water made something vibrate that he could not see. The sound reached his jaw. Elias clenched his teeth and watched Noemi's right hand. The key shook no more than anything else: it was an object firmly held, ready for the point where she would bring it.

She replaced her foot where the edge was not covered by moving water. Her left hand stayed on the rail, the key in her right. To reach the control she had to turn her shoulder beyond the old bracket protruding from the pipe. Elias saw it edgewise: dark paint, a screw, and a part of the support no longer where they had found it before the test.

“The socket's underneath,” Noemi said.

He followed her gaze. The shutoff was lower than her hand, beside the pipe where the shelter did not cover it. Putting the key where the jet was and hoping would not suffice. Noemi knew the control; she sought it with a short movement, sliding the opening against the right point.

Behind them, Yselle called for people to leave.

Elias heard the attendant's name and Marcel's voice, nearer the man than the duke. The attendant had stepped toward the connection as the joint failed. Now the master took his arm and guided him along the dry side, where the shelter left room to reach the door. Gaston caught a container rolling into the passage and pushed it beneath the bench, clear of their feet.

Teren appeared in the doorway. He indicated the corridor to someone who had stopped at the threshold to look inside, then took the place left clear by the first person out. He did not try making three men pass together where one needed to turn his body.

Martin was still near the motionless lever. Yselle showed him the clear side, and he reached Marcel without touching the drive again. The duke moved half a step, letting them pass behind the base. Elias caught their movements between strips of water. Nobody stood between him and the shutoff; nobody needed him to release Noemi to show them the door.

The key struck the socket without engaging.

Noemi withdrew it a little. Elias saw her mouth move but could not hear the words. The jet kept striking the curve, and the noise hid even the brief knock of iron against iron. She changed the angle, guided the opening with her thumb, and lowered the key until she found full contact.

“Got it,” she said.

Elias felt the elbow press against his palm. He supported the new direction instead of pulling her toward him. The rail remained the reference his body returned to when his sight tried to make him retreat. Moving his right foot a little kept his knee from twisting beneath the load.

Noemi began turning.

The control offered a brief resistance. She leaned her shoulder's weight into the key, using the narrow space between shelter and pipe. The old bracket caught her sleeve. Fabric pulled, then opened against the broken edge as she completed the movement toward the shutoff.

Elias saw a red thread appear on her right forearm and vanish beneath the water.

Noemi's grip did not open. Her left hand still held the bar; her right had brought the control far enough to change the sound beyond the shelter. It was not finished. Return water continued, but the pressure against the curved metal hesitated in a way the young man felt through the floor too.

“Again,” she said.

Elias did not know whether she meant the valve or herself. He strengthened his support beneath her left elbow, on the side away from the wound. Blood appeared again lower down, drawn toward her wrist. The wound lay where the old iron had reached; the shelter had not shut it away from the water enough to show all of it.

Noemi sought room for another fraction of a turn.

The shelter's edge prevented her from bringing the elbow farther forward but did not cover the control. She turned her wrist instead of her shoulder. Elias followed with his support, keeping his own hip toward the rail. The key remained engaged. A low sound ran through the pipe, different from the knock that had opened it.

Water on the floor found the drain. Elias saw a small object turning near the curve's edge, caught and released by the current. Beneath his sole the surface remained slippery; he eased weight from that foot enough to seek the rough part of the step. He did not fall. The reference had not dried the floor, but it had let him know where he was putting his weight.

His right hand was empty. For an instant his fingers sought the key; then he saw it in Noemi's grip, where it needed to be. The small exercise was over; she was still doing the rest with her own arm and the support he could give.

Alexander looked toward them beyond the shelter's edge. Elias could see him only from the chest up. The duke followed the key's movement, then turned toward the route to the drain. The curve stayed firm on its supports even when his hand moved to show Gaston an object in the passage.

Teren was guiding the last man back from the doorway toward the corridor. Yselle's voice came more clearly for an instant, calling that the pump was stopped. Elias could see its intact body a short distance away; water continued coming from the old section beyond the connection, against the shelter.

For a moment the woman took her own weight on the higher foot. Elias felt the load in his arm ease and used the little space to straighten his elbow. He had not left his place beside her. His other fingers brushed his jacket, empty and open, while return water continued striking the metal.

Beneath the light dirtied by water, he saw the mark the key had followed on the socket. He could not read all of it, but Noemi could: her wrist changed angle in the final stretch, and her shoulder lowered toward the support he maintained. Elias readied his arm for the weight returning.

Noemi brought the control to the end of its travel.

Elias felt her elbow cease pushing and supported the weight returning toward him. The key remained in her right hand. The blow against the curved wall weakened; water stopped seeking its upper edge and began falling closer to the floor.

The valve was closed.

Chapter 55 — The Reachable Section

The first sound Alexander heard after the valve closed was Noemi breathing.

She still had the valve key clenched in her hand. Water fell from the metal shield in an ever thinner sheet; behind it, the open pipe gurgled twice and went quiet. A washer spun across the floor, carried toward the drain. Alexander stopped it with his foot before it disappeared.

“Don't open it again,” Yselle said.

Nobody moved toward the valve. Teren stood at the door, letting the last man through into the corridor. Elias stayed beside Noemi until she had found a steady place to stand, then looked at his own hand as if he expected something to be in it. It was empty. The key had reached the place it needed to reach.

Noemi looked down at her arm.

Blood ran from her forearm to her wrist, mingling with the water. The broken edge of an old mounting bracket had sliced her skin when she turned toward the shutoff. The cut widened beneath the wet fabric as soon as she loosened her grip.

“Now I notice this,” she said, annoyed.

Alexander removed a glove.

“I can close it.”

Noemi looked at his hand, then his face. “The way you did with the metal?”

“A different way.”

She offered him her arm. “Then yes. Before someone scolds me about the floor.”

He supported her wrist. Contact gave him something precise: living tissue, small severed vessels, the recent break that the body was already trying to contain. Heart did not require pain to become solemn. Alexander invested restoration in the injury he had reached, without extending it to anything she had not asked him to touch.

The blood stopped welling up. The edges joined beneath his fingers; the skin regained its integrity so quickly that Noemi's next movement, bringing her sleeve up to dab at it, became unnecessary. She made half the motion and stopped. Water washed away a red streak. Beneath it there was no wound concealed or merely put to sleep. The cut was gone.

Noemi turned her wrist.

“Does it still sting?” Elias asked.

“No.” She pressed a finger where the flesh had been open a moment earlier. “Not anymore.”

Alexander released her arm. He had seen men wait months for relief he could have given in less time than it took to find a bandage. It was an old memory; the Mutual's corridor, smelling of soap and wet brick, kept it from becoming the whole of the present.

Yselle had watched without speaking. Now she offered Noemi a dry cloth someone had brought her.

“For the blood that's already come out,” she said.

Noemi gave a single short laugh and cleaned her wrist. “That doesn't go back in?”

“It doesn't need to,” Alexander said.

“Good. Because I wouldn't want it back.”

The laughter reached Elias too. His came out crooked and ended in a long breath. Only then did Alexander see his fingers shaking: the young man hid them by gripping the edge of his jacket.

“And you?” he asked.

Elias inspected himself from shoulders to feet with almost offended haste. “In one piece. Very wet.”

Teren came just far enough into the room to check it. “The corridor is clear. Nobody was injured.”

“Apart from this,” Noemi said, showing skin that could no longer prove it.

Alexander could have created a pipe to replace the broken one at once. He could also have created an entire line as far as the wall. He did not yet know what lay beyond, or how the section was secured in the masonry. The distinction lay before him, in the space between something within his reach and a city that continued beneath the stones.

“Show me the piece that needs replacing,” he told Yselle.

His aunt turned toward the joint. She did not look like someone returning to a failed endeavor; she had already begun to take the damage apart in her mind. She pushed back her sleeve and called Elias.

The new pump stood motionless on its base, intact. The failure was in the old service line, beyond the connection they had installed. The flange had lost its support when the joint gave way; a bracket, flaking and dark beneath its paint, now hung from a single anchor.

Yselle pointed without touching it. “First we take the weight off this. Then we open up enough to see both ends.”

“I can hold it.”

“Here.” She showed him the spot. “Not on the cracked part.”

Alexander reached the metal from the dry side of the shield. The weight offered him no difficulty. Elias brought a prop from the entrance, and Yselle placed it beneath the section that was still sound. They did not begin loosening bolts until the load rested on it.

The first bolt turned; the second resisted with a long, hoarse squeal. A man from the Mutual returned with two wrenches, asked whether they needed another pair of hands, and stayed to hold a container beneath the remaining water. It filled in silence. A flake of paint floated on the bottom.

Alexander let them work. The urgency was over, but his intervention still had consequences: the curved shield remained in place, resting with its outlet toward the drain, and obstructed direct access to the lower section. He moved to give Elias's shoulder room. The young man thanked him without looking up.

When the joint came free, Yselle laid it on a cloth instead of throwing it into the container.

“We keep this.”

The fracture ran around the thinned edge. Alexander saw a brighter surface where the metal had broken recently and an older band, slowly eaten away. The defect had a history from before their arrival. Now it had a name, a location, and witnesses too.

Elias measured the exposed ends, then passed the caliper to Yselle. She checked again. Not to challenge him: one measurement belonged on the drawing, the other to the shape Alexander would make into matter. They discussed access to the bolts and clearance for removing the replacement in the future. The pipe no longer had the right to be forgiven an unreachable bolt simply because it was old.

“We'll replace this support,” Yselle said. “And the one beside it. If the first has failed, I'm not asking the second to pretend nothing happened.”

Alexander crouched to look beneath the pipe. “The anchor in the wall?”

“We expose it and check it. We don't cover it up.”

The man from the Mutual put down the container. “Should I fetch the mason?”

“For that spot, yes.”

Noemi, now sitting in a chair brought from the corridor, told him where to find the man. Her voice had returned to that of someone with a day's work to put back together. Alexander was pleased by that before he knew why.

It did not take them long to make the local damage clear. Yselle drew the replacement on a dry board, marked its dimensions, and showed him the removed piece together with the sound surfaces. Alexander followed the configuration, the materials already agreed upon, the position of the seals and complete supports. He asked her to clarify a section the drawing left ambiguous. Then they moved the cloth holding the failed joint and cleared a rectangle of floor.

Cradle brought the shape into the world.

The new section appeared complete, its ends already defined, its metal continuous beneath the light from the doorway. Beside it the supports took substance with their respective fastening parts. A bolt rolled down the slope of the floor; Yselle stopped it with the toe of her shoe and picked it up. The known configuration became something they could lift, bring into position, install.

Elias stood with the caliper open in his hand.

“I prefer it when you at least announce you're about to do that,” he said.

“You're right.”

“Not because of the danger. I always feel as though I'm in the wrong moment.”

Yselle ran her fingers along the new fitting's edge. “I'm beginning to feel I'm in the right one.”

She did not thank him at once. Instead she asked them to turn the piece, checked the seat, and had them test access with the bulkiest wrench they had. The wrench fitted and could complete its arc. A smile changed her mouth before she straightened.

Installation took hands and space. The new metal did not bolt itself onto the pipe. Alexander held the replacement up while Elias guided one end and Yselle checked the other for alignment; the mason, who had arrived with dust still on his shoes, examined the now accessible anchorage. They replaced the damaged fastening. The supports were positioned and tightened, the seals checked in their respective seats. Elias gathered the last nuts from the cloth and passed them to Yselle. When she asked for a second brace of the same configuration, Alexander gave it to her.

The floor dried in patches around their feet. Teren sent one of his men to report that the duke would be staying at the Mutual a little longer, then took up a broom himself to clear the passage. Noemi showed him the better drain. Her authority over the room survived a duke's visit very well.

A boy looking in at the door asked whether he could have the bucket he had left inside. Noemi told him to wait a moment, then handed it to him herself, walking behind the new support. The boy stared at the bright pipe, but what he wanted to know was whether he would still have to carry water upstairs the next day. Yselle told him what time the test would begin. He stayed to count the trips that might disappear. Alexander saw the calculation pass across his face; it was worth as much as the newly born metal, and golden eyes were not needed to read it.

“The test tomorrow morning,” Yselle decided when the final tightening had been checked. “With the tank ready and everyone who will need to run it.”

Alexander looked at the closed line. The local damage had been repaired. The water could return; the room, meanwhile, still bore traces of what had happened: the cloth with the old joint, the soaked clothes, a dark stripe on the wall. They did not need to erase those things to make the result real.

“The shield can stay until then,” he said. “We'll remove it after the test.”

“And the metal?” the man from the Mutual asked.

Yselle looked at Alexander. He nodded.

“You keep it here. We'll find a use for it that doesn't involve saving our legs.”

Noemi stood. The cloth she had used was pink in one spot and gray in another. She carefully folded those parts inward. Alexander thought she meant to hand it back, but she kept it.

“What you did to my arm,” she said. “Can someone ask for that for something that didn't happen today?”

He did not look at her prosthesis before answering.

“Yes.”

The word came out simply. The possibilities it contained were not simple. Noemi looked down at the cloth, and for a moment she seemed more frightened than she had when the water struck the metal.

“There are people here who would ask for it,” she said. “And others who would like to think without someone waiting for them in the doorway.”

“We can find the time.”

“I'm not making appointments for everyone right now.”

“I understood.”

Noemi looked at him again. “I might write you some questions. I want to know what I can say without making promises on your behalf.”

Alexander told her where to send the sheet before he left. She asked him to repeat it, not because she had failed to hear, but to fix the address in the real day's business, among deliveries and food to be ordered. Then she called to someone in the corridor who had left a door propped open with a bucket.

Yselle wrapped the failed piece in its cloth and left the fracture exposed. Elias resumed taking measurements for the updated drawing. Alexander put his still damp glove back on.

When they left, Noemi's hand was already busy writing.

Chapter 56 — The People Who Are Missing

Noemi searched for the wound with her thumb while she counted plates.

At seventeen she realized she had made a mistake. She started again, keeping both hands on the table this time. The cut was gone; searching for it would not make it easier to understand. Eighteen plates, two with chipped rims that must not go to anyone who had trouble keeping a spoon steady. Four bowls were still in the other room. The girl helping in the kitchen brought three and said the last had fallen.

“Did it break?”

“No. It was empty.”

“Empty bowls break too.”

The girl went back for it. Noemi lined up the plates and found herself smiling, irritated with herself. There was nothing to laugh about. They had flooded a room, frightened people, and delayed everything; the salt for the soup had ended up on the wrong shelf, and a man had been waiting since before the test to pick up a new strap.

And she kept touching a healed arm.

She took the strap into the waiting room. The man had placed his prosthesis beside his chair, wrapping its end in his handkerchief so the wood would not touch the wet floor. He looked straight at Noemi's shoes.

“Did you find the river under the house?”

“Part of it. We're leaving the rest where it is.”

She gave him the strap. He folded it, tried the hole, checked its edge with his nail. That was what he had come for. Noemi had known for years how he took his time before saying whether something was right: first a grimace, then another inspection, finally a hand into his pocket for the receipt. Today he only reached the second inspection.

“They say the duke healed you.”

The sentence traveled across the room and reached the woman by the window too. Noemi did not need to turn to know that the woman had stopped running a cord between her fingers.

“I had a cut on my arm. Now it's gone.”

“Let me see.”

Noemi raised her sleeve far enough. The man leaned forward, squinted, and then rested against the chair back.

“There should be a scar.”

“I know.”

“How deep was it?”

She showed him with two fingers, without turning her arm into a medical report. He looked at the strap in his hand. By the window, the cord began to move again, quickly.

“And this?” he asked, touching the handkerchief around his prosthesis.

Noemi had dreaded the question through the whole plate count. Hearing it spoken made it less immense and more difficult: it was not about the people of the Mutual, in the plural, but this man who had to cross two streets to get home.

“I asked whether we could discuss old injuries too. He said yes. I'll bring him some questions.”

“If I bring him my leg?”

“Not today. First I'll ask how to arrange a visit.”

The woman by the window let the cord fall into her lap. “And you'll line us all up? With people watching?”

Noemi turned. The woman held one rigid hand against her stomach; with the other she gathered up the cord as soon as she realized she had drawn attention. She had once argued with a visitor who called her brave, and that visitor had had nothing more to say when she asked him for a cup with a wide handle.

“I haven't lined anyone up,” Noemi replied.

“It always starts with a list.”

The man snorted. “If he gives me my leg back, he can put me in the square.”

“Not me.”

Noemi took a chair and turned it so the woman would not have to lift her chin to answer her. She wanted to explain at once that this was not what she intended. Instead she left the chair empty and went to close the door on two people listening in the corridor.

“We don't need the square,” she said when she returned. “And anyone who wants to watch will have to settle for the pump.”

The woman lowered her eyes. “I didn't ask you to have me examined.”

“I know.”

The man clicked his tongue, but this time he did not speak. He placed the strap against his prosthesis and began threading it himself. The task occupied both his hands and gave the two women room to be quiet.

Noemi returned to the table without securing general agreement. She had not needed it to deliver his strap; she would not obtain it for so vast a possibility before she even knew how to discuss it. In the day's ledger she recorded only that the replacement had been collected. On a separate sheet, beneath the date, she wrote: individual visits, location.

The words were meager beside what she wanted to ask. She crossed them out and rewrote the whole sentence. Where can you meet people without having to gather them all here?

Someone knocked on the already open door. It was the cook, holding a ladle that should not have left the kitchen.

“Time to eat. Or shall we find out whether he can work miracles on the soup too?”

“That has been beyond recovery for years.”

“It's good today.”

“Then you've been replaced.”

He passed her the ladle. “Taste it and apologize.”

Noemi tasted it. It really was good, though too thick for some of the men. She found herself giving instructions to add broth, looking for salt, and sending a portion into the room where two workers were finishing the drying. The relief those tasks gave her almost made her angry: she should not have needed soup to know that the afternoon still existed.

She sat with the others once everyone had a bowl. Her usual place near the door was occupied by a woman who had arrived late; she took the one opposite the window. The change forced anyone who wanted to ask her something to wait until she looked their way.

A young man held his spoon with a band adapted to his wrist. Every time he raised it, the edge tilted a little too far, spilling soup onto his napkin. He had learned the movement and still hated it. He said he would return the next day to watch the pump test.

“For the water or the duke?” the cook asked.

“So I don't have to carry buckets anymore.”

“Then you can come even when the duke isn't here.”

“That's what I'm hoping.”

Noemi watched him set the spoon back on the rim. The wish for a whole hand and the wish for less exhausting work could inhabit the same person without either making the other false. She wanted to give him both. The thought did not make her capable of doing it alone.

After the meal, the woman from the waiting room stopped her beside the storeroom. She held her cup by its wide handle.

“I wasn't angry with you.”

“A little.”

“A little.” The woman looked at Noemi's arm. “What was it like?”

Noemi could have said warmth, light, tingling. There had been no light; and the little warmth she remembered would not explain the moment when she had searched for pain and failed to find it.

“He held my wrist. Then I moved my hand, and it didn't hurt anymore.”

“That fast?”

“That fast.”

The woman ran her thumb along the cup handle. “Ask him whether we can talk without starting right away.”

“I'll write that down.”

“Not my name.”

“The question.”

The woman nodded and returned to the main room. Noemi waited to hear her voice, busy arguing with the cook about the window letting in cold air, before taking up the sheet again. She did not write the name. She wrote the whole question, and this time the words did not seem too small.

Her prosthesis was bothering her. Water had reached a spot that usually stayed dry; she would need to check it before going home. The thought that she might perhaps no longer have to do that struck her as she closed the inkwell. She sat down again, slowly.

She had never loved that weight simply because it allowed her to walk. She had been grateful to it, cursed it, learned sounds another person would not have heard. She could know it intimately and want it to end. For years that wish had seemed like a locked room against which it was not worth spending her fingernails. Now someone had said a single word, and the door was no longer in the same place.

She would have to ask for herself too.

The pen hovered. Outside, someone was looking for a clean cloth; Noemi knew where they were, but waited for someone else to find one. She wrote a third question. It was not about an organization, an address, or a time. It concerned her closely enough that she folded the sheet before finishing.

She opened it again. Alexander was not there to watch what she wrote. Nobody was asking her to decide by evening. She completed the sentence, dried the ink, and put down the pen.

When the cook came in, she was checking the list of people who had not come that day. Two had sent word that they would stop by tomorrow. One needed someone to deliver materials for a routine adjustment at home. Another had not answered the last message.

“Shall we go and see her?” the cook asked, indicating that line.

“Tomorrow. Today we'll send someone to ask whether she'd like company.”

He nodded, took the delivery sheet, and left the other one alone, folded beside the inkwell. Noemi was grateful. If all those people were healed, she would still want to know which had failed to turn up for a meal, which had found work, who did not want visitors. But she would not keep anyone bound to a prosthesis so she could continue asking.

She stood and went to the water room. The repaired pipe ran above the shield, still in place. The new metal reflected cold light; the old joint remained wrapped on the bench with its fracture exposed. She would have to have that bench moved before morning to leave more room for the operators.

The young man with the spoon caught up with her carrying two bowls. He had fitted one inside the other and held them against his chest.

“Where do these go?”

“The kitchen. You don't need to bring them to me here.”

“I was looking for you.”

Noemi took the top bowl; the other came free without forcing him to change his grip. She waited for him to continue.

“If the duke doesn't come tomorrow, will you still do the test?”

“The machine works even when he leaves the room.”

“I want to see it then.”

“You've already said that.”

“Not quite.”

She understood that he was not asking about water. He had seen something extraordinary entrusted to a person who could leave, and wanted to know whether there would still be room for him in front of the result. Noemi told him to arrive before the test began and leave her a chair beside the dry wall. He asked whether it was for her or for him. She said they would argue about it when the time came. The young man headed back toward the kitchen with his own bowl and left the other on the bench.

Noemi bent to pick up the pink and gray cloth she had left there. It was already drying. She thought of throwing it in with the laundry, then stopped, irritated by her own need to keep it as evidence.

The evidence was the arm moving without pain.

She put the cloth in the basket. She went back for the sheet of questions and slipped it into an envelope. Before sealing it, she added that she would prefer to meet him when they did not have a flooded room around them.

That request finally made her laugh without fear.

Chapter 57 — Glue on the Cuff

The message had reached home before Elias, but it had said nothing about the noise his shoes made.

On the first flight he tried climbing on the edges of his feet. On the second he gave up. Water trapped beneath the insoles came out at every step with a small sucking sound; anyone hearing him from the landing would have thought him a man too tired to conceal that he had been foolish.

Adrien opened the door before he knocked.

He looked at the jacket, the pants dark above the knee, the shoes. Then he took Elias's bag and set it inside, beyond the table where he had spread a newspaper.

“There.”

Elias stepped onto the newspaper. The ink darkened beneath his heels.

“Did someone tell you?”

“That something had broken and you weren't hurt.”

“It's true.”

“Take off your shoes.”

That was not the sentence Elias had prepared on the way home. He had imagined explaining the valve, the joint, the moment the key had been too far away. Instead, sitting on the chair by the entrance, he pulled a water-swollen lace and managed to tighten the knot.

Adrien crouched and loosened it with a blunt point he carried in his pocket. Elias let him. His father's hands were suited to that sort of merciful humiliation.

Lise appeared with two cloths. She tossed one to her brother; the other went beneath his first shoe.

“We asked you to take us to see a pump,” she said. “Not bring it here.”

Elias laughed, and it pulled at a part of his chest he had kept rigid without knowing.

“The pump is dry.”

“Of course. You've got all the water.”

His foot came free of the shoe with difficulty. The sock was cold and the skin beneath it pale. Nothing hurt. He checked once again, annoyed by the need.

“Go and change,” Adrien said.

Lise handed him the second cloth as he passed. She did not let go immediately.

“Is the bag dry?”

“Inside, yes. I think.”

“Were there papers?”

“The drawings stayed at the workshop. I'd left the notebook outside.”

She nodded. It was a question about the notebook. About that too.

In his room Elias took off his shirt. There was a pale, hardened stain on the cuff, older than the water. Glue: that morning he had fastened a paper tab to the folded drawing and rested his elbow on it. He rubbed the stain with his thumb. It did not come off.

The absurdity of that remnant made him close his eyes. The glue came from before. When he turned to look for the key, he had stopped thinking about the morning, what he had eaten, or the steps it had taken to reach the Mutual. Now the fabric gave it all back at once, down to Ruy's voice scolding him for putting his elbow on a drawing he had just tidied.

He dressed quickly. He did not want anyone knocking to check whether he had sat down on the bed and been unable to get up again.

Dinner waited on the cleared table. Adrien had moved his work to the shelf, and that unsettled Elias more than a sharp question would have. Lise put his plate in front of him and told him to eat while it was hot.

“It isn't soup,” she added.

“I noticed.”

“In that state, you might have got confused.”

Elias broke the bread. He was hungry, with a concrete, almost unpleasant hunger. For a few mouthfuls nobody spoke about the water. Lise described a man at the market who insisted on paying less for some fabric because it was the wrong color; Adrien asked what color he wanted, and she said he wanted that one, but cheaper.

Elias laughed again. This time he managed to finish the mouthful without stopping.

It was his father who asked what had happened. He had not picked up his bread yet. Both hands rested beside his plate.

“An old pipe. Not the pump.”

“That's what the message said.”

Elias put down his knife. He began with the machine's arrival and the connection. He tried to be precise without recounting the entire morning; his father asked a question about the circuit, then another about the location of the shutoff. Elias showed him the positions with the cup and breadbasket. Lise moved her own glass before it became another part of the installation.

“Yselle had stopped the pump. But water was returning from the old section. The joint gave way before we could shut that off too.”

“And where were you?”

He indicated a spot between the cup and the edge of the table.

Adrien looked at that empty space. “That's where the jet hit.”

“Yes. The duke created a shield. Curved, with an opening toward the drain. Noemi had to reach the valve.”

“And you?”

“I helped her stay on her feet.”

Lise had stopped eating. Elias realized he was describing everything in the voice he used to explain a successful assembly. He changed it, and for an instant could not find another.

“The key was on the work surface. Close, but with the water in between. I brought it into my palm. I gave it to her and she shut the valve.”

Adrien nodded slowly. He knew enough of his repertoire not to ask him for a different marvel. “You transferred it from somewhere within your reach.”

“Yes.”

“While you were holding her up.”

“With my other hand.”

His father looked at him. Elias understood too late that the clarification did not help.

Lise lowered her spoon. “Were you frightened?”

Elias thought of the noise behind the metal, the railing he had reached, Noemi's body that had to remain in a precise position. He did not remember telling himself he was afraid.

“I am a little now.”

His sister watched him another moment, then picked up her bread. Adrien pushed his chair back and rubbed a hand over his mouth.

“You could ask Ruy to keep you on measurements in the workshop.”

Elias felt the food stop in his throat. “That's not all I do.”

“I know.”

“Then you know I can't decide to measure only where everything's already easy.”

“I know a failure can leave a girl and a father waiting for a message.”

The sentence fell quietly. Elias looked at Lise; she did not come to his aid, but neither did she nod in support of their father. She held her plate with one hand, thumb on its rim, as if waiting for the point at which she would have to speak for herself.

“I didn't go in there to be a hero,” Elias said.

“That wouldn't have made you any harder to kill.”

He wanted to answer that nobody had died. He looked at the cup in the middle of the table and felt ashamed of the sentence before using it. His father saw it coming too; he did not need to hear it.

Lise picked up the basket and put it back where it belonged.

“I don't want him to stop working,” she said. “I want him not to tell me the worst part as though he's explaining why he's late.”

Elias opened his mouth and closed it again.

“Before holding her up, I'd reached the railing,” he said at last. “I knew where it was. I thought if I lost that reference I wouldn't be able to hold her too. When the key arrived, I realized I hadn't been breathing.”

The silence changed. It did not become easier, but his father drew his chair back toward the table.

“Noemi got cut,” Elias continued. “The duke healed her. He closed the wound in front of us.”

Lise raised her eyes, and this time wonder did not erase fear but settled beside it. “Really?”

Elias nodded. He described the skin, the movement of the hand, the blood left on the cloth when the cut it had come from was gone. He did not know how to name that precision without reaching for the name of a power he did not possess. He said what he had seen.

Adrien listened to the end. “The pipe?”

“Repaired. New section, new supports. The test is tomorrow.”

“And you're going.”

It was not a question. Elias said yes anyway.

His father nodded once. “Send the message first. When you know you'll be late.”

“We sent it as soon as we could.”

“I know. I'm telling you for the next time it isn't a breakdown.”

Elias accepted that part. He did not promise nothing would ever happen again; his father did not ask him to. His food had grown lukewarm, and he finished it anyway.

Later Lise brought his shirt while he opened the bag to check its bottom. The papers left inside were dry. He set out only what needed air, leaving the space for her papers clear.

“This is glue,” Lise said, indicating the cuff.

“I know.”

“If you rub it like that, you'll tear the fabric before you get it off.”

She took his sleeve and began working the edge of the stain with a little water. Elias let her teach him something he would have no reason to remember in a technical report.

“You haven't asked whether I can see the machines yet,” she said.

“Not today.”

“I thought so.”

“I'll ask when the work settles down. I'm not taking you into tomorrow's test.”

Lise looked up. “You can ask when people can visit without already deciding I wouldn't know how to stay out of the way.”

She was right. Elias realized it with an irritation he was glad to recognize before speaking. “I'll ask what visits they can accept.”

“Thank you.”

She returned the shirt with the stain still there, but less hard. Not every result had to be instantaneous to be real. Elias folded the cuff outward to dry.

Adrien had gone back to his work on the shelf. The lamp lit one temple and the glass of his spectacles. Elias went over, waited for him to finish the motion he was making, and asked to see the tool.

His father put it in his hand without asking why. An ordinary repair, a grip that needed to become firm again. Elias found the play and reached for the right implement. His fingers obeyed him. The afternoon's faint tremor had not returned.

He wished he could hold more things together: a point he had reached, a person, a tool. He wished he understood how much of that desire came from the training he lacked and how much from fear that he had not been enough. There was no answer he could buy that evening. There was a piece in his hand and his father waiting to see it aligned.

He seated it on the second attempt. Adrien checked, then passed him the screw.

Once the tool was closed again, his father had him test its grip against a piece of wood. It held, then slipped a little. Elias was already looking for the point to adjust when Adrien stopped his hand.

“Look underneath.”

A wood shaving had caught between the surfaces. Elias removed it, tried again, and this time the grip held. He handed the tool to his father, who turned it over in his hands without adding anything. For years Elias would have taken that silence as judgment on his mistake. That evening he watched him carefully put away the little piece of wood too, because it would serve for another test, and understood that Adrien was simply working with him. He sat closer to the light.

On the other side of the room Lise asked whether anyone had seen her folded sheet. Elias pointed to the place he had left clear. She found it without having to move anything and sat down to work.

The shirt stayed hanging beside the window. The white of the glue was still visible on its cuff.

Chapter 58 — What Stays Lit

Martin had burned a slice of bread and was scraping it over the sink when Alexander came into the kitchen.

“Don't tell my wife.”

“I'm here,” Yselle said from the table.

“Then don't tell the duke.”

Alexander looked at the black rectangle and the knife that was now removing nothing but crumbs. “I think I arrived too late.”

Martin inspected the result, turned it over, and decided to serve the other side. The kitchen window stood open onto the yard; outside, someone was trying to bring in a cat that seemed to have other intentions. The call came three times, growing less polite. Yselle listened to the last threat and shook her head without stopping her work with the plates.

“That one only understands the cupboard closing.”

Martin went to close the cupboard. The cat appeared on the windowsill.

Alexander laughed. He had left his jacket in the guest room; when he bent his arms to wash, the clean cuff had seemed an unjustified novelty after the Mutual's water. Now he sat where his aunt indicated and removed one of Martin's notebooks from the next chair.

“Don't open it,” the teacher said. “Terrible things in there.”

“Bad calculations?”

“Handwriting.”

Yselle took the notebook and set it on an already tall pile. “Is that how you say you've forgotten to mark them?”

“No. It's how I ask for another evening.”

Dinner began before Martin received an answer. A bowl passed from hand to hand; Alexander broke his bread on the unburned side. It was easy to remember how the duke had sat in this house as a boy, trying to remain composed enough not to be teased and casual enough to seem not to care. The memory offered him a precise place. It did not dictate what he should do with it.

“Has Teren eaten?” he asked.

“Before you,” Yselle replied. “He asked to know when you were going out tomorrow morning.”

“For the test.”

“I told him. He didn't ask whether I could guarantee we'd stay dry this time.”

Martin lowered his spoon. “A polite man.”

Alexander had watched the security chief check the passage, count those coming out, pick up a broom. Now imagining him at dinner, free for a little while at least from the need to watch the door, made him want not to summon him for a question that could wait.

Yselle served herself last. She ate two mouthfuls, then reached toward the table by the window, where a folder lay.

Martin pushed the breadbasket into her path.

She looked at him. “I wasn't reaching for that.”

“I know.”

“I need to remember something.”

“Write it on the back of my terrible handwriting and keep eating.”

Yselle obeyed with an ostentatious sigh. She wrote a few words on the sheet her husband passed her, then returned to her plate. Alexander had known families where such a gesture would have opened a quarrel about authority. Here Martin had merely saved dinner from the workshop's return.

“Earlier you asked whether the pipe was repaired,” she told her husband. “Not whether he knew how to repair it.”

“Because the first question had a useful answer.”

“And the second?”

Martin looked at Alexander. “I saw three machines yesterday. Today I watched you come back with a repaired pipe and a story Noemi wouldn't have believed if she'd heard it from me. The second question is getting larger.”

Alexander put down his bread. “Which part would you like to ask?”

The teacher considered. “The part about lunch tomorrow. Will I still have to make it?”

Yselle laughed, then nudged him with her elbow. The question had left Alexander room not to answer everything. He recognized that and felt affection for the man with an ease that did not come only from inherited memories.

“I'm afraid so,” he said.

“Then we're safe. I still have an exclusive skill.”

“Burning bread,” Yselle said.

Martin broke his own slice with great dignity. Alexander found himself watching his hands: ordinary hands, without secret inscriptions to assign to someone who knew how to teach a machine. The marriage before him needed no hidden force to hold together. It was enough for one to remind the other to eat, and for her, after protesting, actually to eat.

Hooves sounded in the yard. The open window carried the voices of two men arguing over a delivery. One insisted he would not return until he received the payment owed. The other told him that in that case he would have to wait outside the office, and he replied that he would wait sitting down.

There were no chains in that quarrel. Nobody claimed the other's body because he had dared refuse.

Edran went still with a piece of bread between his fingers.

He had wished for that world before he knew what shape a free street might take. He had known the order that made a person property and the terror that taught people to ask permission even to breathe. He did not need to invent a face from the past to recognize what was missing in the yard. A threat was missing, one that had once been so normal it needed no words.

The men kept arguing over payment. They did not become a monument to anyone's victory. That was precisely why Alexander felt his throat tighten.

Yselle touched his forearm. “You don't have to eat that part if it's hard.”

He looked down at the bread. “It isn't the bread.”

She waited. Martin let go of a joke he had been about to make.

“I was listening to the men outside,” Alexander said. “One of them can refuse to return.”

Yselle glanced toward the window. “He can. If they want him back, they'll have to pay him.”

“Yes.”

He could not offer her the weight of that word without opening everything he had kept hidden. Neither did he want to reduce it to a convalescent's whim. He drank some water and found a truth small enough to fit in that kitchen.

“I'm glad he can say it.”

Martin nodded. “Even when he says it about a delivery we're waiting for.”

Yselle shot him a look. “Was it ours?”

“I don't think so. But we ought to remain consistent if it was.”

His aunt threw back her head and laughed. The pressure in Alexander's throat eased. Outside, the cart moved; the man who wanted to wait sitting down had probably found a chair. Nobody in the house knew that a remote part of his life had just found a result still at work.

After dinner they carried the plates to the sink together. Martin handled the water and Yselle dried without letting him choose the order. Alexander began gathering the cutlery; his aunt tried to take it from him, then saw his expression and left him the task.

“You made more noise as a child,” she observed.

“With the cutlery?”

“About the injustice of having to carry it.”

He remembered. The adolescent duke had believed he could be both guest and innocent. “You hadn't argued my case well enough.”

“Your mother argued it. She decided against you.”

The unspoken name occupied a place on the edge of the sink. Alexander set the cutlery down gently. Yselle noticed, but did not withdraw the memory. Instead she told him how his mother had insisted on washing one cup and then broken a much prettier one while trying not to stain a sleeve.

Only half that scene was in the young man's memories: he had already gone out, offended, before the noise. Now the rest arrived. Yselle described Martin, just home, asking whether the cup had fallen because of a problem with noble rank. Alexander's mother had thrown the cloth at him.

He laughed and missed someone without having to distinguish how much of that loss had begun then and how much had grown within him.

When the sink was empty, Yselle asked whether he would write to Celine. She had put paper and ink on the little table in the guest room.

“I promised her news.”

“Tell her I'll write again before you leave too. She asked questions that deserve more than an answer in a margin.”

“About the workshop?”

“And what I'd put in a garden if I were forced to design one.”

“A pump.”

“An entrance wide enough to bring it in.”

Alexander carried the laughter back to his room. Before sitting, he checked the calendar: June sixth. He would leave Lethorne on the eighth, after Yselle had time to face the deadline with the new state of things. Twenty-three kilometers were not a distance to steal from the road; they would still take the carriage, Teren, the men, and several hours. He wrote Celine the day of his return.

He did not turn the failure into news without danger. He said an old section had given way during the test and been repaired; nobody had been hurt. He stopped, crossed out the last sentence, and rewrote it: Noemi had suffered a cut, now healed. Celine would recognize all too well a reassurance meant to end questions.

He told her about the cat, the bread Martin had rescued, and a machine that in the morning should send water where it had previously arrived with difficulty. He promised to bring her aunt's letter. He did not promise a workshop visit without discussing it with her and Yselle.

The pen hovered above his signature. Alexander thought of Elias, the way he had held the caliper after the creation, the need not always to be in the wrong moment. Around him were people capable of telling him what a pipe lacked. He wanted to know someone who would ask him for something to learn and offer him something he did not already possess.

Not a devotee waiting for a miracle. Someone in conversation who could surprise him.

Downstairs a chair scraped in the kitchen. Martin asked whether the folder could at least leave the dry half of the table free. Yselle answered that the dry half was precisely why she had put it there. The discussion resumed, low and domestic, accompanied by the rustle of pages.

A little later Martin knocked with a candle and an envelope. The door was open, but he waited for Alexander to tell him to come in anyway.

“For the morning post. Yselle said otherwise you'll use one of her large envelopes and Celine will think she's received a design.”

Alexander showed him the single sheet. “Not this time.”

Martin set down the envelope. A line of ink remained on his index finger that washing dishes had not removed.

“Do you still have notebooks?”

“Enough to hope someone invents new handwriting tomorrow. I've seen this sort already.”

“May I see one of the exercises?”

Martin hesitated, surprised by the question. Then he returned to his own room and came back with a sheet whose name he had covered. Alexander followed the description of a lever and pointed to an answer that seemed to contradict the diagram. The teacher laughed: the answer was right; it was the drawing that had an unclear point. He shifted the candle and showed him the line concealed in the fold.

“Tomorrow I'll have to correct my question before I correct them.”

Alexander returned the sheet. That small defeat of a teacher had been offered without shame and without asking anything in return. It kept him better company than the thought of all the answers he could have supplied alone.

Alexander signed the letter. He left the window ajar and listened a little longer to what stayed lit in the house.

Chapter 59 — The Full Tank

The bucket waited in the same place as the day before.

Elias saw it as he entered the Mutual's yard and had to walk around it, because someone had set it right where the passage between two crates ended. He could have moved it. He kept looking until Gaston asked whether he had found a new defect in the water.

“No. In the place.”

“You can fix that without the caliper.”

Elias picked up the bucket by its handle and carried it to the wall. He had slept enough not to feel tired and too little not to remember his empty hand after passing over the key. His jacket was dry; the shirt beneath it was a different one. Lise had not yet returned the one with the stained cuff.

Yselle came out of the pump room. She had already opened the folder on the high bench and had a pencil behind her ear. When she saw him, she did not ask whether he was afraid.

“Will you take the readings up top, or do you want Ruy to come?”

Elias looked at the service stairs. “I will.”

“Good. First come and look at the connection.”

The old joint remained on its cloth, moved to the side bench to keep it out of the way. The curved shield held its shape; it had not grown thin overnight, and the wall did not show through the metal. Pump number one was where they had stopped it. The replacement pipe section, held by its new supports, had the same color as the evening before.

Elias checked the points he had measured with Yselle. He touched the railing before climbing to the upper reference, without hiding the reason from himself. It was still there. The simple certainty of metal beneath his fingers let him look elsewhere.

Marcel arrived with Noemi and two operators who would need to run the machine. One had a broad hand with an old crushed nail; the other carried a short pencil in his breast pocket and kept looking for it even while it was already between his fingers. Elias knew the movement. It happened to him with the caliper.

“Today the floor doesn't drink before we do,” Noemi said.

“We haven't laid a place for it,” Yselle replied.

There were two chairs beside the dry wall. A young man arrived with a third and asked Noemi where it should go. She indicated the window, then sat in the one he seemed to have reserved for himself. He looked at her indignantly; she gestured toward the other, and he allowed himself to be persuaded.

Alexander entered with Teren once everyone was in position. He did not stand in front of the pump. He greeted them, asked Yselle whether anything was missing, and received directions to a free spot by the wall.

“You'll see better than we do from there.”

“That could become a problem,” the duke said.

Yselle handed him the reading sheet. “Then at least you have something to do.”

Elias climbed. From the walkway he could see the tank, the inlet, and part of the yard. He could not help locating everyone below as well. He found their positions, made a mental note, and returned to the water level. He did not have to hold everyone in his palm to read a height.

The first opening was small. A sound passed through the repaired pipe, lower than the one he remembered. Water took the circuit's route; Elias saw the surface in the tank tremble before he could distinguish the flow at the inlet.

“It's coming,” he said.

Gaston repeated the words toward the room. Not a shout. Information that could reach the person with a hand on the control without forcing him to look up.

Elias read the first reference. The pencil moved on the sheet below him. They waited, then Yselle called for the second opening. The sound changed, more continuous; no vein of water appeared along the pipe. Elias had imagined that, at the first noise, he would see only yesterday's failure. Instead he also saw what was different: the support's hold, the joint's seating, the pump body continuing its work.

There was nothing to hold up with power. The machine worked.

The tank filled fast enough to make him stop counting seconds in his head and use the agreed reference. He reported the new reading. Ruy asked him to repeat it because he had misheard the last part; Elias repeated it without climbing down or feeling contradicted.

At the next instruction they opened a service outlet. The surface dropped for a little while, then began rising at a different rate. A splash in the yard was followed by laughter. The woman who had carried the buckets the first day was holding her hands beneath the outlet.

“It isn't for washing your face yet,” Noemi told her.

“I'm not washing my face.”

“I know you.”

The woman filled a container and moved it. She had not needed to go down to the basin and climb back with the weight. The missing journey left her a moment without a task; she looked around as if someone might accuse her of standing idle.

Then she took a cloth and began another job.

Elias followed her with his eyes as far as the door. She had not stopped working. She had stopped lifting that water. The distinction entered his readings with a clarity he could not write beside the numbers.

The test continued through the conditions Yselle had specified. The operator with the pencil recorded each change on the sheet and asked for confirmation before writing down the reading. They stopped the pump when they needed to check the resting level, observed the return, and resumed with the circuit in the required arrangement. The new joint stayed dry. The wall soaked yesterday did not get wet again.

Alexander followed the reading sheet. At one point he showed Ruy a line he did not understand; the master came over and explained the difference between the two measurements. Elias could not hear the words. He saw the duke lower his head toward Marcel's finger, then look at the tank and nod.

When the main part was over, Yselle called Elias down from the walkway. He was in no hurry. He left the dry spot where he had kept his notebook, descended, and found himself beside the control where Noemi had needed the key the day before.

The operator with the broad hand moved the lever at Yselle's direction. The pump stopped. They waited for the return check, then the man himself prepared to reopen it. His movement was less fluid than the engineer's, but they did not need to take the control away from him. His partner wrote something on his sheet and asked him to do it again more slowly.

“If you do it on your own, I can watch your hands,” he said. “If you talk while you're doing it, I can't see anything.”

The man showed him the movement without comment. Then they changed places.

Alexander returned the sheet to Yselle. He told her he would be at the workshop and that, if she needed him again, Teren would know where to find him. Elias watched him leave the room, wait for a container to pass in the corridor, and depart on foot with his escort.

The water did not slow when the duke passed through the gate.

The young man with the chairs looked at the place he had left, then at the container filling. Noemi said something quietly to him. He smiled, but did not answer. He stayed to watch the flow as though he wanted to use all the time nobody was asking him to spend on the stairs anymore.

The shield could be removed. First they stopped and isolated the circuit in the way they had tested, leaving the room free of residual pressure. Two men from the Mutual brought ordinary equipment for moving the metal. Gaston cleared the passage and Marcel showed them how to distribute the weight.

Elias took hold at a dry point. The shield moved like a heavy object, not an apparition about to dissolve. They carried it onto supports prepared along the storeroom wall; the curved edge left a strip of floor that Noemi wanted kept clear. She would decide later with Yselle what use to make of it.

Without the shield the room seemed wider. For the first time Elias could see the whole repaired arrangement at a glance. He had associated that metal with protection, but its place would not always be between the people and the machine.

They restored the service. The woman returned with two containers, filled one, set it down, and moved to the second. This time she had no moment of confusion. She had already found a way to organize the rest.

Noemi went to check the upper floor with one operator. From the stairs she called Elias to join her: a door, opening, brushed the handle of the container left beneath the outlet. Together they moved the container and marked the more convenient position on the sheet. Nobody confused that obstruction with a defect in the pump.

“See?” Noemi said. “Now I have to teach them where to leave full buckets. Before, at least they took those away at once.”

Elias showed her the bucket he had moved on arrival. “I corrected one this morning.”

“Then you're ahead of the new work.”

Ruy was gathering the reading sheets when they returned. He compared them with Elias and left Yselle the copy for the file. The share collateral was not written on any of those lines, yet the fact that the tank was full changed what she could ask the next day. Elias knew that without claiming to understand the price of the change.

“Good,” the master said at last.

The master's word had not been followed by a correction. Elias waited another instant for one, then closed his notebook.

The operator with the short pencil came to find Elias before he left. He had copied the pump's number onto his sheet and wanted to know where to find the same reference on the drawing. Elias showed him the heading, then the detail distinguishing the machine from the Mutual's circuit.

“Can I keep this sheet here?”

“Yselle is leaving you the copy. The original goes back to the workshop.”

The man looked for a dry surface to put it on, found only the bench, and grimaced. There it would be under containers before noon. He called his partner, and together they chose a spot on the wall near the entrance, where it could be read without blocking the passage. They had an ordinary clip; Gaston found a board to fasten it to.

Elias waited while they decided how high to place it. The first position suited the tall man and was almost useless to his partner, who made him lower his arm without a trace of deference. They hung the board lower down. The paper stirred in the draft from the door, then stayed still beneath the clip.

“Tomorrow I'll remember I put it here,” the man with the pencil said.

“And the day after?” his partner asked.

“You'll be here to tell me.”

Elias added no advice. The sheet's place had become a matter for them.

A woman passed carrying a container in both hands. She came from the high outlet, without having climbed the stairs with the weight. She asked someone to open the door. Elias stood and held it until she had passed.

Behind the wall the pump kept sounding, steady and sufficiently uninteresting to leave room for voices.

Chapter 60 — The Gift and the Work

Yselle crossed out the price of the second pump and realized she had been holding her breath.

The figure had remained in the estimate even after the first revision. Out of habit she had removed material they would not have to buy, then the operations replaced by the creation; she had left a total in the wrong column, as though the machine's presence were not enough to stop her ordering it a second time. She drew a firm line through it and added the figures again.

The new result was much lower.

She was almost afraid to look at it. Not because it made the miracle suspicious, but because it erased a threat that had occupied her nights so precisely it seemed a property of the future. On the bench beyond the papers, pump number two waited motionless. The third stood beside it. Both existed without having asked her for the money budgeted to build them.

Martin placed a ruler on the sheet before the draft could lift it.

“Did you count something twice this time?”

“This time I stopped.”

She showed him the column. Her husband followed it without pretending to recognize every item at first glance; when he did not understand the distinction between work completed and work no longer necessary, he asked her to indicate the two documents.

Yselle found the prototype invoice and placed it beside the order that would not be sent.

“This gets paid. They worked on it. We aren't ordering that anymore.”

Martin moved the ruler beneath the second line. “Then you don't need to leave the allowance for delivery here either.”

He was right. Yselle crossed out that item too and returned to the sum. She did not thank him in the special tone she might have used for a husband who had exceptionally managed to understand her accounts: she passed him the next sheet and trusted his reading.

Alexander joined them as the papers began occupying the side bench too. He had left Teren at the entrance. He stopped in front of pump two and tapped it with his knuckles as he might have knocked on a door.

Yselle looked up. “Does it answer you?”

“Only if you do it for it.”

“Then today it costs less than expected.”

He came to the table and looked at the new statement. Yselle did not immediately push it toward him. First she took the sheet she had prepared for something other than accounts.

“I want to put what you've given us in writing.”

Alexander read it. “Three pumps.”

“With the replacement parts specified here and those for yesterday's intervention. The pipe section, the supports, the shield that stays at the Mutual. There are descriptions that let people identify them without calling you or me.”

He followed the list. He did not try to add a noble value to the gesture. “That's fine.”

Yselle held the edge with two fingers. “Not in exchange for shares.”

“I haven't asked you for any.”

“I know. I'm writing it for people who weren't there too.”

Alexander watched her a moment and nodded. “Then write that there's no repayment to be demanded later.”

The line made her smile. “I already have.”

Martin showed him where. Alexander read it and sat down. The pen was on the other side of the table; Yselle passed it without ceremony. He asked only whether the description of the spare parts included the specimens already physically present. She indicated the inventory with its references to the crates. She was not asking him to sign for an infinite warehouse she could open whenever an estimate became inconvenient.

“The rest of the work stays ours,” she said.

“I see that.”

“Not just to keep you at a distance. After this morning, we know the first service works. We need to be able to maintain it when you go home.”

Alexander put down the sheet. “I want to go home without taking away what you've received.”

Yselle indicated the two motionless pumps. “These won't follow you.”

“Good. The carriage would be crowded.”

Martin drew the document toward him and checked that the references were legible. The joke had eased some of the tension, but had not replaced the words. Alexander signed the deed describing the donation, Yselle the acceptance. Martin witnessed the act and returned the pen; his hand had not become that of an owner of his wife's shares.

Yselle let the ink dry. She felt no urge to snatch the sheet back at once to stop her nephew reconsidering. He remained seated, looking at the statements beside it, and neither of them needed to ask again whether the gift had been accepted.

“The financing changes,” she said.

Alexander looked at the old total and the new one. “By a great deal.”

“By everything we no longer have to buy or build. Not the work already done. And not what we'll need for installation, support, ordinary stock.”

Martin touched an item with the ruler. “This is the part I misread. I thought it disappeared with the component.”

“The component arrives ready. The people who need to work on it don't.”

Yselle took the test report. Elias and Ruy had left concrete readings, with the water reached, the stops and restart. The replacement of the old pipe appeared as a separate intervention, not concealed among the pump's qualities. She placed it beside the donation deed. Now she had two things she could take to those financing the project: assets already present and a demonstrated service.

But the agreement in force still held her shares as collateral. No line crossed out in the estimate had released them by itself.

“Tomorrow I'm not asking for more time to do the same thing,” she said. “I'm asking them to finance what exists now.”

Martin nodded. “And release the collateral.”

Yselle felt how much it still cost her to say it directly. Fear suggested a weaker sentence: review the collateral, discuss it later, do not ask too much. She looked at the machines and discarded that weakness. She had risked the shares for a need that no longer existed on the same scale. She did not have to pretend the capital was unchanged to seem grateful to those who had believed in the prototype.

“Yes. Release of the shares held as collateral. This is the new capital.”

She wrote the proposal without turning to her nephew to borrow the weight of his title. The first paragraph described the changed supplies; the second listed expenses that remained, the third the replacement of conditions linked to the previous capital. She added no promise of the duke's personal support. His name stood beneath a defined gift, not beneath the future of her enterprise.

Alexander read only the passage concerning him. “This makes it sound as though I have to confirm the material's stability every time it's installed.”

Yselle took up the pen. In haste she had written a sentence that left an unnecessary obligation open. “That wasn't what I meant.”
