“Then today's declaration is enough. The machines don't depend on where I am.”

She rewrote it. The matter remained stable; checks for use remained technical. The new sentence was not more cautious than the first; it was truer and offered her a stronger position.

Martin read the page from the beginning. He stopped at the ownership clause and asked her who would receive the Mutual's copy. Yselle marked the destination and added the delivery reference. Pump one was already on site; the second and third remained in the workshop inventory beside the crates of spare parts. She went to check the numbers on both and returned to the table with the pencil between her fingers.

When the messenger arrived, he found three sheets open and an envelope already addressed. Yselle gave him the packet with a request to get it to the representative before closing. She wanted them to discuss the proposal the following morning, not discover that things had changed.

The man checked the address and asked whether he should wait for an answer.

“Confirmation that they've received it. I'll discuss the rest in person.”

The door closed. Yselle stood a moment, her hand still where she had held the envelope. The deadline remained June eighth. The bank had not become a friend, and the contract had not dissolved because of a good test. But the question she would bring to the table was no longer whether the future would pay for what was missing. A decisive part of what had been missing stood behind her, ready and numbered.

Alexander rose. “Do you need anything else from me today?”

Yselle looked at the signed document. “Stay tomorrow until we know the result. I might need you to read a sentence, not pay a difference.”

“Departure is in the afternoon. Teren knows.”

His answer lifted a small weight. She could have pretended she had felt none, but he was her nephew as well as the man who had filled that floor with machines. She squeezed his arm and let him go.

She and Martin remained. Her husband gathered two copies and aligned them, tapping their edges on the table. He looked at her with a question that was not about handwriting.

“It isn't over yet,” Yselle said.

“I didn't ask you that.”

She sat. “I'm afraid they'll still say no.”

Martin did not assure her they would accept. He took the old estimate, placed it beside the new one, and left both totals visible.

“Then they'll have to explain which of these things they're saying no to.”

Yselle looked at the two sheets. The difference had cost her only an afternoon of corrections, after months spent pursuing a supply she could not afford. The rest had been done by a person sitting before her a little earlier. Admitting it did not diminish her pride; it made her want not to waste what that gift had opened.

The messenger returned before they closed the workshop. He had an acknowledgment of receipt and a smaller sheet in another hand. The representative wanted to see the machines remaining at the workshop and the results from the first installation. He asked for the revised statement to distinguish clearly between commitments already made and canceled expenses.

Yselle read it twice. There was no promise of acceptance. There was someone prepared to discuss the new capital rather than pretend the papers had never arrived.

“The distinction's already here,” Martin said, looking for the corrected sheet.

“I'll make him a more legible copy.”

Her husband took the pen. “I'll do that. You answer the question about the machines.”

Yselle indicated the clear bench where the representative could put his papers without turning the visit into a procession among workers. She needed to ask Elias to be there with the dimensions, and Ruy with the material checks already completed. The master could answer questions about the checks that bore his name. She wrote the two messages and left them ready for ordinary delivery.

Then she replied that pumps two and three were available at the workshop and that she would bring the test report together with the signed donation. Martin finished the copy and handed it to her. In the revised account she recognized his hand before even rereading the figures.

Outside, a cart stopped with a sharp bump. She turned abruptly, then recognized the sound of wheels against the step. Martin recognized it at the same instant and did not tease her.

Yselle returned to the paper. She removed one last duplicate from the final estimate, copied the total, and closed the folder for tomorrow.

Chapter 61 — The Shadow Side

They had lit the lantern above the gate that was supposed to stay dark.

Sybille saw it from across the street, standing beside a woman searching for her key at the bottom of her shopping. The cone of light reached the grate, cut through the passage beneath the cart, and ended against the wall. The day before, at this hour, the gate had offered a continuous stretch through to the yard. Tonight someone had decided to see the lock better.

The woman found the key. Sybille moved to give her room, then crossed without looking at the lantern again. She carried a light parcel, large enough to suggest a delivery and small enough not to hinder her movement. Inside were nothing but folded canvas and a piece of wood.

The man at the gate asked whom she wanted.

“The yard behind. I was told to come through here.”

“During the day.”

“Then they only told me half of it.”

He indicated the corner. The ordinary route added an alley and a gate; he had no interest in denying her that. Sybille thanked him and continued. She did not put out the lantern or stretch night over the whole street. The gatekeeper watched her as far as the first corner, then went back inside.

In the alley the cart's shadow joined the shadow of a low awning. A few meters of real darkness, from the side of a wheel to the wall beyond the axle. Sybille stopped where her body could reach it and checked the exit. An overturned basin, two crates, no feet beyond them. Footsteps in the yard came from farther away.

She invested the passage through the contiguous shadow.

The edge of the cart slid past her without forcing her to crawl beneath its bed. Her body changed position along that reached stretch; the parcel stayed tight against her hip, her sleeve touched the awning's wood. When she stopped, she was on the other side of the wheels, less than four meters from where she had begun. She left no other Sybille behind to bear the risk for her.

The lantern light interrupted the continuation. To reach the gate she would have to emerge, cross the visible section, and begin again from a different shadow. From here she could not even see inside the next passage.

A door opened. Sybille dimmed the contrast of her coat against the wall and stayed still while a man brought out a container. Her steps made no sound; the container did when he set it down. The man bent, letting his shoulders protrude into the band of light, and spat into the dirty water.

Sybille recognized the cloth knotted at his belt. He was one of the porters Mael had used at the warehouse, not someone she had been promised as an accomplice. She should have asked what he was doing there. Instead she waited for him to go inside and noted the detail. Being recognized would turn a route test into a conversation with too many destinations.

She returned along the stretch she had already reached, then left the alley on the side she had entered. The street's air brought the crowd's noise back around her. A man struck her elbow and swore as though she had sought his arm. Sybille answered in kind and let him go. Sometimes the simplest cover was an unpleasant temper.

The route through the gate took several minutes. In the yard she found a guard seated beside a lamp. The man had one foot on a stool; the other kept time to a song he was not singing. The light divided the passage in two and let him see anyone coming from either side.

Sybille could have tried breaking a call to him, if someone had called. That would not remove his eyes. She stopped before the visible point and chose another route.

The alley's second exit was no mystery. It had a squeaking hinge and led to the longer way around, where carts turned. Sybille passed with the parcel in her arms, endured a stretch of exposed paving, and returned to the warehouse street. The guard had no need to realize there might have been someone to stop.

When she reached Mael, she could still feel the fold of the wood hidden in the canvas against her palm. She set it on the desk and checked both doors before sitting. One led to the stairs, the other to the storeroom corridor. The window was not a convenient third exit: a narrow awning lay below, and tonight just enough rain was falling to make it slippery.

Mael looked at the parcel.

“You brought it back.”

“I wasn't supposed to deliver it.”

“The route?”

“The first stretch works. They've lit a lantern at the main gate. The connection ends before the inner gate.”

He took the little sketch he had given her and looked for the point. Sybille moved his pencil to the right corner. She had not traversed his entire trading network; she had checked a street and an alternative, which was the work she expected to be paid for.

“Can it be put out?” Mael asked.

“You can also ask the gatekeeper to let a cart through. Choose which thing you want people to remember.”

Mael looked up. A brief smile crossed his mouth. “I prefer the cart.”

“And there's a guard beyond. I went around him on foot. I won't promise to make a delivery vanish while he watches.”

“I was asking you for a route.”

“I'm giving you the one I found.”

Sybille left the pencil on the sketch. Mael did not insist that her power grow larger than the alley. He asked about the gate and the time the longer route took. She answered up to the point she had actually traveled; beyond it she left the paper blank.

He took out a second sheet. It was not another route. It contained a request worded to seem compatible with many things: delivery, availability, commissioning. Sybille read it standing because he would not bring it close enough.

“The device isn't enough for them,” she said.

Mael held a finger on the edge. “They want to know who maintains it.”

“The draftsman?”

“Or someone who knows how to make it work. That will depend on what they're shown.”

Sybille reread the word availability. She had concealed departures where someone's availability had been decided by somebody else. She knew the smell of closed carts and the too-quiet voices behind canvas. She did not need Mael to explain how far a well-worded delivery could go.

“Ilian still hasn't received his balance,” she said.

“We're clarifying the request.”

“Exactly. If you want an answer instead of a refusal, you'll have to clarify that too.”

Mael withdrew the sheet. He did not ask her to bring Ilian by force. He did not need to for her to understand the distinction in his head between a technician willing to travel and a service that would have to reach its destination regardless.

Sybille looked at her own copy of the assignment in her coat pocket. She did not take it out. First she mentioned the man she had recognized in the yard.

“One of your porters unloads there.”

“Which one?”

She described the cloth and the hand he had used to hold the container. Mael recognized the man, or pretended to; he gave her no new name to seek out. He said the yard received goods from many people.

“Then tell him not to greet me if he sees me.”

“Are you afraid of being recognized?”

“You pay me not to be.”

He liked the answer. She regretted making it so easy. She had done the work, found the obstacles, and brought a result; she did not want every piece of information to become proof of their resemblance.

“Let me see my part again,” she said.

Mael took the assignment file, the one he had shown her, and placed it before her. Sybille took out her own copy. She aligned the sheets, followed the lines with a nail, and checked the point they had discussed on the evening of June second. Leaving the assignment remained tied to the written terms; checking the route did not become agreement to any future destination.

“It's identical,” he said.

“I'm reading it.”

A cart struck the wall in the corridor. Sybille stopped her finger on the line and waited for the noise to pass. She did not want to be distracted precisely when the sheet seemed reassuring. Her name was where she had left it. The words around it were those agreed. Of other papers she could see only closed spines in Mael's case.

She returned the document. She knew an identical copy would not force a man to be honest in the rest of his life. But she could show it to someone if she needed to claim her pay or prove what work she had accepted. For the moment that was enough to keep her seated.

Mael asked whether she had found a better house.

“I didn't tell you I'd found one.”

“You look at it when you think you're looking at nothing.”

Sybille put away the copy slowly. “Then look less at me and more at what I've brought you.”

Mael took the route assignment and added the completed check. Sybille waited for him to finish, then read the note before allowing him to put it with the others. The longer detour was missing. She made him add it. No shadow would shorten a distance he wanted to forget on paper.

“This brings us closer,” Mael said.

“To what?”

“The delivery that pays for your house.”

She leaned against the chair back. “I pay for the house. You pay me.”

He opened his hand as though granting her a verbal nicety. Sybille let the gesture pass. In the house she had visited there was a small room beside the kitchen; the owner had called it a limitation, she had seen a table beneath the window. She could use a key and leave it in the lock without anyone having the right to come in and ask for work.

That wish did not erase what she was helping Mael prepare. She could see a technician between the words of the request and still straighten the road along which they would bring him. The thought troubled her enough to make her close the pocket on the house prospectus, not enough to make her get up and leave without agreeing on what came next.

“You handle the gate with an ordinary delivery,” she said. “If you want me to look at the yard side again, find me a reason to go in during the day.”

Mael wrote that down too. Sybille checked that the sentence concerned access, not transporting a person, then rose.

On the stairs she met a messenger coming up with a letter. She let him pass without trying to read it. She was tired of looking for her name on sheets she could not open, and she did not yet have proof she could act upon.

Outside, rain had wet the street, erasing the sharp edges of shadows beneath the wheels. Sybille reached the lit street and set off toward her lodging. At the first doorway a woman asked her to hold the door while she brought in her shopping.

An onion slipped from the net and rolled against her shoe. Sybille picked it up with her free hand and set it on the inner step, beyond the edge where rain was falling.

She kept holding the door open. She watched the key turn from inside and left before the woman turned to thank her.

Chapter 62 — Footnote's Question

Elias began with the full tank. Livia let him talk until he said the joint had stayed dry.

“Stayed?”

The word changed his face. He had placed his jacket on the chair back with the air of someone bringing good news who deserved at least time to tell it; now he looked at the sleeve as though it held the point where he should begin again.

“The old section broke open yesterday.”

Livia laid her pen across the inkwell. It was the only place she could put it without staining the papers of the work she had not yet finished. “How open?”

Elias told her about the water. Not all at once: first the fitting, then the shield created by the duke, finally Noemi and the key. Livia waited for the moment when he could say nothing had happened. He did not. He showed her with a hand how small a space he had kept clear.

“And you tell me after the successful test.”

“I wanted to tell you today had gone well.”

“I understood that.”

Livia pushed a folder toward the dry side of the table. One sheet slid over the edge and fell; Elias bent to pick it up, she did the same, and their fingers collided. It was not a pleasant or significant touch. They were simply getting in each other's way.

“I got home late yesterday,” he said.

“I don't expect a messenger every time you get your shoes wet.”

“It was more than that.”

“Exactly.”

She took back the sheet. A word was missing from the heading that she had already added on one copy and forgotten on the other. The evening before she had spent almost an hour determining which was correct; only a few hours earlier he had looked for a valve key inside a jet of water. The comparison came against her will and made her angrier still. Her work did not become ridiculous because Elias's could hurt him.

“I thought you'd worry,” he said.

“Yes. That's what happens when someone I know risks getting hurt. It isn't a disease to prevent by withholding the news.”

Elias leaned against the edge of the chair. Livia wished he would defend himself enough to let her stop being right. He looked at the papers and asked whether she was finishing something urgent.

“Yes.”

“I can wait.”

The answer removed a second easy accusation. Livia took up her pen, added the missing word, and reread the sentence. When she had put the copy right, she found Elias in the same position, the fallen sheet aligned with the others and his hands empty.

“Was Noemi hurt?” she asked.

“A cut. Alexander healed her.”

Livia looked at him. Elias began explaining the gesture before she could ask: hand on wrist, blood stopping, whole skin. He had not used Cradle like glue on an arm. Elias had not known how to name the exercise and did not pretend he had recognized it.

“So there's that too,” she said.

“Yes.”

The urge to ask more questions swept over her with unpleasant speed. She had just scolded a friend for wanting only the reassuring result, and now she wanted to extract the largest secret from his account. She returned to the detail she could actually know.

“The cut is healed. He stayed at the Mutual. And you went home.”

Elias nodded. “My father would like me never to leave the workshop again.”

“Not me.”

She said it too quickly. It was true, but not simple. She wanted him to keep finding things to measure in the world; she also wanted their friendship not to become the place where he brought the already dried version of accidents. She would have to learn to ask for the second without sounding like the voice of the first.

Edith arrived as Livia closed the folder. She had held a folded sheet between her fingers, wearing the ink off the crease. Seeing their faces, she asked whether she should wait outside.

“No,” Livia said.

“Did you lose?” Edith asked Elias.

“It wasn't a contest.”

“Then you lost badly.”

Livia laughed before she could stop herself. Edith removed her coat, sat, and listened to the part of the story she had not heard. She did not take his wrist to check for an injury he did not have. She merely asked whether he had eaten; when he said yes, she said at least this time she would not be talking to a hungry man.

They entered Room Six through the ordinary door. Livia lit the lamp they would use and left the others dark, because the long table did not need light at either end to welcome three people. The empty chairs stayed where they were. Marcel did not enter with them; helping the project had not made him a fourth member.

Livia put the folder outside the space they had set aside for the exchange. She opened a new page in the notebook and wrote the date. Footnote appeared on the previous page beside a hasty correction; Northbound was beneath it, neater. Edith took the chair that let her see the door.

“Now tell the good part,” Livia said.

Elias did. The water rose, people learned the controls, the shield had been put away. When he mentioned the woman who had stopped carrying buckets, Edith asked where the outlet was. He drew a short route. The question brought out a detail Livia had not heard the first time: they had not changed only the machine, they had changed part of someone's day.

“And after the duke left?” Edith asked.

“It kept going.”

Elias smiled. Repeating it did him good. Livia saw that and let his satisfaction arrive whole before making notes.

“I'd like to talk to him,” she said.

The pen stopped above the page. She had not prepared the sentence; she realized that from the way the other two looked at her.

“About the cut?” Elias asked.

“That too. But not to have it told like a fairground act.”

Edith rested an elbow on the table. “What would you ask him?”

Livia looked at the notebook. She could have listed wishes large enough to fill the room: records that would not be lost, copies that did not depend on a tired hand, people who did not reach the office too late because a word had ended up in the wrong heading. Not all those wishes belonged to a power whose effects they had only seen.

“How he thinks about what he creates. Whether he needs someone to check the forms, whether he has to know them. Elias showed him a pump. I know how to make a document precise enough for people who have never met to work together.”

“Do you want to offer him work?” Edith asked.

“I don't want to hand him a request for protection.”

“I've heard what you don't want. What do you give him?”

Livia tightened her grip on the pen. The question annoyed her because it was exactly what she would ask someone arriving with a great name and a vague project.

“A discussion,” she replied. “One where someone doesn't immediately say he's right because he can fill a workshop.”

“He can already get that from his aunt,” Elias said.

Livia looked at him, then laughed again. The correction deserved to remain. She wrote one word on the sheet, exchange, and left the next line blank.

Edith unfolded the sheet she had brought. It was an ordinary reminder about a delivery of materials and a time to change. She had rewritten it twice because the person meant to carry it out kept confusing recipient and address.

“A pump doesn't solve this,” she said. “Neither does a closed wound. I know how to notice what's missing after someone has said everything's ready. It might be useful. But I don't want to be invited to thank a man alongside you two.”

Livia took the sheet and returned it after following the wrong line. “You're missing an arrow.”

“I'm missing you reading it without already knowing the answer.”

It was the second time in a few minutes that her skill had stopped short of her confidence. Livia covered the marginal note with her palm and began again. Without the clue, the sentence sent the delivery to the wrong place. An arrow was not enough. She rewrote the sentence, then passed it to Elias. He said aloud where he would go. This time it was the right place.

Edith nodded and folded the sheet. “I can offer this. A test by the person who has to understand.”

The proposed meeting remained on the table without becoming a letter. Elias said Alexander was still in Lethorne, but the following day he had his aunt's financial matter and then the return to Rovenne. Livia did not want to squeeze into the deadline as though their curiosity should take precedence over Yselle's work.

“We'll think more about it,” she said. “First about what we have to exchange. Then about how to ask him to meet us.”

“Outside here,” Edith said.

Livia nodded. They had decided no place, date, or way to present themselves. Nobody looked for an envelope. The page with the word exchange stayed in the notebook, between their names and a space still white.

Edith was about to close her folder when a printed page slipped out. Livia recognized it by the stain on the edge: an installment of the novel they had left unfinished. Elias saw it and asked whether at least the character they were waiting for had decided to come back.

“He came back,” Edith said. “With a terrible explanation.”

“I'll believe him before I believe you,” Livia replied.

She took the page and read the line indicated. The man had been missing a week and expected to explain everything with a lost suitcase. That could not be enough: the previous chapter had left the suitcase in his hand. Livia looked for the passage on the old sheet she had kept in her notebook. It was there. She read it aloud, ready to declare the error.

Edith took the sheet before she reached the end.

“Keep reading the new one.”

The next line revealed that the suitcase was not his. Livia stayed a moment with her finger halfway down the page; Elias laughed enough to need to move his chair. She tried to maintain an offended expression but failed.

“My correction will arrive after dinner,” she said.

Edith took back the page and folded it at the right place. They had not solved anyone's work or found anything precise enough to offer a duke. For a few minutes they had been able to want the rest of a story without needing to be the most competent people in the room. Livia realized it was also for that pleasure that she wanted to return.

When they left, Elias took her folder without trying to conceal how much it weighed. Livia let him carry it as far as the door, then asked for it back.

“Tomorrow tell me even if it goes badly,” she said.

“Yes.”

He added no justification. They parted on the landing; she went back to put away the finished copies and found the pen still resting across the inkwell. The ink on its tip had dried. She cleaned it, put the table in order, and left open the page of work she would resume in the morning.

She marked the exact point in the margin so she would not have to search for it among the papers still waiting to be sorted.

Chapter 63 — A Name on the Envelope

Aurelie's name occupied less space than all the words Alexander had abandoned writing to her.

He had put it on the first sheet to avoid beginning with a title. Then he added a sentence about his improving health and crossed it out: it sounded like an administrator's notice about the condition of a building. The second sentence, more affectionate, said something he had not yet decided he could say.

The name remained.

In Yselle's house the rooms were dividing between tomorrow's work and work left until the day after. From downstairs came the sound of a folder closing; a chair was dragged near the window to keep the papers from damp. The letter to Celine had gone out that morning. Alexander had kept the simpler promise and now faced the one nobody had asked him to renew in writing.

Aurelie Belcour. Twenty years old. He had known her since childhood.

The duke's memories did not arrange her like a portrait in the frame of a contract. They contained the voice she lowered for a joke, the way she could point out his mistake without offering it to the whole table, a shared suspicion of adults who thought they could make them comfortable by suddenly leaving them alone. They also held boredom, waiting, words said too late. No declaration of love hid behind a detail to turn it into a story's inevitable conclusion.

During the previous year they had personally confirmed the engagement. Alexander remembered his own assent and hers. They were not simply two signatures added by others. The memory was clear enough to prevent Edran telling himself a convenient tale of imprisonment he could break.

Yet that clarity did not decide the present for him.

He rose and went to the window. Rain had left a dark line along the sill. Below, a man carried in a crate, complaining that tomorrow someone would expect not to find it right there. Another voice told him to put it where it did not block the door. The quarrel belonged to the world Alexander had learned to recognize in a few days: people who could oppose one another without becoming enemies of an eternal order.

He wanted a sentence equally concrete for his own situation.

He could imagine meeting Aurelie. He had enough memories to predict a tone, not an answer. He did not know what she had thought of the coma, the recovery, the weeks when part of her life had remained suspended before a distant bed. He could not deduce it from the fact that the duke had once known her wit.

There was no letter from her on the table to answer. Alexander stopped seeking the comfort of a question already formulated.

Yselle knocked on the open door carrying a small bundle. “These are dry.”

They were things he had left after the failure. He took them and set them on the chair. His aunt saw the sheet before Alexander thought to cover it. She did not touch it.

“Are you writing to her?”

“Trying.”

“She's one of the few people who can recognize when you're trying to sound more serious than you are.”

Alexander looked at the crossed-out line about health. “Then I'm starting at a disadvantage.”

Yselle laughed softly. She had a pencil tucked into her jacket lapel and noticed it as she sat: she removed it before breaking it. The sound of wood against the table made the name's presence less solemn.

“Last year you knew how to argue,” she said. “I didn't always find you in agreement.”

“I remember.”

“I wasn't asking whether you did.”

He lowered his eyes. Since waking, so many people had asked whether he remembered something that he had learned too quickly to hear the question even where it was absent. Yselle had simply spoken of the past as she might have before the coma.

“I don't know where to begin,” he admitted.

His aunt looked at the sheet without reading it. “With what you want her to know.”

Alexander thought about the difference between the body Aurelie would recognize and what she would find in conversation with him. He did not want to give Yselle the secret so he could ask her for an answer. “I want to meet her.”

“Then ask her.”

“What if the way I speak is different?”

Yselle studied him. Not as though she had discovered another man in the room; with the more ordinary and difficult concern of a relative who had watched someone she loved change.

“She might tell you,” she replied. “You might hear her say it.”

He realized he was still waiting for reassurance that would spare him the meeting. His aunt did not give it. Instead she asked whether he wanted to send the letter with an ordinary delivery: someone would take one last packet into central Lethorne that evening. They could entrust him with this too.

Alexander said yes. Yselle rose to leave him to write, then turned back at the door.

“Don't promise you'll always be as you were just because you think it'll make answering easier for her.”

“I haven't written that.”

“Good.”

Once she had gone, Alexander sat. He took a new sheet but left the crossed-out one beside it. He had no need to pretend he had found the first sentence at the first attempt.

He wrote that he was well enough to travel, that he was staying with Yselle in Lethorne, and that the next day he would return to Rovenne. Visits could not be timed merely to prove he had nothing left to fear; he would not rush to see her tonight to remove a difficulty from the table along with the ink.

Then he said he wished to meet her and speak directly, when it was possible for both of them. He did not ask to see her before his departure. He offered to agree on a later day without choosing it for her.

The letter could have ended there, correct and too distant.

Alexander took up the old sheet and looked at the name. A minor memory returned: as youngsters, during a visit, he had mispronounced the name of a place, and Aurelie had supplied the right form within a sentence, allowing him to repeat it without being corrected before everyone. Later she had teased him in private. The duke had protested against the second part and retained his gratitude for the first.

They had found themselves beside a window that could not be opened because, according to the adults, it would send a draft across the whole table. The young Alexander had insisted he had used an accepted pronunciation. Aurelie had asked accepted by whom.

“By someone who's been there.”

“You haven't been there.”

“Exactly. He has.”

She had pretended to consider this with attention so serious it made him suspicious. Then she asked whether that someone had also taught him to order water, since at the table he had twice received wine he did not want.

Alexander had turned toward the glass left full. He found nothing to answer, and Aurelie laughed without hiding behind her hand. She had a way of laughing that immediately ruined the possibility of pretending to be two older people. The memory held it whole: the warmth by the closed window, the sound of a spoon set down badly, his own irritation destined to end before the visit.

Now Alexander drew the glass of water on his table closer and drank. He was surprised to find himself smiling alone. The scene did not tell him how Aurelie would react to a letter written after his awakening. But it reminded him that the young woman had been able to surprise him even when the duke considered himself experienced enough to predict her. To meet her, he would not have to reconstruct a lost feeling with more solemn words. He would have to leave her room to find an answer he had not prepared.

It was not declared love. It was a person who had known how to hold consideration and wit together. Edran wanted to know whether he could know her that way now too, instead of merely seeing her through a complete and closed memory.

He added that he would prefer not to leave their next meeting solely to their families' formulas. The sentence risked sounding like an accusation against a decision they had also made themselves. He rewrote it: he wished to speak to her as they had already done when personally confirming their engagement. No announcement of a break; no renewal turned into a promise of love he did not remember making.

He set down the pen. Part of him could have filled the page with perfect accuracy, using every memory to choose the most suitable word for the young woman he knew. Precision would not give him what he lacked: Aurelie's answer, present, spoken by her.

In the corridor Martin looked for Yselle and received an answer from the opposite room. Her husband asked whether a copy should remain open; she said to close it, then corrected herself and called him back. Life with another person was not solved even in this house where they seemed so good at finding each other. Alexander felt relief in the sound of their steps returning together.

He reread the letter aloud, quietly enough not to include the corridor. In the passage about waking he had written that he had returned. He stopped at the word. For Aurelie it would mean her friend coming out of the coma. For him it contained something he was not telling her.

He did not replace it with an even more distant formula. Instead he wrote that he wished to speak about his recovery and what he wanted to do in the months ahead. It was true and did not force her to understand a secret through a riddle.

The signature took less time than the salutation. Alexander Valcienne was the name that would open the envelope, the title that would accompany the sheet into Aurelie's hands. He did not try to erase its weight by choosing only his first name. He signed and watched the ink turn dull.

When he went downstairs, Yselle was placing her own packet in a delivery folder. He showed her the sealed envelope. She checked the address so it would reach the right destination, then gave it back. She did not ask to read the contents.

The courier arrived shortly afterward, annoyed by the rain that had begun again. He checked the number of packets, arranged them in his case, and listened to the destinations. Alexander entrusted him with the letter to Aurelie in person. It was a quick gesture; the man's hand hesitated no more than it had before the workshop papers.

“If you find it closed?” the duke asked.

The courier explained how it would be left for the next delivery. No door forced open by the title, no promise to bring him an answer that night. Alexander nodded and let him go.

From the doorway he watched the case move away down the street. He had not decided a marriage with a page or freed Aurelie from a choice she had not asked him to dissolve. He had asked to meet her. That could be enough for the evening, provided he did not mistake waiting for an answer.

He returned to the guest room and picked up the crossed-out sheet. He folded it with the name inside. Tomorrow he would have precise things to do before the journey; for the question just sent, there was no reading to anticipate.

From the corridor Teren asked whether departure was still planned for after lunch. Alexander confirmed, adding that he would wait for news from his aunt before getting into the carriage. The security chief nodded and returned to his men.

Chapter 64 — June Eighth

The man from the bank looked for the price on the machine before looking for it on paper.

Elias understood from the way he bent in front of pump number two. He was not checking its alignment; he was trying to find something in the metal resembling the work he had expected to finance. He had removed a glove to touch the edge of the base. His hand stayed clean.

“This one hasn't been put into service.”

“It was checked here,” Yselle said. “The one installed at the Mutual is number one.”

The man moved to the third. His folder already lay open on the dry bench they had left for him. Elias kept his own closed, waiting to know which sheets would be needed. Marcel, beside the window, watched the visitor with the patience he reserved for someone not yet finished asking the wrong question.

“And they belong to you?” the man finally asked.

Yselle took the donation signed the day before. “Here is their origin. Here the objects are identified. No stake in the project is assigned to the donor.”

The representative read. His sharpened pencil was much shorter than its holder suggested; he had to press twice to get it out. He marked a point in the margin of his own copy.

“I can't disburse funds for supplies you're no longer buying.”

“I'm not asking you to.”

Yselle showed him the new statement. Elias recognized the visible difference between the old total and the corrected one but did not try to read its meaning in the visitor's eyes. His own work was in another folder.

The man followed the column of canceled items. He passed over some quickly; at the prototype work he stopped and went back.

“These remain.”

“They've been completed. We keep them separate from those replaced by the machines already here.”

“The reduction doesn't settle previous commitments.”

“That's written in the proposal.”

Elias realized this was not an argument where the better-informed person could simply correct the other. The man from the bank knew how to read accounts. Yselle knew what she had ordered. Both wanted a document that would survive differing memories of the morning.

The representative turned the page. “An invoice is missing.”

Yselle searched the envelope of supporting papers, then called Martin, who was arranging copies in another part of the workshop. Her husband found the sheet between two drawings and passed it to her. A fold concealed its reference. The man opened it, compared the date, and put it with the expenses already committed.

“This work wasn't given by the duke,” he said.

“No,” Yselle replied. “And the people who did it shouldn't lose out.”

The representative nodded. Their first point of agreement concerned not the miracle but ordinary work that still deserved payment.

Then came the question for Elias.

“How closely do they match the drawings?”

He opened his folder. He had prepared the drawings for a technician; the visitor did not need every dimension named. Elias chose the point where the pump body joined the circuit and showed him the reference marked on number two.

“These are the dimensions checked. I compared the measurements with ordinary instruments. Here is the component that can be removed for maintenance.”

The representative moved closer but did not touch the catch. “Can you open it?”

Yselle looked at Elias. He put down the sheet and prepared the bench so nothing he removed would be lost. Machine two was disconnected; demonstrating access did not require stopping the Mutual's water. He removed enough to reveal the removable component, set it down, and let the visitor examine the seat. The existing seal stayed in place while he indicated the edge holding it.

“Can this be made locally?” the man asked.

“We can manufacture replacements from the known forms,” Yselle replied. “We already have some. We'll need to test their use with the operators.”

“Will you still depend on the donor?”

“For these objects, no. That isn't the service we're asking you to finance.”

Elias put the component back. The catch returned to its seat. His hand seemed slower than Martin's the day they had tested the instructions, but the operation succeeded without anyone needing to show him a hidden access point. The representative made a second note.

Marcel showed him his findings on the materials. He offered no universal guarantee for every substance the duke might create in the future. He spoke of the machines present and the checks completed, with the surfaces and instruments available. The man asked him to repeat a distinction between what he had observed and what would become apparent only in use; Ruy repeated it without irritation.

“The problem,” the representative said, “is that the previous financing covered a process. Now you're showing me existing assets and a different process.”

“That's why I wrote before the deadline,” Yselle replied.

The man looked at the reading sheet. “I want to see the service.”

The Mutual was close enough to walk. Elias gathered only the necessary sheets and went out with Yselle, Ruy, and the visitor. Martin stayed with the papers. The duke was not summoned to escort the bank: Elias had glimpsed him in the yard with Teren, discussing departure.

On the way the representative asked Yselle whether the other two pumps already had final destinations. She told him what was established and what was not. She did not turn the number three into three completed installations simply because he wanted to imagine them productive. The second and third machines were real assets available; their future installation work remained to be organized.

At the Mutual Noemi welcomed them with a bowl in her hand. She took it to the kitchen before approaching. Elias noticed she was no longer hiding her wrist, but the representative was looking at the corridor, not her skin.

“It's been working since yesterday?” he asked.

“The service began yesterday,” she replied. “Now you can see how we use it.”

The operator with the pencil took his sheet from the board on the wall. He did not need to ask Elias where it was. He brought the visitor to a spot where he could watch without obstructing the control and pointed out the tank above. Water was coming in; a woman filled a container and passed behind them.

“We used to carry it upstairs,” Noemi said.

The man from the bank looked at the stairs, then the container. He asked how often the outlet was used. Noemi showed him the morning's rhythm without turning it into a promise of income the Mutual had not made. What they saved was journeys, weight, time. They were not selling every bucket to a new customer.

The representative did not seem disappointed. He wanted to know who would handle interruptions and who would receive a request for a replacement. Yselle showed him the workshop's limited commitment and the instructions still to be completed with the operators. She did not pretend the successful test replaced everything left to learn.

Elias climbed to take the requested reference reading. When he came down, the man was examining the old fracture on the side bench.

“This is what broke.”

“The preexisting joint,” Yselle said.

“It's in your report.”

“Yes.”

The visitor turned the cloth to see the worn edge. He sought no saboteur in the metal; he asked who had made the repair and which section had been replaced. Elias pointed out the new section and supports. The distinction did not erase the failure but made it a fact they could discuss without blaming the wrong machine.

Before leaving, the representative returned beneath the tank. He let the woman pass and waited until her container was full. He made no notes until she had gone. Elias wanted to know whether that silence was a good sign; he decided not to derive a measurement from it.

They returned to the workshop shortly before noon. Martin had prepared a copy where previous commitments and new needs occupied two recognizable sections. The man read it and rested his pencil on the point Yselle had kept separate from the beginning: the share collateral.

“Your request is for their release.”

“Yes. The capital must be reduced to the actual need. I don't intend to leave the entire collateral from the previous plan behind different financing.”

The representative considered. He did not say the machines' arrival obliged the bank to do what Yselle wanted. He asked that the new plan leave no unidentified expenses open beneath the word support. She took the pen and defined the items without removing the work needed to make the service repairable.

Elias stopped following individual words when they reached conditions he could not assess. He could recognize an exact dimension and a clause referring to his report; he could not pretend to know what commercial risk was acceptable to the other two.

An argument drew him back to the papers. The representative had separated a receipt from the sheets of canceled expenses and held it beneath two fingers.

“This advance has already been used. It doesn't disappear with the order you won't place.”

Yselle looked for the reference on her statement. “It's in the section for commitments already made.”

“Here the cross-reference points to the supplies column instead.”

Martin bent over the sheet. The reference was wrong: while copying, he had followed the line above. He said so at once and moved the ruler so both could see the point. Yselle corrected the connection without reducing what remained to be settled.

The representative released the receipt. “This is what I need to take back. If the capital goes down, I must explain what remains in force and what will no longer be disbursed.”

“I'm not asking you to cancel money already used,” Yselle said. “I'm asking you not to finance what's already here now. And not to keep me bound for that need.”

Elias looked at the machine beside the table. The metal had not erased the prototype work, the amount advanced, or the earlier signature. It had removed the need to pay for a second construction. In the discussion the difference had finally found a place that did not depend on amazement.

The man from the bank asked him to move the test report closer because his own arm would not reach without knocking over the pencil holder. Elias did, and picked up the pencil that had meanwhile fallen. The man thanked him, then wanted to verify the attached document was the one shown at the Mutual.

Elias recognized a small water stain beside Ruy's signature. He indicated the date and the two requested readings. The representative put the sheets together in the new order, leaving the receipt in the section that still had to be honored. Yselle watched and finally released the edge of her statement.

At last the representative closed his folder only halfway. The sections to rewrite were marked, previous expenses acknowledged, new needs separated from those that had disappeared. They would prepare the final text for early afternoon, before the duke's departure. Yselle did not yet hold the release of her shares. Before her lay terms both parties were prepared to sign.

Ruy asked Elias to return the drawing beside pump two. He put it back and closed the caliper case. The machine looked identical to the one that morning. He knew that, among those sheets, its weight had just changed.

Chapter 65 — Yselle's Signature

Yselle recognized her own name on the new document before understanding what was making her hand grip so tightly.

It was not the pen. That still lay before the representative, beside the sheet that would release the shares. It was the table's edge: she held it between thumb and forefinger as though it were a piece she had to keep from slipping. She loosened her grip and began at the first page.

The folder returned from the bank contained the rewritten text and attachments arranged in the order agreed that morning. The capital was reduced. Money already used and earlier work had not been swept away by the reduction; they stood in the section that still required settlement. Supplies replaced by the gift no longer appeared among those to be financed.

Martin had brought an extra chair without finding anyone to assign it to. He left it beside the table and came to stand by Yselle. She passed him the copy to compare references, keeping the original herself. The signature at the bottom would be hers.

“Let's begin with the collateral,” she said.

The representative opened the separate sheet. He did not hold it away or preface the reading with a speech about the value of the concession. He indicated where the new agreement replaced the previous terms and where the bank declared release of the lien on the shares.

Yselle read the whole sentence. Then she read it a second time, because the first time she had mainly heard the word release.

“Effective upon signing,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Not after the later installations.”

“Those remain work commitments in the forms indicated. They do not maintain the previous collateral.”

Martin found a reference and indicated it without interrupting her. Yselle followed the line: it still pointed to a paragraph in the old agreement. The referenced text was to be replaced, not preserved alongside the new one.

“This needs removing here,” she said.

“And in the attached copy,” the man added.

They corrected both points and initialed them. Yselle did not feel she had just foiled a trap. She had found an error that, left there, would have become a much harder discussion on another day. The correction showed; she did not mind that it showed.

On the nearby bench pumps two and three occupied the same space as that morning. They were the fact that had changed the negotiation. They had not signed for her and would not become false if the ink needed a minute to dry.

The representative returned to the capital section. “This is the amount remaining to be financed under the revised plan. Commitments already made are recorded here. What has been advanced isn't canceled.”

“It's the account we compared.”

Yselle followed it line by line. In her mind the old sum had occupied an almost physical place: she saw it when she entered the workshop, opened a letter, or Martin asked why she was doing the same accounts again after dinner. The new figure did not ask her to imagine less than had happened. It asked her to stop paying in advance for a fear whose cause had gone.

Real work and obligations remained. She wanted them on paper so they could be finished.

The support item had recognizable limits: what the workshop would deliver, what it would show the operators, checks of spare parts and instructions. There was no promise to be available at any hour for any future machine, nor a sentence obliging Alexander to go on creating every necessary part. The gift was documented by the attached deed, with the objects already present and identified.

“For the next demonstration,” the representative said, “you've indicated tomorrow.”

“Yes. The ninth. On the second pump, here, disconnected. Service at the Mutual continues with the first.”

He found the line. “A check of locally made replacement parts.”

“And how to work on the component without calling its creator. Not another installation delivered tomorrow.”

The man noted the clarification in his calendar. Yselle realized she was looking at that date without feeling the floor vanish. The ninth had become a day with work again, instead of the day after the possible loss of her shares.

Martin moved to let her reach the sheet. The copy matched. The donation did not appear as a loan, Alexander received no shares, expenses already owed remained acknowledged. The collateral would be released by this agreement, not left pending as a reward for future obedience.

Yselle picked up the pen.

The nib left a darker line on her name than she expected. She did not stop to correct the stroke; she finished the signature and dated the document. The representative took it, signed for the bank, and moved to the release sheet. Yselle followed that signature too. The little scratch of the pen was an ordinary sound.

They finished with copies and references to the attachments. When the representative handed her the counterpart intended for her, Yselle checked that the release was included. It was. The shares had not been seized, sold, or transferred to her nephew. The lien she had accepted for the previous plan ceased to hold them.

She placed the document before her.

The paper lifted slightly in the draft from the door. Yselle rested the ruler on it. A man passed through the workshop with a cart and asked whether he could cross. Yselle said yes. She had to move her chair to give him room, and that ordinary motion finally let her breathe fully.

The representative gathered his papers. He asked where to send communications about the new plan. She confirmed the workshop address, for her attention. She did not give the address of the house in Rovenne.

“The duke already has a copy of the donation,” she said. “This matter concerns me.”

The man nodded and noted the address. At the door he stopped to look at the pumps again, but this time did not bend to search for their price in the metal. He greeted Martin, thanked him for the copies, and left.

Yselle waited until his footsteps had faded from the yard. Then she turned to her husband.

“I still have my shares.”

Martin looked at the sheet and then her. “Yes.”

He did not add that they had always been hers. He would have been right in a sense narrow enough to hurt. He knew what she meant. He took her hand, the one that had gripped the table, and opened it with his thumb.

Yselle laughed. It came out too high and ended before becoming something she would need to hide. She sat again and pulled the unused chair closer for him.

“Now will you tell me to stop working?”

“I'd like to try on a day when I have some chance.”

“Today you might.”

Martin watched her, surprised. Then he took the signed papers and moved them away from the edge where they might spill a cup. “Five minutes, then.”

They spent them without opening a folder. The door stood ajar; from the yard came someone looking for Elias and asking where a box had been left. Yselle knew the answer, but her husband showed her five fingers and she stayed seated. The box was found without her.

At the end of the allotted time Martin picked up a sheet left beneath the ruler. It was the reduced alternative she had prepared before her nephew arrived, when the work still had to bend to what they could afford. One edge was full of calculations; on the other stood a repeatedly drawn outline of an object that would not fit the available space.

“Are we keeping this?”

Yselle took it. The silence of that evening returned, the fear of mistaking a smaller project for a free choice simply because it was the only one she could afford. It had been a serious alternative. She had been right to prepare it, and now did not have to carry it out to prove the work had not been wasted.

“We keep it with the earlier stages. Not among the sheets to carry out.”

Martin found a folder and offered it open. Yselle marked the edge so the superseded version could be recognized. She did not call it failed. The paper had done what it could until something better arrived.

Her husband stayed looking at the troubled margin.

“That evening, did you want me to tell you to give up?”

Yselle hesitated. “I wanted to be sure you'd stay even if I had to.”

Martin took the ruler from her hand and put it on the table. “I didn't marry the estimate.”

She smiled, then rested her forehead against his shoulder for an instant. The collateral had stopped tightening around her shares. To stop tightening around everything else, she would need that sentence too.

When she rose, she already knew what to do the following day. She called a worker and asked him to leave pump two free for the replacement-part test, with the tools within reach on the bench. She had the updated instruction sheet set aside. Maintenance had not become a punishment for those who could not create matter; it was the work that made receiving it useful.

Then she went to find Alexander.

She found him near the entrance with some of his luggage already ready. Teren was speaking to one of the two guards; the other was checking a bag strap. Her nephew saw the sheet before her smile.

“Signed?”

“Signed. Capital reduced, collateral released. What was already owed stays in the account.”

He did not reach for the agreement. He waited for her to show him the release clause, read it, and returned the paper.

“I'm glad,” he said.

Yselle wanted a sentence large enough to answer everything. Instead she hugged him briefly because the sheet might crumple between them. Alexander placed a hand on her back. She drew away laughing at her own care for the paper.

“I'll put this somewhere safe. Don't leave without the letter for Celine.”

“I'm waiting for it.”

She returned to the table with a sheet different from those just signed. Her niece's letter was still folded beside the folder; it contained a garden that would drive anyone crossing it with a wheelbarrow to despair. Yselle followed the path with her finger, then drew a more direct passage in the margin.

She wrote the protest against the curve first. Then she told her about the three machines, the first installation working, the old section that had failed, and Martin's bread. She avoided making Noemi's wound family news to distribute, leaving Alexander what he had already chosen to tell. She did not avoid the failure. Celine would find the hidden question even inside a cautious sentence.

When she sealed the envelope, a gray fingerprint remained on the flap. She rubbed her thumb, looked at the mark, and decided her niece would recognize the sender anyway.

The new contract was in the closed folder. The letter waited on the table, ready to leave with Alexander. Yselle looked at both and realized that for once they were not asking for the same part of her life.

Chapter 66 — The Road to Rovenne

Yselle's letter arrived last, when Teren had already checked that nobody had left a bag inside the workshop.

Alexander picked it up from the table. There was a gray fingerprint on the flap and Celine's name on the front, written with care that ran out quickly at the final letter. He put it in his inner pocket, separate from papers he would not need to open during the journey.

His aunt's agreement was signed. She had shown him the result: capital reduced, share collateral released, work already owed still acknowledged. Alexander carried no title to the machines or stake in the enterprise. Behind him, on the workshop floor, pumps two and three remained whole.

Yselle also handed him the book he had left in the guest room.

“Is this yours?”

“Celine's.”

“Then take it back. If you forget it here, she'll write that I've confiscated it to stop her reading interesting things.”

He took it. The marker remained near the beginning; he had read less than he thought. Celine might ask what he had done with it in three days, and changing a workshop would not provide a good answer about that chapter.

Noemi arrived while Martin was telling him where he had placed the last bag. She did not run. She stopped at the gate to let a guard carrying a box pass, then approached with an envelope held flat against her side.

“Before you go.”

Alexander recognized the sheet she had begun writing after the failure, now concealed in the envelope. “The questions?”

“The ones I've managed to put in order. The rest, if you like, I'll ask aloud.”

She handed it to him. The paper's edge had been folded several times and smoothed again. Alexander did not look for a list of people already waiting for a promised healing. Noemi had brought him a way to begin discussing it.

“I'll read them on the way,” he said.

“I haven't written what you must do by tomorrow.”

“I understood.”

The woman looked at her wrist, almost annoyed by a habit she had not yet lost. The skin was whole. She had not needed to keep her arm still to preserve the result.

“There's a question of my own,” she said.

Alexander nodded without asking her to say it in front of Yselle and the men preparing to leave. Noemi lowered her voice anyway, though not to secrecy.

“I'd like to discuss it without coming to represent everyone else.”

“Then we'll talk about you.”

Relief did not erase her fear. It let her smile. “For the water, though, I can speak for the whole house. This morning I had to look for someone I used to find on the stairs with two buckets.”

Yselle asked where she had gone. Noemi described a different task, long waiting for someone with free hands. Martin laughed at how promptly work found the time released. That did not make the result smaller: there was no longer that weight to carry upstairs.

Alexander put the envelope of questions beside the letter for Celine. He would need to speak to Mirel when he had a way to arrange future meetings. He had not already examined the people Noemi knew, nor would their names grant access to their bodies. What he had was a real capacity to offer and an equally real wish not to leave it confined to a cut he happened to witness.

Teren approached. “We're ready.”

The two guards waited with their horses. The driver was in place; the carriage had regained its smell of warmed leather and road. Alexander said goodbye to Martin, then embraced his aunt. Yselle kept a hand on his arm an instant longer than the farewell needed.

“Write when you've arrived.”

“Celine will do it before me.”

“That wasn't permission to answer through your sister.”

He promised news of his own. Martin told him not to judge the whole household's cooking by one burned slice of bread; Noemi asked whether that bread was already in the letter. Yselle said it was now a documented fact.

Alexander climbed in with the book. Teren sat opposite. One guard moved ahead of the carriage, the other stayed behind; the driver guided the horses out of the yard and onto the road to Rovenne.

The city did not fall away all at once. First they passed gates Alexander had learned to recognize, then the shops grew less dense and spaces appeared between buildings. A cart loaded with barrels made everyone wait at a narrow point. Teren opened the door just enough to speak to the driver; the guard ahead returned to indicate where they would be able to pass.

Alexander watched the edges of the other cart's wheels. A few movements would have let the man clear the road, but a badly fastened barrel stopped him reversing as he wished. A second man climbed down, pushed the strap into the right position, and signaled. The cart moved. They passed without anyone needing to recognize who sat behind the glass.

The delay added a few minutes to a journey that would take many more. Twenty-three kilometers had room for a stop, a bend too tight, the horses' sound changing as the surface hardened. Alexander rested the book on his knee and opened Noemi's envelope.

The first question concerned the place for visits. The second asked whether people could talk without having to begin immediately. The third had been written with different pressure on the pen: what should a person who had worn a prosthesis for years tell him about herself?

Alexander reread the last sentence. It did not ask him to hide his power or pretend an old injury must be beyond reach. It asked how to enter the possibility of something she had stopped expecting. The sheet continued with other observations and the wish for a meeting without a flooded room around them.

He folded it without adding answers in the margin. He would write to Noemi after choosing how to arrange the conversation, with a real address and availability. Healing the arm remained a fact; the future of the other request was not yet a completed examination.

Teren watched him put away the envelope. “Will I need to plan another journey?”

“Perhaps. It isn't arranged.”

“Tell me when it is.”

“I will.”

The security chief went back to watching the road. He did not ask him to repeat the woman's questions. Alexander opened Celine's book and within a few lines found a passage he did not remember despite having read it before. He began from the previous paragraph and this time followed it to the end.

A jolt made him lose the line. Teren looked at the book and asked whether it was a document he had to finish before they arrived.

“Worse. My sister will want to know what I think.”

The security chief smiled without keeping his eyes from the window for long. Alexander showed him a diagram: a lever raised a load, resting on a fulcrum indicated by a triangle. Teren asked whether the ground beneath that support was hard.

“The drawing assumes it's fixed.”

“Then it chose a better road than ours.”

Alexander laughed and searched for the lost line. He knew the relationship between distances from the fulcrum; instead he returned to the legend, following how the manual made it readable to someone encountering it for the first time. Arrows distinguished weight from applied force. He showed Teren the point; Teren returned to the road without claiming to discuss the calculation. The carriage took a bend and the horses slowed on the rougher surface. Alexander held the page with one hand until the motion steadied. He thought of the questions Celine would ask looking at that diagram and kept reading even the explanations he could have summarized himself.

For a while the journey held only that: someone else's words, wheels, the sound of a bridle outside the window. Nobody waited for him to make an answer appear. Reaching the end of the page, he realized he had not looked at the road for several minutes.

The countryside offered fields, fences, distant smoke. The duke's memories could give that landscape familiarity; Edran also saw what he had not yet learned to know. Not every house was a story he owned because he recognized its roof. Behind some windows someone might be arguing about a delivery; behind others nobody knew a duke had passed.

He thought of Aurelie and the letter entrusted the evening before. It might already have arrived or still be making its ordinary way through deliveries. The road offered no news of her. He did not search passing faces as though the world must have arranged a sign for every question he had decided to ask.

Halfway along they stopped briefly for the horses. Teren got out and spoke to one guard; the other stayed where he could see the road. Alexander marked his place in the book, checked both envelopes in his pocket, and drank the water prepared for him. When the carriage moved again, the light had changed sides on the seats.

His body's strength did not need to diminish for him to feel the weight of a day. He had said goodbye to people he wanted to see again, received trust that required time, sent a question whose answer he did not know. He could hold metal without effort and still want a quiet chair without someone reading that moment as a measure of his powers.

Rovenne appeared after a little more than three and a half hours, when shadows had lengthened enough to change the pattern on the walls. The guard ahead slowed at the entrance, the driver recognized the route home. Alexander closed the book and looked at the window from which Celine had watched him leave.

Maurice waited when the carriage stopped. He did not rush to the step; he took the bag Alexander did not need to carry inside and asked whether they had met any difficulties.

“Only a cart that wouldn't move.”

Teren confirmed the journey had been uneventful, then attended to the men and horses. The two guards did not vanish as soon as the duke was safe through the door. Alexander saw them exchange a few words before one went away with the driver.

The house's air had a smell the memories recognized before he did. Maurice indicated where to leave his jacket and suggested he sit while something to drink was brought. Alexander agreed. A pale line of dust lay on his dark cuff; he looked at it and thought Celine would find the whole road in that stain too.

“Where is she?” he asked.

Maurice needed no name. “Near the library. She heard the carriage arrive.”

Alexander rose after drinking. He took the book and the letter with the gray fingerprint; he left Noemi's envelope in his own folder so the questions would not mix with family news.

Celine met him in the passage before he reached the library. She looked at the book, then at the pocket from which he was taking the letter.

“Did Aunt write back?”

Alexander offered her the envelope with the volume she had lent him. She took only the letter.

Chapter 67 — Her Aunt's Letter

Celine recognized her aunt's envelope by the stain before she even saw her own name. A gray thumbprint crossed the flap; Yselle had tried to clean her hands before writing and lost patience halfway through.

The book had remained in Alexander's hands. Celine looked at it, then returned to the letter.

“You carried the book all the way to Lethorne?”

“Yes.”

“Then you can carry it back to the library. The shelf hasn't moved.”

He looked down at the spine, and for a moment Celine thought she had offended him. Instead he smiled and set off. He still wore the dust of the journey, a pale line across his dark cuff. He had come home before dinner, tired enough to sit when Maurice suggested it, but not tired enough to escape her questions. He had told her about the road, Martin, the workshop. He had said too little about the pumps.

Celine followed, slipping a finger beneath the flap. She did not want him watching while she read. Nor did she want to go to her room. She chose the armchair by the window and pointed to the opposite chair with her foot.

The letter began with a complaint about Celine's garden design: the curved path forced anyone pushing a wheelbarrow to go all the way around a pool. Her aunt had added a rectangle in the margin and written: The poor man can get through here, unless you mean to work him to death. Celine swallowed a laugh. That passage spoiled the surprise of the avenue, but she had not thought of the wheelbarrow.

Then the handwriting grew smaller. Yselle wrote that Alexander had seen the machines, that they now had three, that one was already working at the Mutual. She also described a breakdown and specified that the part which failed had been old. No description of blood. No name for the person who had been injured. The next sentence concerned a dinner Martin had burned; Celine realized her aunt had put it there on purpose.

Alexander’s message had arrived the day before. It had already mentioned Noemi and the healed cut; it had not said who had healed her. Now Celine searched her aunt’s letter for the part her brother had left out.

She reread the stretch between the breakdown and the dinner.

“Where were you?”

Alexander put the book on the little table. “At the Mutual.”

“When it broke.”

“Yes.”

“Were you hurt?”

“No.”

She examined his hands, his neck above the collar, the line of his hair. She found no bandages. Of course, he could have had something dressed beneath his clothes. The irritating thing about grown-ups was that there was always another way for them to be right without having told the whole truth.

“Aunt Yselle says you were useful.”

“I'm glad she didn't write underfoot.”

She did not laugh. He stopped smiling.

“Someone was cut. We helped her. The pipe was repaired, and the pump worked the following day.”

“Did everyone help, or did you do something strange?”

The question came out louder than she intended. From the corridor came the sound of a tray. Celine waited for it to move away, clutching the letter on her knees. She had not decided whether she wanted the answer to be yes.

“I did something,” Alexander said. “Did Yselle tell you about the three machines?”

“She wrote that you have them. Not that you bought them.”

“I didn't buy them.”

The evening seemed to shift. The window reflected their two faces and the lamp, still unlit, ordinary things that had not received the news. Celine turned to look at her brother directly.

“You made them?”

“Using Yselle's design and her model. I have an ability that allows me to create matter.”

Her first questions were foolish and came all at once. Could he make a ship? A cake? A horse? Had their aunt seen? Celine bit her tongue before saying the third; she did not want him choosing the easy question and pretending they were all the same.

“How big?” she asked.

“The pumps are taller than your desk.”

“Three?”

“Three.”

She imagined a machine growing like a plant out of the floor and immediately erased the image. Perhaps it had appeared all at once. Perhaps it had made an enormous noise. Her brother sat with a fold of his trousers caught against the chair leg, and nothing in his stories had prepared her for this fact.

“Why didn't you write to tell me?”

“I wanted to tell you in person.”

“Convenient. You get three days to think about it, and I have to come up with something clever at once.”

He shifted his leg, freeing the fabric. “You don't have to.”

That answer made her angrier than a reprimand. She looked down at the letter. Yselle had written about her, too: if she still wanted to see a workshop, she would now find a great deal of disorder and something worth the journey. No promise of a date. An invitation real enough to hold on to.

Celine read it aloud to him. Alexander answered too quickly.

“For now, we'd better wait.”

“Wait for what?”

“Until they've finished the trials.”

“You said it works.”

“One machine works. The workshop is still a workplace.”

“I know. I wasn't expecting them to let me stand underneath the machine.”

She took the paper by one corner and straightened it, because her hand was shaking and she did not want him to notice. Alexander watched the movement. That had changed: before the coma he had missed certain things, or pretended to. Now he sometimes looked at her as if he had all the time in the world. It was not always pleasant.

“There was an accident,” he said.

“You've just told me. And you were there. I can't even go afterward?”

“That isn't what I meant.”

“Then what did you mean?”

He had to fall silent. Celine felt a petty satisfaction and lost it at once. She did not want to beat him. She wanted the journey, the carriage windows open, her aunt showing her something without first asking whether she was too tired. She wanted a story to tell that did not begin beside a bed.

A sheet from her afternoon's work lay on the little table. She moved it between them. She had copied the contents pages of two books on elementary mechanics, marking those the library owned. Beside one heading she had drawn a large, ugly question mark.

“I don't just want to watch sparks. I looked for the chapter on levers. The older book leaves out all the steps. The other one uses signs I don't understand.”

Alexander picked up the sheet, not the letter. “This is a ratio.”

“I know what a ratio is. I don't know why the weight of the arm disappears here.”

He read the line. “I can't tell you from a contents page either.”

At last, an answer that did not make her feel small. Celine got up to fetch the volume. She had to pull it from a row packed too tightly; a second book slid against her wrist, and Alexander reached out. She caught it first.

“It wasn't an emergency.”

“I saw.”

“You still made that face.”

This time he laughed, briefly. She set the volume down and found the page. The drawing showed a horizontal lever and two square weights. Apparently the wood weighed nothing. Alexander found the sentence on the previous page; Celine read it twice, annoyed. She had skipped that very sentence.

“Don't say it.”

“I haven't said anything.”

“You were about to.”

“I was about to ask whether you want to write to Martin.”

The suggestion caught her off guard. Writing to Martin meant showing her question to someone who taught for a living. He might answer with an exercise, or discover in three lines that she had misunderstood everything else as well. On the other hand, he had never treated her like a vase to be moved away from the edge.

Celine picked up the pencil she had left in the book as a marker. Its point was broken. She searched the drawer for a sharpener and found a ribbon, two buttons, and her receipt for returning a volume she had believed lost. Fortunately, the library contained those embarrassments too. Alexander passed her a pencil from the table without comment. She drew an arrow toward the lever arm and erased her first question. She would not write that the book was wrong. She would ask whether the wood's weight had been ignored only in the example or whether there was a way to include it in the calculation. That seemed a question less easily dismissed.

“I can write to Aunt Yselle and put in a sheet for him.”

“You can.”

“And the visit?”

Alexander leaned back. She recognized the temptation to postpone, that pause in which an adult searched for something else to do. This time she let him speak.

“Let's write to Yselle and ask when she can have you. With someone accompanying you, on a day she chooses. Not tomorrow morning.”

“I didn't ask for tomorrow morning.”

“I know.”

“You didn't just say yes to make me stop?”

“I said we can arrange it. If there's a reason to change the day afterward, I'll tell you.”

It was less than she wanted and much more than she had possessed five minutes earlier. Celine retrieved the letter. The middle crease had grown soft. On the back, Yselle had added that her nephew ate two portions of something Martin cooked after criticizing its appearance. That was something she could read aloud without consequences.

She did. Alexander protested at the absence of a reliable description of the dish. Celine asked whether his ability could produce a less ugly dinner; he answered that he would have to know the design. She laughed properly, and the sound seemed almost outrageous in the library where, for weeks, she had learned to keep her voice down.

Maurice came to light the lamps. He paused at the threshold only long enough to ask whether they needed anything else. Celine requested paper, then remembered it was already in the drawer and blushed. The butler did not point it out.

Alexander returned to the book about levers, but she did not begin her reply immediately. Inside her folder, underneath the garden drawing, lay another sheet. She had written Etienne Ravelle's name and then three lines, none of which she liked. His father's name appeared in conversations that stopped when she entered. She knew enough to hate that silence and not enough to fill it.

“If I write to Etienne,” she said, “do I have to let you read all of it?”

Alexander raised his head. The pause was different from the one about the workshop.

“No. But if there's anything about the proceedings, don't try to settle it on your own.”

“I don't want to settle it. I wanted to ask how he is.”

“Then ask him.”
