He had not said Etienne was innocent of everything, or that his father would come home. Celine would have found either answer unbearable. She looked at the three lines: they discussed the weather and the garden, as if they could throw a sheet over the entire room.

“I don't know whether he'll answer.”

“Neither do I.”

She left that sheet in the folder. She did not want to write every letter of her life in one evening. To her aunt she first described the wheelbarrow, admitting only that perhaps the side passage would be useful. Then she asked about the visit. For Martin she prepared a precise question and a copy of the drawing with the square weights.

Alexander sat on the other side of the table. The lamp lit one hand; the dust of the road still marked his other sleeve. Celine tried to picture that hand making metal appear and could not. She did not ask him again. At the bottom of the sheet she added a line for Yselle: I want to see the real pump, not just the drawing.

Before she finished, she picked up her garden again. With her ruler she opened a passage beside the pool. It was too straight and annoyed her, so she drew an arch of roses over it. The wheelbarrow would still get through. That, she thought, was something she could allow without losing everything else.

Chapter 68 — The Open Account

The glove was too small for Mael's hand and too large for the name assigned to it in the inventory. Operator's textile accessory, the card said. It did not mention the two fingers sewn inside because its owner had been missing the real ones.

He set it beside the goods ledger without putting it on. The hardened leather had preserved the curve of a grip. Whoever confiscated it had found it cheaper to leave the grease than pay for cleaning. Mael had bought the crate along with four unusable instruments, and the glove was the only thing that had yet to bring him an offer.

Sybille came in carrying a folded newspaper and a loose sheet. She did not sit. She looked at the courtyard door, the corridor door, and the passage between the shelves, then laid the newspaper over the glove.

“They're manufacturing miracles in Lethorne now.”

Mael moved the paper, irritated by the way she treated things without a price. “The newspaper or the workshop?”

“The newspaper sells the headline. The workshop apparently has the pumps.”

The article occupied little more than a column. The duke had visited Yselle Valcienne's project; three machines had been provided, and one was already serving the Mutual. The writer's name did not appear. One sentence promised benefits the rest of the text did not establish. Mael read the words attributed to a worker twice and those attributed to the financier only once.

“Who gave you the other sheet?”

“News bought along with the newspaper. The workers saw three machines. Nobody unloaded them from a wagon. That's the story.”

“Who saw it?”

“Not the person who sold the news.”

Mael pushed the sheet back. A complete lie would have been more agreeable: at least he would have known where the work began. The duke was young, rich, and close enough to Yselle to let her risk a piece of publicity. He might have paid for machines, brought them by a means the others did not know, or created them. The last possibility changed the value of many agreements. It did not tell Mael what abilities he possessed or how much he had chosen to show.

“Someone says he healed a woman,” Sybille added.

“The same source?”

“Another rumor. I don't have a statement from the woman.”

Mael drew a line beneath two lines in his own note. He wrote rumors, not facts, and hated the caution of the word. In the past he had seen men lose a warehouse because an implausible report proved correct. He had also seen entire investigations chase after a paper seller. The difference cost time.

The loose sheet also contained a drawing. A rectangle, a wheel, three parallel lines: the seller had called that a pump. Mael held it near the lamp and found a pencil impression on the back. The man had copied the drawing through another sheet, or written over it. It was not evidence of the machine. It was evidence of a desire to sell a drawing.

“How much did you give him?”

Sybille named the amount. Little enough to make an argument pointless, enough to ensure the seller would seek another piece of news by morning.

“Don't buy the next installment.”

“If he brings a name?”

“He'll bring the name he thinks you want to hear.”

She took the sheet away from the light. “Sometimes he brings the one he heard. We aren't the only clever people in the city.”

Mael could have reproduced the handwriting before him, remembered every irregularity, compared it with something authentic. He would not have turned that pencil stroke into a visit to the workshop. He let Sybille put the drawing back on the desk and pointed to the only useful line: the name of a street where the story circulated among people delivering materials. A place to check later, without showing up carrying an open newspaper and questions about the duke's wonders.

“That one,” he said.

“I know it.”

“That isn't the same as knowing who works there now.”

Sybille nodded, and he realized he had just repeated an objection she might have made to him. He let it pass without correction.

Sybille looked at the glove. “What is that supposed to do?”

“Nothing.”

“Then why keep it on the desk?”

“I'm looking for the right crate.”

It was not a lie. Nor was it the reason he had picked it up. The glove reminded him how quickly inventories freed objects from people. He had learned his trade in rooms where a desk could be sold before its owner finished protesting. He had been good. He had remained good when his sister was the one protesting.

He closed the ledger before reaching pages where he might have looked for a name. The past did not need his help to continue existing.

“Is Ilian coming back tomorrow?” he asked.

“He's supposed to contact someone who can answer about the collar. He hasn't promised to bring anyone here.”

“I didn't ask him to.”

She looked at him. Mael allowed her time to decide how much of the sentence was true. The returned sample and the rejected request had taught him at least this: pressing the same point of access increased its price without making it any more available. Ilian might still obtain an answer about an ordinary component. People willingly gave a small question the attention they denied a large one.

“His balance?” Sybille asked.

“When the job is complete.”

“The old one.”

Mael took a sheet from the pile on the left. The amount had been acknowledged; payment remained suspended. Each true word made actual money less necessary. Ilian had worked and wanted to be paid. As long as he came back to ask, there was a conversation to continue.

“I'll write that the account is under review.”

“He knows that already.”

“Then he'll be pleased to hear it hasn't been forgotten.”

Sybille did not smile. He had purchased her abilities, not her amusement. That boundary had always seemed generous to him.

He rose to fetch two shipping documents from the shelf. One concerned a disused press, the other a maintenance kit. They were ordinary forms, full of spaces meant to receive names no one would remember at the destination. The buyer of the access wanted a usable device; the latest letter asked whether a person capable of keeping it working was also available. It no longer said only device.

Mael left the letter in its folder. Sybille had read her own contract, the terms of payment and access to the premises. She had not read the sheet through which he had arranged for her possible delivery. He had no reason to show it to her that evening.

“If the duke really made the pumps,” she said, “he could make other things.”

“We aren't going to ask him.”

“It wasn't a suggestion.”

“Good.”

His answer came out too sharply. He turned the document toward her, showing only the section concerning the route. A shipment bound for the port did not have to appear bound for the port the entire way. An intermediate warehouse with a credible reason to receive it would suffice. That was work they both understood.

“That address doesn't receive machinery anymore,” Sybille said.

Mael went still. “Since when?”

“Last month. They've leased the yard. There are crates of fruit.”

He had an updated map in a drawer, a list of concession holders, and three genuine attestations; none had told him the courtyard smelled of pears. He crossed out the address with a single stroke.

“Another one.”

“I'll give you one after I've seen it.”

“I didn't ask you to go tonight.”

At last she sat. She picked a thread from her sleeve and wound it around a finger. “Varek wants to know how much longer she should keep asking about the same suppliers.”

“As long as the questions look like her work.”

“It is her work. That's how she knows when they start sounding strange.”

Mael accepted the correction with a nod. The distributor had a reason to be in the street, to know doors and shifts, to ask who had moved shop. A reason that could be used up. She was sixty-one and had no wish to spend the remainder of her life explaining why she sold newspapers beside the wrong person.

“Tell her to stop mentioning the duke. We need supplies and deliveries, not family gossip.”

Sybille tilted her head. “She'll like that order.”

Mael returned to the desk. From the drawer he took an old form that had already been canceled. The paper bore a destination that no longer existed and the stamp of an office that had moved. He could have copied the marks. He did not need a false stamp inviting the first conscientious clerk to ask a question. He needed a story ordinary enough not to require a visit.

He wrote grinding equipment, then crossed it out. Too specific for a device they did not possess. He wrote sampling equipment. Beneath it, accompanied technical kit. The second expression seemed better until Sybille read it upside down.

“Accompanied by whom?”

“Whoever knows how to set it up.”

“Then it isn't a kit. It's a work trip.”

“That depends on how you declare it.”

“It also depends on who gets off at the destination.”

The thread broke between her fingers. She had not raised her voice. Mael felt an unreasonable need to make her hold her contract again, to force her to look at the precision of her signature. Instead he folded the form in half.

“We have nothing to transport yet.”

That was the point on which they could agree. No hinge had arrived at the warehouse, no maintenance worker had accepted a job, no door to their objective had opened. The temptation to act simply to stop waiting was an expensive form of impatience. He could send them against the workshop, and perhaps obtain an enormous machine of no interest to the buyer and a duke interested in him. A very poor exchange.

“Tomorrow Ilian asks the commercial question,” he said. “A measurement, a part, a price. If they ask about the recipient, he names whoever is supposed to receive the work. No visits to the Mutual out of curiosity.”

“Can we pay for the part?”

“If it's worth what they ask.”

“Ilian is asking for something that's already worth it.”

Mael rested his palm on the closed ledger. She saw the gesture and rose before he answered. She was also good at choosing where to break off a conversation. She still had the advantage of knowing she could leave it through the door.

“Get me the new address,” he said.

“After I've seen it,” Sybille repeated.

She collected the newspaper but left the sheet of rumors. The glove lay uncovered on the desk. After she left, Mael listened to her footsteps as far as the courtyard; he did not attempt to follow them beyond what he could hear. The bolt returned to its place, and the warehouse resumed the small noises of badly settled crates.

He opened the buyer's folder. Sybille's second contract lay beneath the recent letter, protected from the light. He did not touch it. Instead he found the line recording the payment owed to Ilian and copied the date of the new contact above the old one without crossing it out. Even an open account needed to be kept in order.

For a few minutes he reread the news about the pumps. The paper offered nothing more. The duke remained distant, his power undefined; those who worked with him had schedules, creditors, hands that could grow tired. Mael would not try to buy the miracle. He could look for whoever brought it tools.

He picked up the glove to return it to the crate. A strip of cloth came out of the cuff, bearing a coarse seam: a repair made at home, not a function of the object. He carefully tucked it inside again. Then he changed the card. Under quantity he wrote one, not a pair; under condition he added incomplete. It was an accurate description. He extinguished the lamp, satisfied to have corrected at least that.

Chapter 69 — The Part That Wears Out

The first gasket made in Lethorne had nothing miraculous about it. It was a dark ring, still damp where the cut had exposed the fiber, and it insisted on remaining oval. Elias laid it on the drawing. The man who had cut it turned it over with one finger.

“You can see the edge this way.”

The edge was not beautiful. Three spots retained marks from the shears, too small for Elias to feel without running a fingernail over them. Yselle had chosen a sheet of material available from a local supplier. If it held, they could order more parts without waiting for his return.

Pump number two waited on its base in the workshop. The white mark on its casing was almost dry, and they had set a basin beneath its feet. The machine was not connected to the Mutual's network. It would draw from the low container and send water through the test pipe, which returned to the container from the other side. Elias had followed the route with his hand before they began: none of their valves could interrupt the service provided by pump number one.

Number three remained covered a little farther away. That morning it seemed less impossible than number two, which they were about to open.

Marcel Ruy set a box for the fasteners on the bench. “Which one have you decided to sacrifice?”

“Only your good humor,” Yselle answered.

“That hasn't held water in years.”

Elias smiled without looking up. He had lined up the wrenches from smallest to largest, and now it seemed a foolish display. He moved two of them. Marcel noticed but chose to examine the ring instead. Before opening the machine, he handed Elias the workshop's safety goggles; Elias had to adjust the strap, which hung too loosely behind his head.

The maker said he had cut two from the same sheet, along with a scrap for identifying the material on the next order. Yselle asked him to leave the waste as well. He grimaced: waste was not something you paid for. She pointed to the drawing, and he understood that she wanted to preserve the shape around the cut, not buy his shop's rubbish.

Elias removed the cover with Marcel's help. The interior did not gleam as it had in his imaginings on the first evening; it bore a trace of water and the thin shadow of oil where there ought to be oil. The parts were real down to their smell. Ruy placed the fasteners in the box without counting them aloud. Elias counted them anyway.

The original gasket came out whole. The new ring was slightly stiffer and needed help settling into its seat. Elias held one side while the man who had cut it pushed the other. Their fingers covered part of the edge. Yselle asked for room to see; he moved aside, the ring seemed to settle, and Marcel brought the cover close.

Elias had prepared a sheet with three columns: assembly, test, inspection. He wrote successful in the first before he had even put down his pencil. He left the word there.

Noemi arrived while they were connecting the return pipe. She carried a folder under her arm and a small parcel wrapped in bread paper. She set it on the bench farthest from the water.

“I was told you were taking a new machine to pieces in here.”

“A malicious description,” Yselle said.

“The old one is still working at our place. Gaston threw me out because I kept watching his hands while he checked the pump in service.”

There was no old pump in service: she meant the first, already old merely because it was in its place. Elias felt an absurd pleasure at the usage. Noemi approached just close enough to examine the circuit, then sat on a stool. She did not ask about Alexander, and no one had to invent a reason to mention him.

Marcel indicated the drain valve. It was closed. Yselle checked the container, Elias the pipe, and the test began without a solemn declaration. Motion filled the workshop with a steady noise. The water rose, crossed the transparent inspection section, and returned to the tank. For a few moments Elias watched only that return.

The first drop landed on his wrist.

It might have come from the wet pipe. Then another arrived, and a dark line appeared beneath the cover. Marcel said “Enough” at the same moment Yselle reached the stop. The noise ceased; the thread of water did not. Elias shut off the test circuit's feed, keeping his hand away from the gap. The drain sent the remaining water into the basin with a much more modest sound.

No jet, no fall. Elias's heart still beat too hard. Beneath his sleeve, the memory of the wet railing mingled with his dry wrist. He glanced toward Noemi and found her with both feet on the floor, the folder on her knees.

“I like that noise better,” she said, indicating the drain.

The maker bent over the empty container. “It could be the sheet.”

“It could,” Yselle answered. “Let's open it first.”

Elias would have liked to nod along with her, but he had been the one holding the edge. He took off his glasses, wiped them with the wrong part of his sleeve, and had to start again. Marcel waited for the circuit to finish draining. He did not touch the cover until Elias showed him the level in the open section.

This time there seemed to be far more fasteners than before. He removed them without hurrying, irritated by his own desire to make them disappear. When they lifted the cover, the defect was there: a small section of the ring had folded inward. The material was not torn. It retained the flattened impression of the edge, like a bitten lip.

Elias pointed. “Here.”

The man reached for the part, but Yselle raised a hand. “First I want to see it where it is.”

The seat had accepted it badly, or they had put it in badly. With Near Coordinates, Elias reached the geometry of the exposed edge, the proximity between the bottom of the groove and the closing surface. Its arrangement became distinct in his perception, a relationship of distances made difficult to see by the reflection on the oil. It did not tell him how the material had folded. They could already see that.

He took the calipers and compared the accessible points. The dimensions agreed with those recorded. The groove had not narrowed. The maker breathed out; Elias felt that small acquittal and envied it.

“I let go when it looked as though it was in,” he said.

“So did I,” the man answered.

Marcel turned the cover over. On its underside, a trace of oil broke at the same spot. “It went in crooked, and we closed it.”

He did not say who had tightened it last. Elias remembered the wrench in his own hand. He would have preferred Marcel to say it, perhaps so he could answer something. Instead he had to erase successful and write leaks: folded edge. The pencil eraser spread a gray shadow across the first column.

Noemi opened the parcel: four pieces of bread with cheese. “Have one before you start blaming breakfast.”

The maker laughed with too much relief. Yselle took the bread and asked whether he could obtain a more regular cut by using a support rather than holding the sheet in his hand. The man answered at once, then slowed, thinking about his own bench. He could make a template. The first order would not pay for it by itself.

“I'm not ordering a hundred rings,” she said. “Tell me what it costs to make two we can fit.”

“These can be fitted.”

“Then let's fit the second.”

Elias ate standing up. His shame was beginning to retreat, leaving the more useful irritation of something that needed doing again. He washed his hands before touching the other ring. The folded one remained on the paper without becoming evidence that the material had failed.

They laid the second part on the clean surface. The maker trimmed a small ridge with a sharp tool, well away from the open machine. Marcel checked that no fibers remained in the seat. This time Elias guided the ring around its entire circumference and released it only after the other man had removed his fingers. Yselle looked from one side, then asked them to turn the lamp.

The cover came down slowly. To see the opposite edge, Elias had to kneel. The stone pressed through his trousers; that tiny discomfort had the virtue of restoring his whole body to him. When he stood again, he had found no hidden fold. He wrote nothing in the first column yet.

They tightened the fasteners evenly, without trying to compensate for the earlier leak with extra force. Marcel made a mark on the sheet when they were ready. Water returned to the circuit. Elias ran a dry cloth beneath the joint before the test, then laid it white on the bench where everyone could see whether it changed.

The machine started again. The first circuit of water raised a bubble in the return, the second carried it away. Elias waited for the drop on his wrist. It did not come. He bent without placing his fingers on the joint, looked from the basin side, and moved to make room for the other man. He found no water either.

Yselle prolonged the test. The noise that at first had seemed an answer became a noise again: they could talk without lowering it at every sentence. Noemi finished her bread and took a page of notes from the folder.

“When this part has to be changed at our place, what do we need to have?”

“The replacement,” Elias answered, and felt an idiot before she showed him the list.

“I'd guessed that much. I need the right name to order it by, who sells it, and the sheet that has to stay near the machine. I'd rather not ask for a gasket and get twenty different answers.”

Yselle handed her the scrap the maker had saved. He added his commercial description to the sheet, correcting one letter. Noemi asked whether he could recognize the material without seeing the original part. The man assured her he could, then asked to keep a sample. They divided the scrap.

Elias copied the seat and the sides of the ring onto his drawing. He did not write an explanation of every pump in the world. He drew the one they had opened, with the space needed to reach the fasteners. Noemi made him move the key: a dirty hand would land over the corner he had chosen. The erasure obscured part of the first line, and the drawing stopped looking ready for an exhibition.

When they stopped the machine, the joint was dry. They drained the circuit again and inspected the second installation. The ring remained seated, without the distortion of the first. Yselle accepted the outcome of that test; the material's long-term performance would only be answered after more work. Pump one at the Mutual had not lost a minute of service to give them that answer.

Elias wrote successful in the second row. This time the word seemed the right size. Beneath it he added the maker's name and the reference to the retained sheet. He returned the pencil without trying to clean away the gray stain from the first test.

Noemi gathered the documents and the last piece of bread. “I'll take this to Gaston. Otherwise he'll think I only came for lunch.”

“You also came to watch our hands,” Marcel said.

“Yours needed company.”

Elias laughed. As he accompanied her to the door, he heard an argument begin about the price of the template, concrete and unmusical. He turned to look at the open pump. Morning light crossed the high window and fell on the casing without distinguishing what had been created from what had been cut in a shop. On the bench, the two rings no longer looked enough alike to confuse. He picked up the folded one and placed it in the sample box with his sheet.

Chapter 70 — A Teacher's Mistake

Martin had brought the wrong wrench, and for almost half an hour that fact allowed him to appear very competent.

The wrench sat well in his hand, did not slip, and reached the nut at an elegant angle. He had taken it from his demonstration box, where every tool had its place outlined on the bottom. Beside pump number two, already drained after the morning's tests, he showed the movement to the two workers still at the workshop. He had not brought a class to interrupt Yselle's work. These were people who would sooner or later need to open a similar assembly, and they had asked to see how to reach the rear fastener.

“The number tells you the opening,” he explained. “Not how much force to use.”

One of the two nodded. The other studied the sheet Martin had set on the crate. It had been corrected often enough to deserve a clean copy. He thought he could make one that afternoon, between a lesson and dinner. Since the security over the shares had been released, even a dinner had recovered a conceivable length.

He loosened the fastener a fraction and turned it back. The machine was stopped, open where necessary, with no water in the circuit. They did not need to repeat the gasket installation. The part tested that morning remained on the bench with its history written beside it.

“Would you like to try?” he asked the nearer worker.

The man put down Martin's wrench and took his own. Its opening was the same, but its handle was straight. He fitted it over the nut, attempted to turn it, and met the side support before completing the movement. He reversed it. The wrench fitted; his hand could not find enough room to work.

Martin looked at the support as though it had moved while he was talking.

“From the other side,” he said.

The man made room for him. Martin bent, took the straight wrench, and tried from the other side. The back of his hand struck the same obstacle. He did not even have the excuse of a pupil misunderstanding. The wrench obeyed the instruction he had written perfectly.

Elias, on the far side of the bench, stopped scraping at an erasure. He did not smile. Martin would have preferred it if he had: a smile would have offered him a line with which to escape.

“Yours has a bent neck,” the other worker said.

At last Martin looked at the tool he had brought. The offset in the handle was so familiar that it had become invisible. It raised his knuckles above the support and permitted a sweep the ordinary wrench did not have.

“Yes,” he said. “I showed you that one and described a different one.”

Yselle, discussing a supply order at the entrance, turned at the sound of the sentence. The morning's maker had already gone. She came to the bench without the anxious expression Martin had learned to look for over the past few days. She seemed annoyed by something small. It pleased him out of all proportion.

“What did you describe about it?”

He showed her the sheet. Reach the fastener with a wrench of the specified size. The size was right. Everything else was inside his head.

Yselle laid the straight wrench beside the offset one. “We can supply this.”

“We can,” the worker said. “But if I take it to be repaired, or someone borrows it for another job, the sheet doesn't know it's missing.”

Martin raised his pencil. He wrote offset wrench and added a small drawing. The man did not seem satisfied. Not hostile: he kept looking at the machine, as though waiting for it to provide the rest of the answer.

“This is what's in my box,” he said, touching the straight wrench. “I saw that other one for the first time this morning.”

Martin lowered the pencil. The exercise was not to find the word that would make his sheet correct. He took the man's toolbox and set it open on the bench. There were wrenches, pliers, a hammer with a darkened handle, and a strip of cloth with two pointed tools wrapped inside. No empty space shaped like his wrench.

“Then let's begin with what you have.”

The worker picked up the pliers and put them down again before touching the nut. “That would damage it.”

“Probably.”

“Not probably. I've already done it to another one.”

Martin nodded. The statement was worth more than an elegant supposition. Elias approached with a blank sheet and asked where they wanted to mark the obstruction. He had already attended a lesson on May thirtieth; since then he had asked other questions, worked, corrected drawings. Martin did not have a boy before him who needed to be taken back to the first line. He yielded the place beside the machine.

Elias drew the support, the nut, and the room needed for the hand. The worker stopped him before he finished: when working at the site, his toolbox would be on the opposite side. To fetch another tool he would have to turn around in the passage. On the sheet, the passage did not exist.

“Don't draw the whole room,” Martin said. “Mark where you're standing.”

The young man added a very ugly outline of two shoes. The second worker said nobody had feet like that. Elias enlarged one shoe out of spite, and for a moment they all stopped concerning themselves with Martin's mistake.

Using the straight wrench required a less brilliant solution than his demonstration. They could remove an auxiliary support with the machine stopped, clear the sweep, and then replace it; that meant extra fasteners, time, and an opportunity to forget something. Yselle did not want it as the usual method. She marked it as an alternative to be completed and checked before they gave it to anyone, rather than a sentence to improvise at the bottom of the page.

“A dedicated wrench costs less than the time we waste,” she said.

“Provided it arrives with the pump,” the man answered.

Martin picked up his own tool and held it over the box. Habit had already set him thinking of lending it. Then he pictured the following morning: himself at school, the empty outline on the bottom, someone at the Mutual looking for a tool that still belonged to him.

“I need this one too,” he said. “There needs to be one assigned to that job.”

Yselle looked at him with one eyebrow slightly raised. “A very bold financial proposal.”

“Wait until you see what I charge for the drawing of the feet.”

The older worker took the offset wrench and tried it. His hand cleared the support without striking it. He completed the slow movement, stopped, and repeated it from the starting point. Then he passed the tool to his companion. Martin watched both: neither used his grip, and the second held farther forward on the handle. It worked that way too. A detail he had not anticipated and did not need to correct.

The second worker glanced toward the door. Martin had already opened his mouth to invite him to repeat the movement when he noticed the man holding his folded jacket over one arm.

“Do you have something else to do?”

“A delivery to receive. I said I'd be back before noon.”

Martin reached for his watch and discovered he had left it in his jacket pocket. The man told him the time without waiting. The demonstration that should have taken a few minutes had eaten into the time allowed for his return.

“I'll do the last trial,” his companion said.

They were not asking permission to be uninterested in the machine. They had another job that would not disappear because Martin had found a good reason to detain them. He promised a copy of the sheet through the workshop, and the younger man left, stepping sideways around the crate they had placed in the passage. Martin moved it beneath the bench. The other man helped with his foot, then returned to the tool without demanding thanks. If Martin wanted to keep his attention, he needed to finish something.

They recorded shape and size on the sheet, with a visible reference to the fastener involved. Yselle added the tool to the equipment list. She did not sign a promise to supply it forever: she arranged for it to be procured for the machine and for its replacement to be identifiable. Martin tried to draw the profile of the neck and produced something resembling a crushed pipe. Elias took the sheet from him with rather vengeful politeness.

“I'll copy the shape.”

“It wasn't a pipe.”

“I didn't say it was.”

Yselle laughed. It had happened before during that week, but this laugh did not immediately have a page to defend. Martin put away his wrench and felt relief with a delay that annoyed him. He wished he had felt it the previous evening, when she told him about the signature. Instead he had asked three times what was still owed, as though the worst must be concealed in the best answer.

The remaining worker tried following the page from the beginning without his commentary. He reached the right point, searched for the drawing of the tool, and found it too far from the sentence. They moved the reference. Then he asked where to put the small fasteners removed for access. That question concerned only the alternative they had not yet approved, which Martin had left in the middle of the main page.

He crossed it out and gave it a separate sheet. It was not ready to be read as an instruction. The sound of the pencil was the only comment needed.

When they finished, the older worker set his own box beside his feet again. He looked at Martin, then at the corrected sheet.

“Now tomorrow I won't have to guess which wrench you had in mind.”

Martin could have turned that into a fine line to repeat at school. He left it in the man's voice. Instead he asked whether the number beside the profile was readable. The man said the second digit looked like a letter; Martin rewrote it larger.

He and Yselle remained before the bench while Elias carried the sheets to the other side to copy them. A call came from outside about loading a wagon, followed by an argument about where to leave it. The workshop was beginning to ask ordinary things of the person in charge of it again.

“Have you eaten?” Yselle asked.

“I was planning to, before my perfect demonstration.”

“Then you must be very hungry.”

He wiped his fingers on a rag and realized she too was waiting for a different answer. Since yesterday they had discussed the agreement as something to inspect from every side. He had not yet asked what she wanted to do once it was no longer urgent to do something else.

“Will you come home at a reasonable hour tonight?”

“That depends on how long you take to define reasonable.”

“Before I burn dinner.”

“So I have to arrive before you.”

Martin smiled, then stopped searching for a joke. “I'd like to have dinner with you. Without the contract on the table.”

Yselle held the rag between two fingers. “There's still work to pay for.”

“I know. I'm not asking for the table to be free.”

She tapped his arm lightly with the rag. It was not peace earned forever; it seemed enough to sit down to. She said she would come back after arranging the tool order. He promised not to choose a recipe requiring three hands.

Before leaving he picked up the demonstration box. The empty outline of the wrench was filled again. He could have kept that simple pleasure and forgotten the rest, but he left the lid open until he had copied the precise mistake into his notebook: correct size, shape unspecified. Beneath it he noted that the next demonstration would begin with the worker's toolbox.

Elias handed him the copy, still damp. In the margin were two shoes that finally looked credible. Martin indicated the larger one.

“Is that me?”

“That's the space you take up.”

Martin put the sheet in his folder, keeping it away from the oil. “Then we've saved paper.”

Chapter 71 — The Person to Be Measured

Ilian had prepared a sentence and forgot it when Mael offered him a seat. On that side of the desk lay his old receipt, folded along the line showing the outstanding balance. Seeing it there, ready, robbed him of the pleasure of laying down his own copy as a surprise.

“First we discuss this,” he said, and the prepared sentence returned uselessly to his head.

Mael moved a brass weight beside the paper. “The account is still open.”

“The work is finished.”

He added no threat. He was forty-nine and carried an apprentice draftsman's folder; he knew the sound of someone who could not afford to walk away as much as he wanted. He placed his own copy beside the other. The same numbers, the same work delivered. No dimension had become wrong while waiting.

Mael ran a finger along the margin, not the figure. “That isn't in dispute.”

“Then tell me when it's being paid.”

The small room received the sounds of the warehouse without revealing all of it. Someone slid a crate past the inner door. Ilian waited for the noise to end, and Mael let him wait. It was a civil gesture he would have liked in a different meeting.

“Not today,” Mael said at last.

Ilian felt something retreat from his face. It was not surprise. He wished it were, because then he could have believed he had not foreseen the answer. He closed the folder clasp too firmly and had to reopen it to free his trapped sleeve.

“The new work doesn't include the old.”

“Nobody is asking you to give it away.”

“You're asking me to continue before I'm paid.”

Mael looked at the two receipts. “I'm asking you to take a question. The answer will determine whether there is a job.”

Ilian drew his own copy toward him. That, at least, he could do. He returned it to the folder and left out only the sheet about the collar. He would not give Mael the satisfaction of watching him leave with all the papers still needing to be distinguished.

The drawing occupied half a page. An outer circle, an inner one, a fastening recess, and a section nobody had dimensioned for him. Nothing about the part required a portable hinge. The smaller request he had taken to Livia remained just that: comparison of the collar, not delivery of a device. But the lines beneath the drawing grew every time someone looked at it.

“You've added available with operator here,” he said.

“It's an option useful to the recipient.”

“It isn't a measurement.”

Mael did not answer at once. Ilian did not talk over his silence; he waited for a real answer, and for a few moments the room seemed to belong to the person who had not yet explained.

“The recipient wants to know whether the comparison can be performed on site,” Mael said.

Ilian drew a light line beside the phrase. It could mean that. It could mean that the person taking measurements must then accompany a specimen elsewhere. A craftsperson had the right to ask where he would work, not merely how wide the part he would measure was.

“Whose site?”

“The owner's.”

“Who must authorize access.”

“Naturally.”

The ease with which Mael agreed did not supply a name. Ilian straightened the sheet. He had learned drawing late enough to retain a memory of what it meant not to know how to read a section. That was ignorance one could remedy. Not knowing who ought to open a door was a different thing.

“For an estimate I'll need the location. For a visit, the name of whoever can authorize it. And I want to know whether they want a check of the collar or an opinion on the whole device.”

Mael took a second sheet. He did not hand it over. He read it on his own side of the desk, then copied two lines onto a slip: dimensional check and compatibility of the ordinary part; no test of the complete device.

It was a better answer than the first. Ilian read it and did not feel any better himself.

“Can you sign that?”

Mael looked at him. “It's a preliminary question.”

“Exactly. It can't commit you as much as an order.”

The signature was brief. Mael set down the pen so it would not roll. Ilian let the slip dry and clipped it to the drawing. He would take those words, not the sheet Mael had read without showing him.

Ilian returned to the incomplete section. The inner line ended before reaching the fastener; it might have been a choice by whoever copied the part, or the outline of a recess. He marked it with the point without widening it.

“Is this supposed to remain empty?”

Mael looked at the spot. “I don't have the sample here.”

“I'm not asking you to guess. I'm telling you why someone will have to see it.”

Working from the drawing cost something different from taking dimensions from an actual example. Ilian had discussed that the first time, yet the small gap survived all the new sheets. It happened often: people who could not draw considered an interrupted line a space into which the technician ought to put his intelligence. Ilian put a question mark there. The difference had cost him at least one order and saved him from paying for another out of his own pocket.

He turned the page and sketched two possible sections. In one the collar rested against a continuous shoulder; in the other the fastening reached a recessed part. Mael indicated the first, then withdrew his finger.

“I think it's this.”

Ilian wrote to be checked beneath both. “Resemblance isn't enough for me.”

Mael's pen remained still. He did not seem offended by the correction, and Ilian was grateful for those few seconds. It was easier to talk about metal. Metal did not imply that asking for what one was owed was a failure of trust.

Outside the window a carter shouted for someone to move an obstruction. Mael stood, looked into the yard, and closed the window. The noise diminished enough for the pen cap to be heard against the desk. Ilian realized he had not yet named the technician.

“I was thinking of Veyne,” he said.

“Is he available?”

“I haven't asked.”

“He works with the workshop.”

“That doesn't mean he works for me.”

Mael sat again. Ilian remembered his question to Livia: was Veyne working again? He had not asked it to prepare a chain of compulsory favors. He had wanted to know whether that young man had kept his trade. Now it was useful to know, and the coincidence bothered him.

“If he can't, ask someone else.”

“If he can't, I'll report that he can't. I don't have a list of people at your disposal.”

The sentence risked sounding braver than necessary. Ilian left it without a friendly addition. He had one person to whom he could take a sufficiently precise question; he did not want to start distributing it all over Lethorne like a request for technical charity.

Mael pushed the now-dry slip toward him. “Then take it to him.”

“I'm not promising a visit.”

“Nor am I arranging one for you.”

Ilian looked at the drawing. The collar still had its simple, almost offensive shape. He could have said the place gave him a bad feeling and refused everything. Instead he had an unpaid receipt, a name on a sheet, and no proof that the new question was unlawful. Demanding that every job arrive free of its commissioner's character would have left him very little to draw.

“A technical answer will require technical access,” he said. “I can carry the question. I'm not selling anyone's access.”

“You've already made that clear.”

Ilian gathered the papers. He would have preferred an argument: it left the body something to do. Mael's polite farewell forced him to leave at the same pace at which he had entered. He did not know whether he had been respected or merely allowed to speak.

In the courtyard he stopped to arrange the slip. The crate he had heard moving contained machine parts secured with battens. There were no secrets to recognize in what he could see, only someone's poor work in leaving a nail projecting at hand height. Ilian pointed it out to a porter. The man bent it over with the hammer at his belt and returned to his load.

The response calmed him more than the interview. Something pointed out had been corrected. He crossed the gateway without knowing what he ought to have pointed out inside the room.

The street was hot, full of the sound of wheels on paving stones. He stopped before a shopwindow to check that the folder had not opened. Inside, the old receipt and the new request were separated by a blank sheet. The separation seemed insufficient. He copied the request onto that sheet, keeping the signed words and leaving out available with operator.

He stopped before crossing that phrase out on the original. He had no right to pretend they had not written it. Beneath the copy he added: clarify required attendance and authorizing person. He would let Veyne see the point that remained open. He did not want to present a paper more reassuring than the one he had received.

At the laboratory a man was closing a high window. Ilian recognized the smell before the doorway: oil, metal, and wood warmed by the afternoon. He did not enter with the urgency of customers who decided for themselves that they deserved an interruption. He waited for the man to step down and asked for Elias.

“He's finishing,” came the answer. “We aren't accepting any more deliveries today.”

“I'm not bringing a delivery.”

It was the same distinction Mael had used with him, and hearing it in his own mouth made him hesitate. He looked at the man whose hand rested on the door. For all he knew, this could be another person destined to work after hours thanks to Ilian's good reasons.

“I can leave a question,” he corrected himself. “If he's finished already, I'll come back when he tells me.”

The man pointed him to a space near the entrance bench. Ilian remained standing. From inside came Elias's voice, then the sound of something being returned to its own box. It was not the sound of someone rushing to rescue a customer. He liked it and found it irritating at the same instant.

While he waited, a gust moved a sheet near the door. Ilian held it down with two fingers. It was a parts list, and the first readable word was gasket. He read no farther. He placed an available weight on it and withdrew his hand, returning his own receipt to the folder before Elias appeared.

“Uren?” the young man said.

Ilian nodded. Elias had a trace of graphite on his fingers and one sleeve rolled farther up than the other. He looked tired and present, without the suspended expression Ilian remembered from the days when the boy's work had been a question of other people's permits.

“You've kept me waiting less than I feared.”

“I didn't know you were waiting for me.”

The answer contained neither an excuse nor discourtesy. Ilian smiled despite himself. It was a good place to begin, if he did not spoil it by asking a kindness before showing the work.

“It's after hours,” he said. “If you'd prefer, I'll leave the sheet and we can discuss it another time.”

Elias looked at the folder. “What is it about?”

Ilian took out the drawing and the signed slip. The receipt stayed inside. The young man did not owe him that money, and he did not want to seat him on Mael's side merely because he had arrived later.

He laid the drawing of the collar on the bench, with the section facing Elias. “An ordinary measurement,” he said. Then he touched the note he had added on the way. “And an attendance requirement they still need to explain.”

Chapter 72 — A Conversation to Propose

Elias had already closed the caliper case. He opened it again out of habit, then stopped before taking out the instrument: there was a drawing on the bench, not a part to measure. Ilian gave him time to notice without explaining his trade to him.

“Is this what you discussed with Livia?” Elias asked.

“The reduced request. There's a signed clarification.”

The slip bore the name Mael Vaur. Elias read it before returning to the collar. Compatibility of the ordinary part, no test of the complete device. The distinction limited the work but offered no surface on which to place the calipers.

“Where is the specimen?”

“They need to specify that.”

“And who's making it available?”

“That too.”

Ilian did not try to improve either answer. Elias showed him the note about the operator's presence. The man opened his folder and placed an earlier copy beside the clarification, where the phrase still appeared in full.

“I asked whether they meant a visit on site. They said yes. I haven't agreed to anything else on your behalf.”

Elias drew the sheet closer to the light. One possible section had an arrow, the other a question. Ilian indicated the gap in the drawing and explained which parts still required checking. That, at least, was a problem Elias could understand without deciding whether to trust the customer.

“To give you a figure, I need to know whether checking a loose part is enough. If I have to reach an installed machine, the time changes.”

“I know.”

“And I have to be able to get there without someone discovering afterward that I shouldn't have been there.”

“That's exactly the question I took back.”

Ilian removed his hat. Its edge had left a line across his hair. Elias might have mistaken weariness for impatience and answered badly; he remembered how Marcel looked at him when he could not stop justifying a question.

“I'm not saying no to the part,” he said. “I'm saying I don't yet have a job I can say yes to.”

Ilian set the hat on a free corner. “I can report that.”

From the laboratory came the noise of cabinets being shut. Elias did not want to detain people finishing work for a commission that did not exist. He collected two sheets to reuse and copied the missing information: location, authorization, condition of the specimen, nature of the required attendance. He left room beneath for the answer.

“Don't come tomorrow morning thinking I've arranged a visit.”

“I haven't arranged one either.”

“If they send me these details, we'll discuss it. Then there needs to be an agreement on payment. Separate from what they owe you.”

Ilian looked at his folder without opening it. “Livia told you.”

“She told me there was an outstanding balance.”

“There still is.”

Elias offered neither to surrender it on his behalf nor to work well enough to make them pay it. He made a second copy of the question and left the first to Ilian. The draftsman folded it far more carefully than the paper deserved.

“At least this says what's missing.”

“It isn't an acceptance.”

“I heard you.”

Ilian smiled, and the small rebuke did Elias good. They had understood one another before the last repetition. When the man left, the bench had acquired a pending question and no new commitment. Elias closed the calipers again. Outside, the air seemed clear enough to preserve part of the day for something he had chosen himself.

Livia was waiting at her work address, with a stack of copies already tied. Edith sat near the window, holding the novel open at a page she was not reading. Elias saw Livia's thumb pressed against the side of her hand and asked whether it hurt.

“Yes. And no, I don't need you to become a doctor.”

Edith closed the volume on her finger. “We were beginning to wonder whether your duke had ordered another twenty pumps.”

“My duke is in Rovenne.”

“Long-distance ownership, then.”

Livia moved the copies to make room for him. Elias described Ilian's visit first, so she would not discover later that another part of his life had passed their door. He showed the question without giving the customer a face he had never seen. Livia read the sentences, then compared the new limits with the note she had kept.

“Better than before,” she said. “Still incomplete.”

“I haven't accepted.”

“Good. Now stop proving it to me and sit down.”

Edith moved a chair with her foot. Beneath her book she had the sheet on which she had already tried the beginning of the other letter. Elias recognized Your Grace and two lines crossed out so vigorously they had nearly pierced the paper.

“Was it too much?” he asked.

“It sounded as though we wanted to ask him for a roof.”

Livia picked up the sheet. “You wrote that we wish to submit a modest initiative to his benevolent attention.”

“I wrote it to show you how it sounded.”

“It sounded like the roof.”

Elias rested his elbows on the table and noticed he had brought a graphite stain. He wiped it with the edge of a scrap. The duke had never asked him to write benevolent attention. He had listened while Elias discussed dimensions and water, and had made three machines appear whose material origin Elias still could not comprehend. He did not know whether that difference made inviting him absurd or more necessary.

“Why would he come?” he asked.

Edith took the scrap away before he spread the stain. “To learn something he doesn't know.”

“We don't know what he doesn't know.”

“We can begin with ourselves,” Livia said. “We haven't spoken to him.”

She was right. Elias had thought about the duke's power, the materials, the healing. The other two had not even had a conversation with him. In a letter full of his own admiration, he would have turned them into appendices to the workshop visit.

“What do you want to ask him?”

Livia drew the blank sheet toward her. “How he chooses which requests to answer. I've spent the afternoon separating a false word from a badly written name. I'd like to understand whether someone who can do a great deal still sees that difference.”

“Write it shorter,” Edith said.

“The question was for us.”

“Then I want to know whether you can learn something without promising an entire life in exchange.”

Edith was no longer smiling. Elias let the sentence remain on the table. She had no Effigy to set beside theirs, yet she had asked questions to which their exchange already owed part of its existence. He did not want to write a plea for her to be given something she had not asked for.

“I'd like him to see a piece of finished work,” he said. “Not just the times when something is missing that he can give.”

“He's seen the pump,” Livia objected.

“I don't mean that.”

He stopped. For a moment he had wanted to set the entire room before him, the table, the places they did not fill yet, and wait for a man so powerful to find them interesting. It was the same haste with which he had written successful too soon. He took the pencil and wrote conversation on a new sheet.

“We aren't inviting him to join. We're inviting him to talk.”

“And somewhere other than our place,” Livia said.

Edith nodded. “Somewhere we can also finish and leave.”

The first version became short almost by necessity. They introduced themselves by their ordinary names and the fields to which each could contribute: surveying and technical work, comparison of texts and documents, observations and questions from a reader without powers. Edith crossed out without powers.

“If he wants to know, I'll tell him. It isn't my job title.”

Elias put down the pencil. She replaced the phrase with ordinary study and practice. It did not seem perfect, but finding a formula into which Edith fitted better was not his decision.

They gave no location for their group, named no objects in their keeping, and included no aliases. They wrote that they sought a conversation about abilities, tools, and knowledge that might be useful to one another. No request for funding. Livia crossed out that qualification: it already sounded like the beginning of a request for funding. She replaced it with a sentence about questions to be agreed before the meeting.

The venue remained. Elias suggested Yselle might propose a suitable outside space, if she wished to help them. Edith pointed out that they would be handing her their problem along with the envelope. Instead they wrote that place and day would be agreed after a favorable reply. No compulsory appointment and no seat assigned beforehand.

Livia dipped the new nib and began the copy. Elias held the corner steady until she told him he would stain it. He withdrew his hand. Her writing came out narrower than in the morning's copies; it was not only fatigue, he thought, but a desire to fit her own voice into a space nobody had already decided for her.

“What if he has an administrator answer?” Edith asked.

Elias looked at the heading. “We'll have an answer.”

“Not to the question we're asking him.”

Livia raised her pen. “If he doesn't want the conversation, we can't make his administrator want it. But I'm not writing three more lines to prevent that.”

Edith nodded and turned her book facedown. Elias abandoned the idea of adding a reference to the pumps that would make ignoring them harder. That was precisely the work for which a payment had been agreed; he could not use it as a little chain around the recipient's wrist.

They read the text aloud. The first time Livia corrected a repetition. The second time Edith said they could have read it without knowing the recipient performed miracles. Elias waited for a third, but it was unnecessary. All three signed.

When they passed him the pen, he hesitated over the space beneath his name. He did not need a grander qualification to fill it. He signed in the handwriting he used on work sheets, then made room for Edith, who had to turn the page to avoid resting her wrist on the fresh ink.

He took the envelope to the workshop before the correspondence left for Rovenne. Yselle had the order book open and a strip of cloth tied around an ink-stained finger. Elias told her who had written and what they were asking. She did not immediately hold out her hand.

“Do you want me to recommend it to him?”

“I'd like you to get it to him.”

Yselle took the envelope. “I can do that. He'll read the rest for himself.”

She did not ask to see their premises or learn things the letter did not contain. She had the envelope added to the business correspondence entrusted to someone already traveling toward Rovenne. The journey would take hours, just as the duke's journey had: Elias could picture the paper in the carriage much more easily than the recipient reading it.

He returned to the other two empty-handed. Edith had reopened the novel; Livia was comparing the final copy against its original. They had not waited motionless for his return.

“Has it gone?” Edith asked.

“It's going with the correspondence.”

“Then now I can read to the end of the page.”

Elias sat down. He had one question to take to a customer and one answer to await from a duke. The two had not resolved each other. Livia passed him the sheet of Ilian's request to put away, then showed him the point in the novel Edith found unbearable. For that evening he could still argue about something nobody had signed.

Chapter 73 — An Unassigned Place

Elias Veyne's name was the second one Edran read on the envelope. The first, written larger, was his own. He expected nothing more from the evening yet, and that arrangement seemed almost a small offense: the recipient occupied so much space that one had to turn the paper to reach the sender.

Maurice had brought it with the business correspondence arriving from Lethorne. The carrier had taken almost four hours and waited at the gate for someone to receive the packet. No alarm, no message shouted from the courtyard. The letter had traveled the road he knew and now lay beside the cup left from dinner.

“Is it from Aunt Yselle?” Celine asked.

“It came through her.”

His sister had the book about levers under her arm. She seemed to have decided to carry it even when she was not reading. She looked at the envelope without trying to crane her neck to read.

“If you reply, can I put my sheet in the same post?”

“For Martin?”

“And for Aunt Yselle.”

Edran said yes before he knew whether he would reply. Celine thanked him with an absent nod and returned to a page marked by two ribbons. He opened the envelope with the paper knife. Inside he found a single sheet and three signatures: Elias Veyne, Livia Varn, Edith Torrel.

They were not asking for a pump. The first line brought relief so swift it seemed ungenerous. He went back to read it slowly.

Three people proposed a conversation about tools, abilities, and knowledge useful to an exchange. Elias named surveying work, Livia comparison of texts and documents, Edith ordinary study and practice. Questions and subjects could be agreed beforehand. For place and day, they were waiting to learn whether he was interested.

There was no moving story to reward, nor a promise that everyone would tell him the truth. The sheet left out enough to make the choice evident. No address to reach that night, no description of a rare object. A conversation, and three people willing to put their names beneath that word.

In the surname Veyne, Edran recognized the young man who had shown him water, weight, dimensions. He did not know the other two. Alexander's memories offered no forgotten encounter to attach them to; the present remained empty where it had not been lived.

“Good news?” Celine asked, still bent over the book.

“I don't know yet.”

“Your face doesn't say bad.”

He realized he was smiling. He folded the sheet along its original crease, then opened it again. He might have given that expression to any request simply because the sender was not asking him to save something. It was not a good reason to invite three strangers into the house.

They had not asked for the house.

He looked toward the door. Maurice was collecting the tray and asked whether a reply should be prepared for the first post in the morning. Edran said he would write it himself. The butler nodded and allowed him the time to do so, a form of service he valued more than tea that evening.

First he looked for Noemi's sheet. He had brought it from Lethorne with the other documents, keeping it separate from the financial ones. The paper contained questions about people who wanted to meet him, about those who did not want to be called forward before others, about pain and impairments not easily described in a line. It was another piece of work yet to begin, not a list of healings already promised.

The three people's invitation did not erase that sheet. He moved it beside the book he would leave for Mirel, so he would not use a pleasant novelty as a reason to forget what had come before.

The doctor came to collect a file shortly afterward. She wore her working dress and had already pinned up her hair to go out. Edran indicated the questions from the Mutual.

“Noemi would like to arrange some meetings. I haven't given her a day.”

Mirel looked at the sheet before looking at him. “Do you want me to write to her?”

“I'd like to know what you need before speaking with the people. You can note it here. Then I'll answer her.”

She read the first question. She did not ask him to demonstrate again what he had done for the cut. Instead she asked whether Noemi knew she might receive a different answer for each person.

“Yes.”

“Then let's begin with names and how they want to be contacted. This sheet doesn't contain all of them.”

Edran left her the paper. He did not need to pretend to be powerless to notice that he did not know those people. The memory of tissue joined beneath his hand was sharp, real. Equally real was the distance between a question written by Noemi and a person choosing to offer him their body.

Mirel picked up the file she had come for and told him when she would leave her notes. He did not assign her Elias's letter. She had not become the keeper of all his decisions merely because she could ask a precise question.

After she left, he carried the invitation into the study. The light was stronger there and the desk less occupied. The chair reserved for visitors stood on the other side of the desk; from that place, anyone entering had to speak toward the portrait behind the duke. Edran sat for a moment on the side opposite his own.

The surface seemed wider. From there he would have had to put his hands far enough forward for the man behind the desk to see they concealed nothing. Alexander's memories contained conversations held at that table, clothes, titles, and the sound of the door after a dismissal. Older memory carried other tables, other ways of forcing a body to appear harmless.

He rose without completing the comparison. That chair was not a chain, and destroying it would not improve the letter. He would not use the study for the first meeting.

Teren knocked with the final security update. The man in charge had a problem with an entrance to the courtyard, already watched, and a solution to carry out in the morning without changing the other shifts. Edran listened to the end. Only then did he show him the invitation.

“Three people want to speak to me outside this house.”

Teren read the names, then the place left blank. “Have you answered already?”

“Not yet.”

“Veyne is the man from the workshop?”

“Yes.”

“The other two?”

“I don't know them.”

Teren did not fill the silence with suspicion. He asked whether Yselle had added a recommendation; Edran showed him the envelope, which bore only the address. His aunt had delivered the letter. The rest was not hers.

“Once there's a place and a day, I can tell you how to get there and what is needed,” the security chief said. “There isn't a route to prepare yet.”

“I don't want to turn the conversation into an official visit.”

“I can work without announcing you to an entire street.”

He liked the answer more than necessary. Part of him would have liked to test everyone around him, force them to reveal the point where care became possession. Teren offered only the work for which he was responsible. Edran handed him the invitation again and waited for him to finish reading the last line.

“I'll reply that I'm interested,” he said. “The place will be agreed. Nobody comes here tomorrow as a result of this letter.”

Teren nodded. He spoke once more about the courtyard entrance and withdrew carrying his own instructions, not the duke's pen.

Edran remained before the blank page. The first formula that occurred to him promised availability in a tone so generous it erased the limits he wished to leave to choice. He discarded it. The second sounded like a summons. Merely changing the order of two sentences turned three people who had invited him into people called to present themselves.

On the first attempt he had written Dear Mr. Veyne. He stared at the formula. It would have been easy to ask him to convey his thanks to the other two, leave him the work of representing them, and continue knowing only the man he had already met. But the letter was not signed by Elias and two people accompanying him. One signature had a broad stroke, the other occupied little space; he did not even know which woman had held the pen for the text.

He turned the sheet and tried writing all the names beneath a single salutation. He did not know whether Livia or Edith was married, and he did not want to choose a title by guessing at a life. It seemed ridiculous to be stopped there after succeeding in creating three complete machines. The pen's point dried while he hesitated. He had to clean it, dip it again, and accept the blot left on the scrap.

In the end he used the names as they had written them. The reply did not have to resemble the ones a recipient was supposed to desire from a duke. It was enough to recognize it tomorrow without feeling the need to explain it.

He took a smaller sheet.

He wrote the three names, not Elias's alone. He thanked them for the proposal and said he was interested in a conversation on the subjects indicated. He asked to receive the questions they preferred to address first. For place and day they would find a solution suitable to everyone through Yselle's address.

He reread the sentence about the conversation. He had promised neither teaching, money, protection, nor membership. He did not want to add a list of everything he was not offering. It would sound as though he accused the senders of requesting things they had not written.

Instead he added: In turn, I would welcome the chance to ask some questions about your work. It was the missing part, and the hardest to leave on paper without reducing it to politeness. He did not merely want to examine useful people. He wanted to enter a conversation in which one of his questions could receive an answer he did not know.

Celine passed outside the study. She stopped with her own sheet already folded and set it on the edge, without reading the open page.

“You've decided to reply.”

“Yes.”

“Then don't forget mine.”

He looked at the smaller envelope. Celine had rewritten Martin's name after getting one letter wrong and left the correction visible. Edran put it beside his own. His sister did not ask where he was going, perhaps because he did not know either. She said good night and continued toward the stairs.

He signed Alexander Valcienne. The pen moved without hesitation, in the hand he knew from the household documents. Inside the letter there was no older name to explain, no power to display as a credential.

When Maurice returned, the sheet was dry. Edran handed over the reply for Yselle, asking her to pass it to the three signatories, and Celine's separate letter for their aunt and Martin. It would leave with the first post on the tenth. He did not demand that the road shorten because he had made a decision.

He remained in the study a moment longer. The visitor's chair faced the portrait, exactly where it had been. He had not moved it. But the place where he would listen to these people was no longer decided by the furniture, the title, or the fear of someone who did not know them.

Before extinguishing the lamp, he turned the copy of the reply toward him. Three names at the top, his own at the bottom. No seat occupied among them. For the first time in days, the thing he awaited was not a report of something that had already happened.

Chapter 74 — Service Water

The first man Elias met at the Mutual did not want to see a pump. He wanted to get through with a basket of laundry and considered the surveyor, standing in the worst part of the doorway, to have chosen a rather useless trade.

“Excuse me.”

Elias moved aside. The man passed without recognizing him and asked someone whether the shelf near the water was finally clear. A voice farther in answered that he could leave the basket, but not there. It was an argument about where things belonged, identical to a hundred others. The pump worked behind the wall, and nobody had interrupted a sentence to listen to it.

Elias had imagined arriving with the finished instructions and finding a little anticipation. Not a ceremony; enough, though, to show that this morning completed a job. He had to wait for a second basket to pass before he could enter.

Gaston greeted him by raising a hand from the log. “Have you brought the new page?”

“And the replacement reference.”

“Then come this way. We're not taking the old one down until we've put up the other.”

The board bearing the first instructions still hung beside the entrance. The paper had a bent corner, a finger stain, and a correction not in Elias's handwriting. He followed the mark. Someone had written where the drain container actually stood instead of where they had planned to keep it.

It was not an elegant correction. It was accurate.

The new page retained the directions they had been using since June seventh and added the results of the test with the local replacement. The part had not been tested by dismantling the machine in service. Elias showed Gaston the number two recorded on the sheet; the man checked the number one on the casing, as though determined to prevent the two tests from becoming confused on paper too.

“Does the part fit here the same way?”

“The seat is the same. This is the drawing of the seat, and this is the material reference. The test was done on the workshop bench.”

Gaston followed the line with a finger, then asked for the wrench specified in the equipment list. It had arrived in an ordinary case along with the sheets. He took it out, compared it with the profile, and returned it to the case beside the machine's tools. Nobody needed to open the cover to prove they knew how.

Water rose through the service section. At the tank, a worker filled the next container and slid it toward a woman, who lifted it from a comfortable height. She had not climbed the stairs carrying that weight. Elias looked at the floor, expecting to find a trace where the jet still returned to his memory. The floor was dirty with work, not flooded.

Noemi arrived with a folder and a handkerchief tucked into her cuff. “Have you found something to reopen already?”

“Not yet.”

“At least wait until the handover is finished.”

She stopped beside Gaston and read the page's heading. She had trimmed the blank edge of the old board because a passing sleeve kept catching on it. Elias noticed only when he laid the larger sheet beside it.

“The new one is too wide,” she said.

“I can fold it.”

“Not over the name of the replacement.”

Gaston found a wider support among two boards saved for another job. He leaned it against the wall and showed where it would need shortening. The worker at the tank finished filling and came to fetch it. He did not look to Elias for confirmation. He had a saw, a bench, and enough annoyance with the old board to want the problem fixed at once.

Meanwhile Elias went over the final scheduled check with Gaston. They were not looking for an accident. The level was the one required for service, the return followed the repaired route, the supports remained stable. The point where the old pipe had failed was visible. The repair had not erased the other joints, but neither had every sound in the room become a warning.

A man pushed a crate into the corridor and left it against the access guard. Before Elias could open his mouth, the tank worker called him back.

“I can't get to the tools there.”

“It's only for a moment.”

“The moment fits on the other side too.”

He indicated a free space. The man moved the crate with a grumble, without asking who had given him that authority. Elias followed the movement and felt an intervention he was already preparing taken away. Then Gaston put the log before him and asked him to check a line. He did not have to invent something else to do.

The line recorded two level readings taken before and after filling the containers the previous day. The times were noted, the worker's signature readable. Elias asked whether they had noticed differences in the sound and received a concrete description of the two moments. There had been no miracle of a machine that prevented every doubt: someone had listened while it worked and written down what they saw, without having to interrupt service.

Noemi then pointed to two columns on the handover sheet. “This one is for what we're receiving today. Is this one about subsequent service?”

“The contact and work to be agreed. It doesn't mean every future intervention is already included.”

“I'm asking so nobody writes repair instead of request. We lost a week on a prosthesis order over a word like that.”

She corrected the heading in larger handwriting than his. She did not ask for a promise that would commit Yselle without her being there. She wanted to know which door to knock on if the work returned.

The new board arrived smelling of fresh-cut wood. The worker had rounded the corner that projected into the passage. Gaston held it steady, Noemi chose the height, and Elias attached the page. The material sample remained protected in a separate envelope, its reference readable, rather than hung above the tank to get wet.

They stood before the work a moment. Elias searched for a sentence resembling the right conclusion and found none that Noemi would not have been able to tease him about. She saved him by asking him to move because the woman with the container was coming back.

The woman set it down, looked at the water, and said they had begun washing earlier than usual. She knew nothing about dimensions or securities. She was angry because the laundry table was not ready yet, and glad she could be angry with a bucket already full.

Elias asked how long it had taken them before. She told him to try making two trips with an empty container, then imagine it full. He did not need to turn the answer into a number to believe her.

The woman lifted a corner from the basket. It had caught in the handle beneath the rest of the laundry; if she pulled, she would overturn the container just filled. She asked Elias to hold it steady. He slipped two fingers beneath the wood and left both her hands free. The knot of fabric came out slowly, wetting his cuff.

“This wasn't in the handover,” she said.

“I can add it to the sheet.”

“Don't. Then tomorrow they'll think you're coming.”

She showed him the space beneath the bench where they would put the baskets once it was cleared of the containers previously used to carry water upstairs. She had already moved two. Two paler rectangles remained on the floor, along with a button nobody had bent to collect. The woman picked it up and put it in her apron pocket.

Elias released the handle once she had arranged the cloth. He had thought finished work was recognizable by a motionless machine and a sheet without corrections. That morning nothing was motionless: laundry, baskets, people, even the objects that had lost their usefulness were finding a place. The woman asked him not to leave his folder on the shelf she had just cleared. He picked it up without explaining that he would not have kept it there long.

In a dry corner, on a different shelf, the drinking jug remained. Nobody filled it from the service tank. The distinction belonged to the room's habits; the new machine had not changed the water into something it was not.

Noemi took him to the office table for the signatures. The room had a window open to the courtyard, and a draft immediately tried to carry off the first sheet. Elias set the weight too far from the corner; Noemi moved it before handing him the pen.

“You know they'll have to find you another job now?”

“Marcel is probably thinking about it already.”

“I meant the pump. We can't keep having you check the same screw to keep you here.”

He laughed, then looked at the form. He had found the service already running. The need for which they had measured, argued, and transported machines had truly been met. It might seem small beside what the duke had made appear. On the sheet, though, that small thing was enough to be handed over.

He signed for the check performed. Noemi signed for receipt of the sheets and equipment, and Gaston added his confirmation. Nobody turned those signatures into certification of drinking water or the entire city. The room, the service, and the machine had boundaries Elias knew.

While the ink dried, a man appeared in the doorway. He did not enter. He asked Noemi whether she had news about the visits they had discussed.

“Not a date yet,” she answered. “If you want, when I have one I'll have you called on your own. You don't have to come with the others.”

The man nodded, looked at Elias, and seemed about to add a question. Noemi waited. In the end he merely said to leave a message at the address she knew, then went away.

“He didn't want to ask in front of me,” Elias said.

“Or he didn't want to ask today.”

Noemi picked up the sheet again and showed him where to put his provisional copy. On her right forearm, where the old fastening had cut her, there was no mark where Elias still expected one. The healing had remained true even when nobody discussed it.

“Have you written to the duke already?”

“I gave him the questions before he left. Now I'm waiting for him to tell me how to proceed. Meanwhile I can ask people whether they want to be there.”

She did not say it like someone awaiting permission to return to life. She still had a correspondence box to open, two people to receive, and a new board to stop someone covering with an apron. Elias let her return to those things without offering to bring an answer he did not have.
