Passing the machine again, he heard the worker explain to his colleague where to find the offset wrench. The man opened the case and showed him. Their voices continued over the pump's noise: a brief argument about who had put it back the wrong way around. It had become theirs enough to be disordered by hands that did not belong to the designer.

At the workshop Yselle received the original handover record and read the signatures before asking about the level. Elias told her about the moved crate, the board, and the service he had found already running. She checked Noemi's additions and placed the sheet in the project folder.

“I'll keep your final copy here,” she said. “Come and collect it when it's back from comparison.”

Elias nodded. That copy would belong to him without his having to remain attached to the machine to deserve it. He set the now-lighter folder on the bench and realized he had brought nothing away to repair. For once, that was exactly what he was supposed to deliver.

Chapter 75 — The Third Name

Livia had written a clear explanation, and Edith had circled the word at which she stopped understanding anything.

It was not even a difficult word. Reference. Livia used it in documents, notes, and sentences explaining why a comparison was useful. In the notebook she had written: compare against the reference. Edith had drawn a circle and, beneath it, a question: Which one?

The irritating thing was that the explanation was hers, not some distracted clerk's work she could complain about. The previous evening she had promised Edith a page about the most common abbreviations in cross-references. She wanted her to read three lines of their work without having to ask her every time. Now three of her lines produced a new question.

She had finished the morning's first copies, and the work address was still quiet enough for her to open the notebook. Outside, two men argued about who had left a parcel near the step. Neither thought it necessary to lower his voice out of respect for the clarity of her prose.

Edith arrived with the original page, a small parcel, and a book beneath her arm. She put down only the book. “I haven't crossed anything out.”

“I saw. You chose a color you can see from a street away.”

“The other one ran out.”

Livia took the pencil and wrote the document's name beside the circle. Edith stopped her with a finger.

“You know it this time. What if I get a different sheet?”

Livia searched her own note for the answer, as though the circle might have concealed a line already there. Then she turned the page. She had constructed an explanation that worked only for the example she had been thinking about. Beneath the heading Abbreviations she added Where to find the title in full.

Edith sat without asking whether it was a bad question. She showed her a cross-reference in the book she had brought: two letters and a number. Livia immediately said the second letter indicated a volume. When they opened the page referred to, they discovered it meant a section.

“Don't write that down,” Livia said.

“I haven't got the pencil yet.”

“Worse. You'll remember.”

She laughed despite herself and took the book. She could have preserved the shape of those letters exactly, but that would not oblige the author to use them as she did. The clarification was in a note at the beginning, far from the point where they had stopped. They read it and changed the example. Livia wrote a line admitting two possible meanings instead of choosing the more usual one.

Edith offered no money for the correction. Beside Livia's page she placed a sheet containing the words that had tripped her up and a rewritten sentence for each. In exchange she asked to keep the corrected copy of the abbreviations. Livia accepted. An Effigy had not written the bad page in her place, and it would not prevent an ordinary reader from improving it.

“Shall we let Elias read this too?” Edith asked.

“Especially him. He might think a reference is a place he can reach with his hands.”

Edith smiled, then at last pushed the parcel between them. Inside were two shapes of dark fabric, cut to cover the upper face, with ribbons not yet attached. Livia lifted them by a corner.

“Are they for a play?”

“They were for a play. These are copies of the pattern. I wanted to find out how uncomfortable they were before making something serious of it.”

They were cloth, thread, and a proposal. Edith held one before her eyes without tying it. The edge touched her lashes; she had to lower it, leaving her nose half covered.

“Very,” Livia concluded.

“What if I cut more here?”

She placed the fabric in Livia's hand. Livia tried it. The world shrank to two openings that did not quite match the distance between her eyes. She could see Edith or the edge of the table, but passing from one to the other required moving her head. She did, and the loose ribbon slipped across her cheek.

Edith opened the book before her and asked her to read a line. Livia began in her own voice, stopped at a word hidden by the edge, and turned her head to find its ending. The fabric pressed against her cheek. She might learn to hold the book higher, but during a conversation someone could set a page in a different place.

“The margin won't fit,” she said.

“The margin fits. You're looking through the wrong hole.”

“If I enlarge one, you can see half my face.”

Edith held one of the shapes to the light and turned it. The pattern had been copied for an actor looking toward an audience, not for people sitting around a table. Livia indicated the seam point that could pull toward the eye. They marked it with chalk without pretending they had found protection. The face might become less readable; the voice that had just complained about the margin was the same one Livia had used to collect payment for a copy the day before. Edith recognized it by imitating her sentence, and Livia surprised herself by laughing while she still held the cloth over her nose.

Someone knocked on the doorframe from the corridor. Livia removed the shape too quickly and shoved it beneath the notebook. It was only someone coming to collect the finished copies. They checked the number of sheets, asked the price already agreed, and went away without noticing the small drama they had missed.

Edith watched the fabric protruding from the cover. “Very discreet.”

“I didn't say it was a good idea.”

“I didn't say it had worked.”

Livia pulled it out. Her heart was beating harder over a mask to be sewn than over a letter sent to a duke. She waited for it to settle. She laid the cloth flat without pretending nobody could recognize her hand as she arranged it.

“He'll have our names,” she said.

“That doesn't mean everyone has to.”

“No. But it means we can't present ourselves as three people he's never seen and expect him not to ask questions.”

Edith nodded. She had already marked the point to enlarge without cutting it. If they used coverings in a future conversation, they needed to be able to look at one another and read a page, not merely make an impression entering. They had not even agreed on a place yet. Livia preferred not to spend the morning dressing a meeting nobody had accepted.

“Let's wait for the answer before sewing three,” she said.

“That's why there are two.”

The count silenced her. Edith had not asked her to guess her problem: she had set it on the table, where Livia could see it without immediately managing to give it the right name.

Elias arrived late in the morning, his folder emptier than when he had passed the previous day. Livia asked whether he had lost a job. He answered that he had handed one over and set his hand on the table as though to show he brought no parts to repair.

“The Mutual received the sheets. They're keeping my final copy at the workshop.”

“And our letter?”

“I don't have an answer yet. I'll stop at Yselle's when she's finished with the handover copies.”

The dark shape caught his eye. “What's that?”

“A proposal that doesn't see very well yet,” Edith said.

Elias lifted it. He held it too high, then too low. Livia realized he was trying to solve the problem of the cloth because it was easier than the problem the cloth was supposed to solve. She took the ribbon from his fingers before he could try knotting it behind his head.

“If we choose coverings, we choose them for a precise reason. Not to pretend the letter wasn't signed.”

“We can ask when he replies,” he said. “Tell him we'd rather avoid other names in front of outsiders.”

“We've already given him ours.”

“We can't take those back. The others aren't in the letter.”

Livia lowered her eyes to her notebook. Footnote did not appear on the cover; she had left it inside, beside notes about the exchange. Northbound belonged to Elias. Neither could become a second face capable of erasing the rest of a life. They were names chosen for a use, and they had already begun to seem firmer than they were.

Edith smoothed the second shape. “Don't choose one for me while you discuss the other two.”

“We weren't doing that,” Elias said.

Livia touched his arm before he added an explanation. Edith had not accused anyone of something already done. She had said where she did not want to end up.

“You don't have one yet,” Livia said. “It can stay that way.”

“I'd like it to remain something I choose. Not the empty place to fill so the count comes out right.”

Elias put down the shape. “All right.”

He did not ask for a date by which she should think about it. Livia laid the two coverings together and returned them to the parcel; for now there was no third thing to add. Edith's name was already beneath the letter, beside theirs. For the duke, nobody had signed from a more secret position than anyone else.

Something less conspicuous remained to prepare. Livia reopened the notebook and asked what questions they would want to address if a yes arrived. Elias began with the limits of creation, then stopped to look at Edith. She did not lend him a question to complete the round.

“I said mine yesterday. But I don't want him answering before he knows what I'm trying to learn.”

She showed him the book with the mistaken cross-reference and the page corrected with Livia. Elias read the explanation and found a sentence clear that Edith had circled.

“You already knew the document,” she observed.

He went back, searched for the title, and found the added note. Livia saw comprehension change the way he held the sheet. She did not need to make them argue to prove the corrected note had value. She let them finish comparing and stood to close the window against the noise of a wagon.

When she returned, Elias had added an arrow at the useful point and Edith was copying the line. They passed the pencil without counting the favor. Livia thought she would rather show the duke that table at that precise moment than a row of successful masks. It was a preference, not a reason to give him an address they had not decided to share.

“Tonight we'll meet at Room Six,” she said. “After work. If the answer arrives, we'll read it together.”

“And if it doesn't?” Elias asked.

Edith pointed to the notebook. “We'll make you read the whole reference.”

Livia laughed. They agreed to use the ordinary entrance and wait for no one who had not been invited. Three people that evening, whatever the answer. The future meeting with Alexander, if it happened, would have a different venue and terms to discuss. Nobody was holding open a door for him across the city.

Elias picked up his folder. Edith collected the book but left Livia her corrections. For a moment Livia tried to return the sheet out of habit, then remembered the exchange: that page was hers now, and the copy of the abbreviations belonged to the other woman.

Left alone, she checked the afternoon's copies and moved the parcel of shapes into a drawer. The notebook did not promise that nobody would discover who she was. It contained a circled mistake, a correction, and two working names that did not exhaust the people. She left the next line free without writing the third.

Chapter 76 — The Morning News

Varek took the newspaper out of Sybille’s hands before she had finished reading.

“That one’s sold.”

“I haven’t dirtied it.”

“You’ve opened it.”

The distributor ran two fingers down the fold, closed the sheet and tucked it under the arm of the waiting man. She accepted a coin, found the change without looking and called to a boy tying a bundle of copies too tightly. Sybille stepped back to let the customer pass. It was noon, and the sun had brought the queue to the wrong side of the stall.

Varek did not move into the shade. She was sixty-one, with a basket to watch and a street corner somebody else would occupy before she finished complaining about the heat.

“The top one,” she told the boy. “You’ve bent the pages.”

He loosened the string. A copy slipped free and Sybille stopped it with her shoe without stepping on it. She bent to pick it up.

“I’ll buy this one.”

“You will now.”

She paid. Varek put the coin into a different pocket and finished checking the bundle. Her hands were dry, two nails broken so short that merely looking at them made Sybille uncomfortable. Once she had seen this woman veil a luminous signal in shadow, leaving the lamp alight. Now she watched her fight a piece of string.

“We weren’t supposed to meet here,” she said quietly.

“You chose to come. I’m working.”

The boy shouldered the bundle and asked whether he ought to stop at the printing house before returning. Varek answered with three addresses and a reprimand about some wrong change. He left without looking at Sybille. A good sign, or merely a boy with other things to do.

The stall remained between them. Varek pushed over a copy of an old edition, open at an advertisement for a house.

Sybille did not touch it.

“That isn’t what I’m looking for.”

“I’m not offering it to you. Put your paper on it. Cover the lines.”

The sheet underneath carried a list of business addresses. None was a private home; Sybille recognized a paper merchant and the entrance used for workshop deliveries. Varek had written each name beneath a printed heading, leaving enough space between the letters to make them look like part of an account.

“Ilian asked about the surveyor,” she said.

“I know.”

“He asked. He isn’t bringing him along.”

“I know that too.”

The distributor stopped her finger on a line. “Then explain it to whoever sent you. I’ve been asked when he’ll arrive. There is no arrival. There’s a request for an inspection after hours and somebody who hasn’t answered yet.”

Sybille followed the queue reflected in the neighboring shop window. A woman carried an empty basket; a man was looking for the sales page without wanting to buy the paper. Nobody was listening closely enough to justify an escape. She disliked realizing that she was already choosing where to disappear.

“Mael doesn’t want another supposition.”

“Neither do I. That’s why I’m not selling him one.”

Varek slipped the sheet out from beneath the newspaper, folded it in the right place and put it away. The words lasted less time than the movements required to serve another customer. He wanted a piece of news he had heard shouted at the bridge. Varek showed him the page number and told him that headline belonged to yesterday. The man argued that it ought to cost less, then.

“You should have read it yesterday too,” she replied.

Sybille hid her smile behind the copy she had bought.

Once they were alone again, Varek leaned forward a little. “How often do you think I can ask about the same deliveries? Once, I’m a woman who knows the street. The third time, I’m a woman people remember.”

“Did they answer?”

“They asked if I wanted work for a nephew.”

“And you?”

“I told them if I had a capable nephew, I’d make him work here.”

A wheel struck the curb. Varek pulled the basket toward her before a cart could clip it; Sybille caught the other handle. For a moment they supported the weight of the newspapers together. The driver shouted something intended as an apology. The distributor answered loudly enough to make two people turn.

She did not use Silence. She wanted the driver to know he had heard her.

With the basket back in place, she checked the bundle beneath the stall. She drew out a folded sheet and opened it on her own side only.

“This has been paid,” she said.

Sybille recognized the creditor’s signature, not the amount. A stroke crossed the sum owed; below, somebody had written that nothing remained to collect on that account. Varek rubbed the margin with her thumb, checking that the ink would not come off.

“Then you’ve received something.”

“I’ve stopped owing something. I prefer the distinction.”

She returned the sheet to an inside pocket, close to her chest. The calmness of her voice did not keep Sybille from remembering how still that hand had been. Varek would not have shown the receipt unless she needed to believe in the result.

“Would you rather have money next time?”

“I’d rather choose what to pay. But you don’t decide that.”

Sybille looked beyond the stall. Her own house was still for sale; Varek’s settled account already had a paper to keep. She felt a brief, unpleasant envy, then thought of Ilian returning to argue about work he had finished. She did not know which of the three was closest to obtaining what they had been promised.

A group of boys ran up and stopped in front of the illustrated sheets. Varek let them look until the first put a wet finger on the paper. She moved it away with the back of her hand without interrupting the argument about price. One of the smallest asked whether the duke had really made a factory appear.

“Three pumps,” she said. “They’ll be selling the factory tomorrow.”

The boys laughed. The one who had asked stayed serious. Varek showed him the available article, found a copy with a torn edge and let him look while she served the others. Sybille waited until they were far enough away.

“Don’t buy rumors about the duke.”

“I’m not buying them.”

“You’re collecting them.”

“This is my street. I hear them even when you people aren’t paying me.”

Sybille had no answer to that plural. Mael could distinguish permissible news from inconvenient news inside a closed room; at the stall, a child had just asked aloud precisely what he no longer wanted to ask.

Varek pointed to a sheet at the bottom of the basket. “This one says the machine has stopped.”

“Which machine?”

“Exactly. It might be a workshop test. It might be a pump that hasn’t worked in years. Whoever tells the story can’t distinguish the Mutual from the street behind it. I’m not going to ask an injured person whether the duke has come back to fix the water.”

Sybille opened her purchased paper and found the workshop news. Nothing told her where Elias would be that evening. A nobleman’s name filled enough space to make the names of those carrying tools seem tiny.

“We need to know who answers Ilian,” she said.

“When they answer.”

“That too.”

Varek looked up. “It isn’t the same request.”

It was irritating because it was true. Following somebody who delivered a letter did not mean knowing the text inside. Seeing a technician enter a workshop did not prove he had accepted a job. Sybille had charged men for smaller distinctions they could not afford; now hers were coming back with a newspaper.

“The business address,” she said. “Not the house.”

Varek went still for an instant. “Whose house?”

“Anybody’s.”

The woman glanced at the pocket where Sybille kept the folded particulars. She could not see what paper it was; what showed was Sybille’s instinctive gesture to protect it from the basket’s edge. Sybille moved her hand.

“This assignment doesn’t include finding where people sleep.”

“Write that down.”

The reply came at once. Varek picked up a pencil stub and offered it wooden end first.

Sybille looked at the blank space on her newspaper. Mael could easily ignore the sentence if he wanted to; without it, though, there would not even be a sentence for him to lie against. She wrote business addresses, then added delivery of the reply and stopped. She had almost written the name of somebody to watch. She did not yet know who would carry the message.

Varek read without taking the sheet.

“I can do that while I deliver. If I have to leave my route, we discuss it first.”

“Don’t send the boy.”

“The boy carries newspapers. I haven’t asked you how to use him.”

Sybille felt the rebuke and let it arrive whole. Smiling to pretend she had been doing a kindness would not help. The boy was already coming back along the street, without his bundle, rattling coins in a box. Varek made him count out the change in front of her.

One coin was missing.

He protested, searched his pocket and found two. One belonged to him. She made him explain which, and he blushed to his ears before showing her the mark on its edge. Sybille waited. There was nothing quick about a person who knew her own stall.

Once the account balanced, Varek sent him to eat and closed the box.

A man stopped to ask for a paper on credit. His fingers were gray with dust and he carried a rolled apron beneath his arm. Varek opened a small notebook, found his name and showed him a line.

“This is yesterday’s.”

“They pay me tonight.”

She passed him the copy. He said he would bring both coins before she closed and set off almost at a run. Sybille watched the notebook close. That debt had required no different service, no address or invitation. It had a name and the price printed on the newspaper.

“Will he come back?” she asked.

“Usually.”

“And if he doesn’t?”

“Tomorrow I remember him. I don’t sell his life to recover two sheets.”

Varek slipped the notebook beneath the basket, leaving the joke where Sybille could not pretend she had failed to hear it.

“Does Mael want the updated address too?” she asked.

“The intermediate depot?”

“I saw the old yard. The pears really are there.”

Sybille nodded. Crossing out one destination did not provide another. “I need to see the access first. If a crate won’t fit, it won’t get narrower because the buyer’s in a hurry.”

“Will it only be a crate?”

The noise of the street went on between them. Sybille thought of the empty bench, the two chairs, Mael’s question about what a technician might know how to measure. She wished Varek’s question had remained a commercial one. She did not give herself that comfort.

“There’s nothing to transport yet.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Sybille raised the paid copy. “Send the answer to the usual contact.”

Varek took back the pencil she had forgotten to return. She did not ask about the crate again. She watched her leave with an expression Sybille preferred not to try interpreting.

At the corner, sunlight ended beneath the portico. She could have concealed her step and emerged on the other side without offering the passersby a face. She kept walking, the paper beneath her arm and her pocket clear of the wall.

She had to take Mael a question still unanswered. It was accurate information. That did not make it feel innocent.

Chapter 77 — Work Paid For

Marcel made him count his payment before discussing the pump.

Elias arranged the coins on the sheet. Six crowns. The second had a nicked edge; he turned it over without knowing why and put it with the others.

“That’s everything,” he said.

The master pushed the receipt toward him. Elias signed where indicated. The work had a completion date, his name and an amount he could actually put in his pocket this time. He did not have to wait for somebody to decide whether his contribution had been grateful enough.

“Has Yselle confirmed the handover?”

“This morning. Noemi too.” Marcel collected the receipt. “Did you leave the instructions with the right copy?”

“The dry one, in the cover.”

“Then stop looking for another reason not to take the money.”

Elias wrapped the coins. Their little weight pulled at his jacket pocket, giving him a pleasure so precise that he smiled.

On the bench, the old regulator waited with its cover off. Marcel had arranged the springs in a box and found something he had not wanted to show him before settling the account: a thin wire caught beneath a grip. Elias recognized one of the repairs left unfinished during their visits to the Mutual.

“This isn’t included in the six crowns,” the master said.

“I was hoping not.”

“It’s included in your ordinary work.”

Elias sighed and removed his jacket. He put it well away from the oil, pocket uppermost. Marcel saw and said nothing.

The wire came free on the second attempt. Elias sought the source of the play, compared the seat with the neighboring component and understood where the edge had deformed. A small correction would suffice. Exactly the sort of work that, a few weeks earlier, would have seemed something to finish before he could begin his life.

Now it felt good to have an answer in his hands.

Marcel checked the result. The regulator moved again without catching. Elias closed it and realized he still had a question.

“I’d like to prepare for the second stage.”

The master did not look up at once. He finished putting the spare spring in its place, closed the box and only then looked at him.

“Since when?”

“Since before the Mutual.”

“I asked since when, not what you’re defending yourself against.”

Elias rubbed his thumb against the edge of the bench. “Since we tested the Hinge. First I wanted to understand why it worked. Then I thought about what stays mine when I don’t have that object.”

Marcel let him finish. Upstairs, a window struck as it opened; somebody propped it with an object that rolled away immediately afterward. They both looked up, waited for a complaint and returned to the bench.

“You want to travel through,” the master said.

“I want to learn to open a passage. Not just send a key.”

Spoken aloud, the desire seemed larger than when he had kept it among the things he would do someday. He had watched Marcel walk through the artifact’s opening. He had not watched a future version of himself emerge by right of apprenticeship.

“And if I told you to wait?”

“I’d want to know what I was waiting for.”

The answer brought an expression to the master’s face that Elias seldom saw: not approval, but interest unburdened by a fault to repair.

Marcel cleared part of the bench. He made two small marks, one beside the tray and the other near the spring box. Then he picked up a nut and laid it in his palm.

“Look here.”

The air above the first mark acquired an edge.

Elias bent closer before deciding whether it was wise. There was no frame: a portion of the bench seemed to look at the world from a different angle. Beyond its margin he saw the spring box much too close. Raising his head, he saw it again where it belonged.

Two views. One box.

Marcel introduced the nut into the opening. The metal disappeared from his palm and fell from the other end into the tray. Elias heard a single impact. The distance it had traveled was not the one his eyes still assigned to the bench.

Both openings closed. The master lowered his hand and rubbed his fingers.

“Not the Hinge,” Elias said.

“No.”

The derivative remained safely stored elsewhere. One of its initial openings was still available; that nut had not consumed it. Elias watched the space become uniform again and tried to separate what he had seen from the exercise he already knew.

“I could have transferred the nut.”

“With Small Transfer. Here it went through a threshold. I invested the ends myself.”

“Can I sense them?”

“They aren’t there anymore.”

Elias straightened, disappointed enough to make the master laugh.

“First I teach you to ask during the demonstration. Then we’ll see about the rest.”

Marcel retrieved the nut from the tray and put it in his hand. It was cold, one side dirty with oil. It retained no spark, inscription or answer he could extract by squeezing harder.

Marcel cleared the tray and had him put the nut on the bench.

“Now yours.”

Elias reached the little object and its destination. Small Transfer shortened the wait between the two positions: the nut left the wood and arrived in the tray, offering no edge into which he could introduce something else. A single sound again. The result resembled the demonstration enough to have deceived him before he had seen it.

He extended a hand toward the starting point. Marcel touched his wrist.

“What are you looking for?”

“An opening.”

“Did you make one?”

Elias lowered his hand. He had transferred the object; he was looking for the door of a different exercise. He picked up the nut from the tray again, and this time the metal seemed heavier.

“So the passage has to remain while something crosses it.”

“The passage has to exist. Then size, distance and duration will each demand their own work. Wanting to put something bigger through isn’t enough.”

He remembered the caliper clenched in front of the duke’s pumps. He had not wanted a second machine: he had wanted a way to understand what the first put before him. That question was still his now. Marcel gave him time to formulate it without filling the silence.

“You don’t need to convince me you’re interested,” he said. “You need to tell me where you want to begin.”

Elias tried to hold all the answers together and lost most of them. The Mutual’s handrail returned clearly: the point beneath his palm, his unbalanced body, Noemi needing to stay upright. He could have wished for more strength, more space, a world with fewer things to hold up.

“With the reference,” he said. “When I keep that steady, at least the rest stops carrying me away.”

Marcel nodded. He took a sheet and asked him to distinguish the exercise from what he had done with the other hand. Elias began with the handrail, not the key. The master stopped him at one position, had him repeat the movement without investing anything and moved the stool preventing him from stepping back.

On the second attempt, Elias realized he had tightened his arm when he only needed to change his footing. On the third he could hold the reference without stiffening his shoulder. He had gained no threshold. He had found something to practice the next day.

Marcel wrote down two instructions and left room for his observations.

“Preparing for the second stage isn’t only this,” he said. “We’ll discuss access too, and what you need to face it. Not today between one repair and the next.”

“When?”

The master looked at the calendar. He chose an interval still free the following week and marked it. Elias copied the note into his notebook. At last he had asked for a date without waiting for desire to become an emergency.

When he put on his jacket, the pocket reminded him of his payment.

A shop at the corner displayed used instruments. He stopped at the window. A small level had an almost intact case; beside it, the price of an incomplete device promised a bargain to anyone who could obtain the missing parts. Elias followed the place where its second support ought to have been.

He could have gone in and asked.

He took out the sheet on which Marcel had written the exercise. For that, the workshop and a little agreed time were enough. He put the paper away and walked on before imagining how easy it would be to find a support he had not yet begun to look for.

Lise met him on the stairs. She carried a folder so full she had to support it underneath and asked whether he had finished or merely decided to take a break.

“Paid,” Elias said.

She stopped on the step. “The six?”

He nodded. His sister showed him her free hand, open.

“To help you carry them.”

Elias laughed and took the folder instead. Lise surrendered the weight with a relief she tried to conceal. On top lay a drawing of a facade with an enormous erasure over the entrance.

“You changed the entrance?”

“The drawing. The entrance knows nothing about it.”

They went up. Adrien sat at the table and had left a place free without knowing it would be needed for a folder. Lise resumed her work; Elias laid the payment before his father and removed the wrapping.

Adrien looked at the coins. “The pumps?”

“The additional work. It’s finished.”

His father counted them once and pushed them back. “Where do you keep them?”

Elias had prepared three answers about spending them. The question made him look for the box he already used for his money. He found it beneath a book and brought it to the table. Inside lay small accounts that would buy nothing merely because he regarded them more optimistically.

He added the coins without deciding the fate of the entire sum.

“I’d like to contribute to these days’ expenses,” he said.

Adrien removed his glasses. He neither refused out of pride nor held out his hand from habit. He told him which purchases they would make together after comparing the week’s account. Lise asked whether that included a lamp that would not force all three of them to crowd beneath the same light.

“It includes discussing it,” their father replied.

“Excellent. I’ll start now.”

Elias let her show him the page. The erasure was not on the drawing she would submit, but on a trial copy. The entrance was wide; the problem concerned hinges that would make a leaf strike the step. He traced the arc with the pencil and stopped before correcting it for her.

Lise moved the pivot, considered the new movement and took the pencil from his fingers.

“Now I can make my own mistakes.”

Their father smiled over his work. Elias closed the box, put it away and realized he did not mind leaving a sum without a destination. He had asked for an exercise and a date; money could remain money until he had something to buy.

He had to go back to Yselle to collect a copy of the handover. He picked up his jacket and found the nut in the notebook pocket. He had carried it away without noticing.

He showed Lise.

“This isn’t the change.”

“I hope they paid you for what you understood too.”

Elias thought of the two openings above the bench. He would return the nut to Marcel the next morning. For that evening, he liked having something small that had really gone to the other side.

Chapter 78 — The Price of a Miracle

Yselle held the letter between two pencil-stained fingers.

“This one isn’t mine,” she said before Elias could confuse it with the handover copy.

His name was written on the envelope. Below, somebody who had delivered it had corrected the workshop address. Elias recognized the forwarding mark and instinctively turned toward the door, as though the duke’s carriage might have deposited this too.

“It came by post,” Yselle clarified. “He’s in Rovenne.”

Elias accepted the envelope. He had not reckoned on how difficult it would be to decide whether to open it immediately. He had waited for it as an ordinary reply, and now he kept holding it by the sealed side.

Yselle returned to the bench without asking him to read it aloud.

Pump two stood still. The third retained its cloth folded over the top, clear of the grips. Beneath a window, a man measured material for a delivery unrelated to the duke. The workshop had already found other things to do with the presence of those machines.

“Your copy,” the engineer said, showing him a separate sheet.

Elias checked pump one’s number, the reference to spare parts and the date. Water had reached where it needed to go. The Mutual had received instructions it could use, not merely the memory of a demonstration. He put the document in his folder without folding it through the signature.

“And for you?” he asked.

Yselle followed his glance to the surface where she had kept the accounts.

“The new agreement is signed. What remains to be paid hasn’t vanished. But my shares no longer stand behind capital we don’t need.”

She paused, almost surprised by the pleasure of saying it to someone who had not participated in the negotiation.

“I haven’t asked you for another measurement,” she added.

“I’d already opened my notebook.”

She smiled. Then a man at the entrance asked whether a component could leave that afternoon, and Yselle answered first with a question about its destination. The miracle had not freed her from needing to know where things went. It had given her something to work with besides a list of missing parts.

Elias said goodbye. The letter stayed closed until the street.

He did not want its first lines to belong only to him. The decision felt very solemn until he caught himself holding the envelope against the sun to guess the length of the sheet inside.

He put it back in his folder and laughed at himself.

Livia waited near the room’s ordinary entrance. They had agreed to meet after work; Edith would come later. The copyist carried a parcel beneath her arm and the expression of somebody who had already decided not to answer a question.

“It isn’t a present,” she said when he looked at it.

“I didn’t ask.”

“You were doing it.”

They went in through the door. The journey required stairs, a turn, time to find the key. Elias felt habit begin erasing that route because it held nothing extraordinary. He retraced it in his mind while Livia unlocked the door. That very route brought them where they needed to go without asking anything of the Hinge.

The oval table occupied the room with the confidence of an object intended for more people than used it. Twelve chairs; three would suffice that evening. Livia set down her parcel, changed the chair she had taken and found one whose edge did not press against her leg.

Elias left the folder closed in front of his place.

“He answered?”

He nodded.

“You’ve read it?”

“No.”

Livia looked at him long enough to make him fear she knew about the experiment against the sun. Then she rested her hands on the table and stopped asking questions too.

Edith arrived breathless from the last flight. She removed her coat, spread it over the chair back and asked for water before saying hello. Elias passed her the glass. She drank half and looked at the envelope.

“You aren’t going to make me put a hand on that paper and swear something.”

“We might,” Livia said.

“Then I’m eating first.”

Bread and something separately wrapped emerged from the parcel. Livia pushed it toward her with the offended expression of somebody whose sensible intention had been discovered too soon. Elias broke the bread. They waited for Edith to set down the glass; then he opened the letter along its edge.

Alexander agreed to talk with them. He asked which questions they wanted to tackle first and said he had questions of his own about their work.

The lines fixed no place, took no ownership of their request and did not promise solutions to every problem they might care to write about. They answered people who had asked for a conversation. He wanted to know how to arrange it.

Elias read the final part again. The signature was the same one he had seen beneath the donation of the machines. His own letter did not yet contain a place for a signature like his.

He passed the sheet to Livia.

“It’s a yes,” Edith said, leaning far enough to read with her.

“To the conversation,” Elias replied.

“I know. We didn’t ask him to live here.”

Livia laughed and read the rest. Her finger stopped at the part about arranging the meeting.

“We’ll have to choose where.”

The ensuing silence had nothing to do with finding a table. Elias looked at the empty chairs, the broad surface, the distance between their three places. He had seen the duke bend to examine a liner seat, then make three complete machines exist. It would have been easy to picture him at the head of the table and call that figure a solution.

“Not here the first time,” he said.

Edith looked up. “Why?”

The question did him good. He could have hidden his fear inside a rule nobody had established.

“Because I want to be able to ask him something without watching to see whether this room convinces him. And I want you to be able to get up without feeling you’ve invited him into everything we have.”

Livia moved the sheet to the middle. “He’ll need to understand what he’s answering too. We aren’t an assignment from Yselle.”

“We wrote through her,” Edith pointed out.

“To get the letter there. Not to have her guarantee every question.”

Edith took another piece of bread. She thought while chewing, looking irritated at how visible her reasoning must be to the others.

“One meeting, then. Afterward we decide whether we want another.”

Nobody put the duke’s name above a chair.

“And if we don’t like what he proposes?” Edith asked.

“I’ll tell him,” Elias replied.

Livia looked at him. “Even if I like it?”

The ready answer stopped in his mouth. He had imagined taking the unpleasant part to protect them; in doing so he had already chosen where both of them would stand.

“Then I’ll tell him for myself.”

Edith pushed her chair back just enough to free her coat. “That suits me better. I don’t want to have to be brave about what you’ve decided to endure too.”

Her frankness made him lower his eyes. Livia did not smile to remove his discomfort. She turned the letter toward him and asked whether he truly wanted the meeting now that he had an answer he could use.

Elias thought of the nut on Marcel’s bench, the two views of the same box. Being safe from everything he did not understand was not enough.

“Yes,” he said. “Even if we disagree afterward.”

“Then let’s arrange it,” she replied.

Elias found a free page. The thirteenth was a day to propose, far enough off to receive an answer without pretending the road between two cities could fold because they were impatient. He wrote it at the top and left the time and place blank. They would have to find an available room and learn whether the day suited Alexander too.

“What do we ask him?” Livia said.

Elias had prepared an answer about the ability to create matter. He thought of Noemi’s skin becoming whole and the way she had searched for pain. The next question might occupy every evening of his life.

“I’d like to understand how he recognizes that what he creates is what he’s been asked for.”

Livia frowned. “The design?”

“That too. But he asked us for measurements, processes, the model. He didn’t produce a pump just because he knew its name. I’d like to know where what he has to learn ends and what he can do begins.”

Edith stopped eating. “For people it’s a different question.”

Elias looked at her. She was not only talking about Noemi.

“I don’t want to ask for healing on somebody else’s behalf,” Edith said. “I want to ask what one needs to know before offering it. If he can tell us. When I asked about Heart, I realized I kept picturing only the moment when pain ends.”

The sentence remained on the table. Livia did not try to complete it with a promise. Instead she picked up the pencil and wrote her own question about the difference between a preserved form and a thing truly present.

“This one is mine,” she said.

Elias read. He had not decided whether all three belonged in the same conversation; for the first time, though, the letter did not oblige him to prove his friends were interesting enough to deserve a duke’s time.

They were their questions. If Alexander had something to answer, they could listen without giving them away with the room.

“And what do we bring?” Edith asked.

Livia indicated the sheet. “A conversation, to begin with.”

“I didn’t mean a reward.”

“Neither did I.”

Elias thought of Yselle’s bench. The duke had needed somebody to show him part of the world before making another part exist. He did not know how rare it was for him to encounter that need. He could offer a problem he truly knew instead of pretending to possess a larger secret.

“I’ll bring a drawing and the test that changed it,” he said. “Not the Hinge drawing. The liner. If he wants to understand how we work, I’ll show him.”

Edith nodded. “Then I’ll bring what I don’t know.”

“Is it heavy?”

“Very. Don’t offer to lift it before I finish explaining.”

They laughed. The table did not become smaller; it simply seemed less empty.

Livia gathered their questions and left Elias room to write a proposal. Not an oath, not a list of favors. He put down the possible day, specified that they would confirm an ordinary venue and reread the sentence without adding an explanation of their entire lives.

The commercial request Ilian had brought lay in the other part of his folder. It still awaited a decision. He did not take it out to make the meeting with Alexander seem more urgent; the duke had not agreed to settle an unknown draftsman’s account.

When they finished, Edith put the bread back in its wrapping. Livia opened the parcel to check she had not left her notebook inside and found only a crumb. She brushed it into her palm. Elias folded the proposal for delivery the next day and put it away separately from the letter received.

All three stood. He was last to leave, after looking at the table once.

The Hinge still had a single opening available. Elias did not use it to save himself the stairs.

Outside, the evening air had lost the hot smell of workshops. From a nearby street came water striking stone: a bucket emptied, not the Mutual’s pump audible all the way here. Elias walked on without seeking a miracle in every noise.

He had received six crowns and an answer. Part of the work was finished; another part finally depended on a question he had chosen to ask.
